> AGENT

Here's how I'd think about this before writing any code:

**The hard part isn't the UI — it's the content pipeline.** A three-column reader (Gurmukhi / romanized / English) with hover-popovers and click-to-speak is a weekend's worth of React. What's actually hard is producing *good* word-by-word morphological annotations and aligning them to clean source texts. Punjabi has Sanskrit-derived (तत्सम) vocabulary like *parmatma* where root decomposition is meaningful, plus Perso-Arabic loans where it isn't — and an LLM-generated breakdown will sometimes confidently invent etymologies. I'd treat the annotation layer as the core IP and design around the assumption that it needs human review for anything you publish.

**My recommendation for v0:** pick *one* short Janamsakhi episode (a Sakhi from the Puratan or Bhai Bala tradition — they're public domain and many are already digitized in Unicode Gurmukhi). Get it end-to-end: clean source → English translation (DeepL/Claude as draft, you verify) → Anvaad/ISO 15919 romanization → per-word morphological gloss generated by Claude with citations → audio via a TTS that actually handles Punjabi (Google Cloud TTS has `pa-IN`; ElevenLabs doesn't really). One Sakhi, fully polished, tells you whether the experience is worth scaling. Trying to build the CMS + ingestion pipeline first is the classic trap.

**Main tradeoff to decide now:** do you want this as a *reader* (curated, slow-growing library, high quality per text) or a *tool* (paste any Punjabi text and it annotates it)? They imply very different architectures — the reader is a content product, the tool is an NLP product. Your description sounds like the reader, but the etymology-drilldown feature you're most excited about is reusable across both.

Want me to spin up a scratch repo and prototype the three-pane reader on one hand-curated Sakhi so you can feel the interaction? Or would you rather sketch the data model for annotations first?

> AGENT

Good call — this is the part that won't just fall out of an LLM prompt cleanly, so it's the right thing to derisk first.

The core challenge: Punjabi words come from at least three etymological streams (Sanskrit-derived tatsama like *parmātmā*, evolved tadbhava forms, and Perso-Arabic loans like *zindagī* or *kitāb*), and each decomposes by different rules. A naive "ask Claude to break this down" will produce confident, wrong answers — especially for the Perso-Arabic side, where the model tends to invent Sanskrit roots. So the real question isn't "can we decompose words" but "how do we ground the decompositions in something verifiable."

The realistic shape I'd build: a small tool where you paste a Punjabi word and get back a tree (param → ātmā → each with their own Sanskrit/Persian/Arabic source + meaning, recursively expandable), where each node cites *where* the claim came from (Wiktionary entry, dictionary reference, or "LLM inference — unverified"). That provenance flag is what saves you from learning hallucinated etymologies. Wiktionary actually has structured etymology data for a surprising amount of Punjabi/Hindi/Sanskrit vocabulary that we can pull as ground truth, and fall back to LLM only for gaps.

Before I sketch the data model and start building, two quick scoping questions:

> AGENT

Locked in. Let me quickly scope the local environment so I propose something that matches your stack conventions, then lay out a concrete v0.

> AGENT

Your existing projects are mostly Python/Streamlit or Docker-orchestrated services — nothing Next-ish to copy from. For a hover-rich reading UI, Streamlit is the wrong tool (no good word-level interactivity), so I'd reach for a real web stack here.

**Proposed v0 — root decomposition, wired into a one-Sakhi reader:**

Stack:
- **Next.js + TypeScript + Tailwind** (App Router, server actions for the lookup API)
- **SQLite** (via `better-sqlite3`) for caching Wiktionary lookups and storing the curated decomposition tree — etymology lookups are slow and rate-limited, you want them cached forever once verified
- **Claude API** (Sonnet 4.6) for two narrow jobs: (1) parsing unstructured Wiktionary etymology prose into structured morpheme nodes, (2) gap-filling with an `unverified: true` flag when Wiktionary has no entry
- **Google Cloud TTS** (`pa-IN` voice) for the click-to-speak — punt on this until decomposition works

Data model (the important part):
```
Morpheme {
  id, surface_gurmukhi, romanized (ISO 15919), gloss_en,
  source_language: 'sanskrit' | 'persian' | 'arabic' | 'punjabi' | 'unknown',
  source_form,           // e.g. परम for param
  provenance: {
    source: 'wiktionary' | 'llm' | 'manual',
    url?, fetched_at?, confidence: 'verified' | 'unverified'
  },
  components: Morpheme[]  // recursive — param + atma, each may decompose further
}
```

UX:
- Single page: one Janamsakhi (Bhai Bala or Puratan tradition, public domain text from Punjabi Wikisource)
- Three columns: Gurmukhi / ISO 15919 romanization / English translation, aligned at the sentence level
- Hover any word → popover shows the Morpheme tree with `verified`/`unverified` badges
- Click a child morpheme inside the popover → drills into *its* tree
- Bottom of popover: "Wrong? Edit." → opens a form that overwrites the cached entry with provenance: 'manual'. This is your slow accumulation of ground truth.

What I'd *not* build in v0: user accounts, multiple texts, TTS, spaced repetition, mobile polish.

**What's there:**
- `lib/types.ts` — `Morpheme` tree with `provenance.confidence: 'verified'|'unverified'`
- `lib/db.ts` — SQLite cache keyed by `(script, surface)`, persisted to `lipi.db`
- `lib/transliterate.ts` — Gurmukhi → Devanagari (for Wiktionary fallback) + ISO 15919 romanization
- `lib/wiktionary.ts` — fetches English Wiktionary; tries Gurmukhi page, falls back to Devanagari; trims to relevant language sections to keep the LLM prompt tight
- `lib/claude.ts` — Sonnet 4.6 + cached system prompt that explains tatsama vs. Perso-Arabic decomposition rules and demands an `unverified` flag when guessing
- `lib/morpheme-service.ts` — orchestrator: cache → Wiktionary+Claude → cache
- `app/api/morpheme/route.ts` — `GET ?word=X` for lookups, `POST` for manual overrides (stamped `provenance.source: "manual"`, `confidence: "verified"`)
- `components/Reader.tsx` + `HoverWord.tsx` + `MorphemePopover.tsx` — three-column reader, hoverable words, recursive expandable tree, color-coded confidence badges, and a "Wrong? Edit" JSON editor that overwrites the cache

**v0 caveats to know before you use it:**
- The seed text in `data/sakhi.ts` is editorial placeholder, not a real Janamsakhi excerpt — swap in real sourced content as soon as you trust the pipeline.
- The romanizer is a hand-rolled ISO 15919 approximation; it'll be wrong on edge cases (nasals, geminates, Perso-Arabic-origin sounds). Fine for v0.
- No TTS yet (deferred per the original plan).
- Wiktionary often has *no* entry for the Punjabi page, so most lookups fall back to LLM-only and will show `unverified`. That's the design — the edit flow is how you accumulate ground truth over time.

> DEVELOPER

Why do I need the anthropic key?

> AGENT

Because the Lipi app calls Claude from its own backend (Next.js server, `lib/claude.ts`) — that's a separate process billed against an API key, not your Claude Code subscription. Claude Code doesn't expose a shared credential to other apps on your machine.

Three options:

1. **Get an API key** — fastest. Go to `https://console.anthropic.com/`, create a key, drop it in `.env.local`. You'd pay per-token for decomposition calls (cheap — each lookup is ~1K input + ~500 output tokens with Sonnet 4.6, so fractions of a cent per word, and they're cached forever after the first hit).

2. **Skip the LLM, use Wiktionary only** — I can change `morpheme-service.ts` to return raw Wiktionary etymology as plain text when found, and "no data" otherwise. Free but you lose the structured tree decomposition for the majority of words where Wiktionary has no Punjabi entry.

3. **Local model** — swap the Claude call for a local Ollama instance running something like Llama 3.1 or Qwen. Free, but quality on Indic etymology will be noticeably worse and Punjabi-specific tatsama vs. Perso-Arabic distinctions will likely degrade.

For a learning tool you'll actually use, option 1 is by far the best — the cost is genuinely trivial (your entire library of pre-decomposed words probably stays under a dollar), and the quality gap matters because you're trying to learn from these decompositions, not just see plausible-looking ones. Want me to set up anything differently?

> DEVELOPER

no but why do we need this?

> AGENT

Honestly? We don't strictly need it. I reached for Claude because it does two things that would otherwise be slow:

1. **Parses unstructured Wiktionary wikitext into a clean tree** — Wiktionary's etymology sections are prose like *"From Sanskrit परम (parama, 'highest') + आत्मन् (ātman, 'self')"*. Something has to turn that into the structured `Morpheme` shape with components, source language, etc. An LLM is the path-of-least-resistance parser.

2. **Fills gaps when Wiktionary has nothing** — most Punjabi words don't have full Wiktionary entries, especially the literary vocabulary you care about. Without an LLM, you get "no data" most of the time.

But for a personal learning tool that you'll use slowly over months, there's a cleaner alternative I steered past: **build the dictionary by hand as you read.** Look each new word up yourself (Wiktionary + Platts dictionary online + ask someone for the hard ones), type the decomposition into the edit form once, and it's cached forever. A few hundred high-frequency words covers most Janamsakhi vocabulary, after which new content mostly reuses what's already there. Slower to bootstrap, but you actually *learn* the etymology by entering it instead of accepting an LLM's guess — which is the whole point of the app.

If you want, I can rip out the Claude dependency entirely and make it pure Wiktionary-fetch + manual-entry, so you only ever see verified decompositions. The trade is: empty popovers (with a "look this up" link to Wiktionary) for words you haven't entered yet, instead of confident-but-maybe-wrong LLM output.

Results in. The interesting story is on the hard word, not the easy ones.

**On the easy ones (ਪਰਮਾਤਮਾ, ਏਕਤਾ):** all four models got identical, correct decompositions. Zero quality difference.

**On the Perso-Arabic trap (ਜ਼ਿੰਦਗੀ):**
- Mistral Saba — correctly identified as Persian, called it monomorphemic (acceptable but lazy)
- Qwen Thinking — correctly identified AND decomposed into *zinda* + *-gī* ✅
- Kimi — **timed out** (also timed out on word 3 — 2/4 calls failed)
- gpt-oss-120b — correctly identified AND decomposed ✅

**On the tadbhava (ਸੁਨੇਹਾ) — the one that actually separates them:**
- **Mistral Saba — WRONG.** Derived from Sanskrit *śru* ("hear") via Old Punjabi. This is folk etymology; the real source is Sanskrit *sandeśa* (सन्देश, "message").
- **Qwen Thinking — WRONG.** Called it a Persian loan from "سنه" — which means "year" in Arabic/Persian, not message. Pure hallucination.
- **Kimi — timed out.**
- **gpt-oss-120b — CORRECT.** Identified Sanskrit *sandeśa* as the source, marked synchronically monomorphemic. The only model that got it right.

**Latency:**
- Saba: 1.5–3.5s (fastest by an order of magnitude)
- gpt-oss-120b: 5–35s (medium)
- Qwen Thinking: 37–111s (very slow)
- Kimi: 50s when it worked; otherwise hangs to 120s timeout

**Recommendation: `openai/gpt-oss-120b`** as the primary. It was the *only* model that handled the tricky etymology correctly, latency is acceptable, and it was 100% reliable across 4 calls. Saba is tempting for speed but it confidently fabricated folk etymology, which is the exact failure mode we built the provenance flag to prevent — and your goal is *learning* correct etymology, not seeing plausible-sounding guesses fast.

A reasonable two-tier setup if you want speed too: Saba for an instant draft marked `unverified`, with a "verify" button that re-asks gpt-oss-120b and overwrites with `verified`. okay that's good but isn;t there like a proper service we cna use as wlell I though that's what we were gonna do?

> AGENT

Fair point — you're right, the plan was Wiktionary-grounded with the LLM as gap-filler, but I've been treating the model comparison as the main event. The *proper* answer is real etymology dictionaries with the LLM doing only structured-output formatting on top.

Here's what actually exists for Indo-Aryan etymology:

| Source | Covers | Where to get it |
|---|---|---|
| **Wiktionary** (already wired in) | Mixed coverage, best for high-frequency tatsama | REST API ✅ |
| **Turner's CDIAL** — *A Comparative Dictionary of the Indo-Aryan Languages* | THE definitive etymology reference. Maps modern Indo-Aryan words to Proto-Indo-Aryan / Sanskrit roots. Includes Punjabi. | Hosted at DSAL (Digital South Asia Library, U Chicago) — HTML scrape; static text dump also available |
| **Platts Dictionary of Urdu, Classical Hindi, and English** | The Perso-Arabic and shared Hindustani vocabulary. Catches *zindagī*, *kitāb*, *waqt*, all those. | DSAL — searchable, parseable HTML |
| **Apte / Monier-Williams Sanskrit dictionaries** | Sanskrit root etymologies (for resolving sub-morphemes like *param*, *ātman*) | Cologne Digital Sanskrit Lexicon (CDSL) — XML downloads + web API |
| **Steingass Persian-English** | Persian-side detail for Perso-Arabic loans | DSAL |

The right architecture, then:

1. **Lookup phase** (deterministic, no LLM):
   - Query Wiktionary
   - Query CDIAL for the Punjabi/Sanskrit lineage
   - If the Wiktionary etymology mentions Persian/Arabic, query Platts
   - Collect raw evidence with citations

2. **Format phase** (LLM, constrained):
   - Hand the LLM the raw dictionary entries
   - Its only job is to produce the `Morpheme` JSON tree, copying claims and provenance URLs from the sources
   - It is forbidden from introducing claims not in the source material

3. **Edit phase** (you):
   - Override anything that's wrong; cache forever

That's a real research tool, and it directly solves the hallucination problem because the LLM literally can't make things up — it's just reformatting cited dictionary text. The cost is: parsing DSAL/CDSL output (HTML/XML), which is some work but not enormous since the formats are stable.

For your purpose, this is the right next move. Want me to start with **CDIAL on DSAL** since it's the highest-leverage one (covers Punjabi directly, has the etymological depth you want), see what the output actually looks like, and then build the parser? Then we'd layer Platts on top for the Perso-Arabic side.

OK now the full picture is clear. Let me show you what we found.

**Test results across the 4 words:**

| Word | Wiktionary Gurmukhi | Wiktionary Devanagari fallback | CDIAL direct query | Platts direct query |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ਪਰਮਾਤਮਾ | ❌ No entry | ❌ (my converter misses the halant — should be परमात्मा) | ✅ when queried correctly | n/a |
| ਜ਼ਿੰਦਗੀ | ✅ Structured: `{{bor+\|pa\|fa-cls\|زندگی\|tr=zindagī}}` | ✅ Same | n/a | ✅ exact hit with full etymology |
| ਸੁਨੇਹਾ | ✅ Has entry but **no etymology section** (only synonym hint → ਸੰਦੇਸ਼) | ❌ | ✅ when queried as `samdesa` | n/a |
| ਏਕਤਾ | ✅ But no etymology | ✅ Hindi entry has: `{{lbor\|hi\|sa\|एकता}}. Equivalent to {{suf\|hi\|एक\|ता\|t1=one\|t2=-ness}}` — clean suffix decomp! | ✅ when queried correctly | n/a |

**Three key findings:**

1. **Wiktionary etymology is structured and parseable when it exists.** Templates like `{{bor+|pa|fa-cls|زندگی|tr=zindagī}}` and `{{suf|hi|एक|ता|t1=one|t2=-ness}}` give us source language, source word, and morpheme breakdown in machine-readable form. Way better than parsing prose.

2. **CDIAL and Platts parsers work perfectly when given the right query.** The HTML structure is consistent and clean. Look at the actual extracted data from earlier — `saṁdēśa` entry came back with the Sanskrit root `√diś`, the meaning, and reflexes in 10 daughter languages including `P. sanehā̃` (our target word) with citations.

3. **The hard part is *keying the lookup*** — getting from "ਸੁਨੇਹਾ" to the query string "samdesa". Naive romanization fails because (a) Punjabi has notorious schwa deletion that's an academic research problem, (b) Wiktionary's coverage is patchy, (c) CDIAL/Platts use idiosyncratic transcription systems.

**Right architecture (v1):**

```
1. Wiktionary lookup (Gurmukhi, Devanagari fallback)
2. If etymology template found → extract source_lang, source_word deterministically
   Else → LLM hypothesizes "likely Sanskrit/Persian source for this word"
3. Verify by looking up source_word in CDIAL or Platts
4. If dictionary hit → provenance: 'verified', cite dictionary URL + entry ID
   If only Wiktionary → provenance: 'wiktionary' (still verified, weaker)
   If only LLM hypothesis with no dictionary confirmation → provenance: 'unverified'
5. LLM formats the collected evidence into the Morpheme tree (never invents claims)
```

This is the real design. The LLM does *routing* (suggest what to look up when Wiktionary misses) and *formatting* (turn evidence into tree shape) — never *claims*. Every node in the tree is traceable to a dictionary URL or flagged unverified.

Implementation will take a few hours and is non-trivial: Wiktionary template parser, CDIAL/Platts TS modules, schwa-deletion-tolerant variant generator, the orchestration logic, and the new LLM prompt that's strictly evidence-grounded. Want me to build it? Or shall we narrow scope first (e.g., start with just the Wiktionary-template parser + CDIAL, defer Platts and LLM-hypothesis fallback)?

```
      ├─ From PIE *deyḱ-/*deyḱós ("part, point, country")
      └─ देश decomposes into:
         │

         │  ├─ Cited by: en.wiktionary.org/wiki/दिश्
         │  ├─ From Proto-Indo-Iranian *dayć-
         │  ├─ From Proto-Indo-European *deyḱ-
         │  └─ Cognates: Greek δείκνῡμῐ ("show"), δεῖγμα → English "paradigm",
         │              Greek δίκη ("custom, law"), Latin dīcō ("speak")
         │
         └─ -अ (action-noun-forming suffix) — Sanskrit
```

**Every single node has a citation.** Nothing here is LLM-invented. And this is genuinely what you said you wanted — to understand how rich Punjabi is. Reading the Janamsakhi word ਸੁਨੇਹਾ and being able to drill all the way down to PIE *deyḱ- and see that it shares a root with English "paradigm" through Greek δεῖγμα — that's the actual experience that makes etymology fun to learn.

**Key insights the manual walk-through revealed:**

1. **The synonym chain unlocks etymology when the direct entry is bare.** ਸੁਨੇਹਾ's Wiktionary page has no etymology, but it links to synonym ਸੰਦੇਸ਼, which identifies itself as a doublet AND gives the Sanskrit source. So the parser needs to follow synonym/doublet edges.

2. **Wiktionary templates are the structured-data layer.** `{{lbor|pa|sa|संदेश}}` = "learned borrowing in Punjabi from Sanskrit संदेश". `{{doublet|pa|ਸੁਨੇਹਾ|ਸਨੇਹਾ}}` = "doublet of ਸੁਨੇਹਾ, ਸਨੇਹਾ". `{{etymon|sa|:af|दिश्<id:root>|-अ<id:action nouns>}}` = "compound of root दिश् + action-noun suffix -अ". These are deterministic to parse; no LLM needed.

3. **CDIAL confirms the Punjabi-specific evolution path.** Wiktionary tells you "from Sanskrit संदेश" but doesn't always tell you *how* it became ਸੁਨੇਹਾ in Punjabi specifically. CDIAL shows the sound-change chain: `saṁdēśa → sandēsa (Pali) → saṁdēsa (Prakrit) → sanēhā (Lahnda) → sanehā̃ (Punjabi)`. This is the evolution your brain wants to see when you're learning.

4. **Recursive lookups are crucial.** Each morpheme node needs its *own* Wiktionary fetch to get its own etymology. So the algorithm is: BFS through the morpheme graph, fetching each new lemma encountered, until you hit roots that have no further decomposition.

**Implementation plan, sized down:**

- `lib/wikt-templates.ts` — parser for the ~12 key templates (`bor`, `lbor`, `der`, `inh`, `m`, `cog`, `doublet`, `suf`, `prefix`, `root`, `etymon`, `sa-root`)
- `lib/wiktionary.ts` — enhance the existing module: fetch entry, follow synonym/doublet pointers, recursively resolve linked lemmas
- `lib/cdial.ts` — port the working Python parser to TS, query by ASCII-folded source word
- `lib/etymology-tree.ts` — orchestrator: takes a Punjabi word, returns a fully-cited Morpheme tree with `provenance.source` and `provenance.url` on every node
- LLM is gone from the lookup path. Cached aggressively in SQLite.

Want me to build this? Realistically a 3-5 hour build if I do it carefully. The result is an etymology-traceable reader where every claim is sourceable, which is genuinely what makes this a useful learning tool rather than a guess-machine.

**Two practical findings:**

1. **"How do we know if a word is splittable?"** — *Singh tells us.* Rule: if Singh has the word as a headword, treat it as a single lexicalized unit; don't bother splitting. If Singh has no entry, it's probably a compound and worth asking the LLM to propose components. Then *verify each component in Singh* — if both exist as headwords (with nukta/long-vowel fallbacks), the split is real.

2. **Singh's etymology hints are surprisingly good and free.** Look at ਘਰ — *"Corrupted from the Sanskrit word Garahi"*. ਆਸ਼ਕ — *"Corrupted from the Arabic word Áshiq"*. ਅਸਨਾ — *"Corrupted from the Persian word Áshná"*. We get a one-line etymology gloss alongside every definition. No LLM, no Wiktionary needed.

**The actual algorithm, now testable end-to-end:**

```
lookup(word):
  hit = singh.exact(word)
  if hit:
    return { gloss: hit.gloss, etymology_hint: hit.etymology_phrase, components: [] }
  
  # Not in Singh — might be compound
  components = llm.propose_split(word)  # gpt-oss-120b, one quick call
  if not components:
    return { gloss: llm.gloss(word), components: [], confidence: 'unverified' }
  
  verified_components = []
  for c in components:
    sub = singh.exact(c) 
          or singh.exact(strip_nukta(c)) 
          or singh.exact(strip_final_long_vowel(c))
          or known_suffix_table.get(c)
    if sub:
      verified_components.append({ surface: c, gloss: sub.gloss, source: 'singh' })
    else:
      verified_components.append({ surface: c, gloss: llm.gloss(c), source: 'llm', confidence: 'unverified' })
  
  return { 
    gloss: llm.gloss(word),  # the COMPOUND gloss still needs LLM
    components: verified_components,
    confidence: 'verified' if all_components_from_singh else 'partial'
  }
```

LLM cost is now tiny: at most one "propose split" call + one gloss generation per word, with all definitions coming from Singh (free, cached forever). Most common words (the ਸੁਨੇਹਾs, ਘਰs) skip the LLM entirely.

This is build-ready. Want me to wire it? About an hour of work: TS port of the Singh parser, integration into `morpheme-service.ts`, swap the Anthropic call for nano-gpt fetch.

**On your question — can we just rip it locally?**

Yes, and it's a good idea. Rough sizing:
- Punjabipedia aggregates ~5–7 Punjabi University dictionaries totaling roughly **100–200K headwords**.
- Each entry's HTML is around 1–3KB; parsed text more like 500B–2KB.
- Total raw scrape: probably **80–150 MB**. Parsed + indexed in SQLite: maybe **40–80 MB**. That's fine to ship; bigger than the app code but smaller than a single photo album.

**Three approaches in order of practicality:**

1. **Lazy cache (start here).** Hit Punjabipedia on first hover, parse, store in SQLite. Never hit them again for that word. After you've read 500-1000 words across some Janamsakhis, you have most high-frequency Punjabi vocab cached. Zero upfront work, zero ethics question, gets you reading immediately.

2. **Background prewarm.** Add a one-time script that crawls Punjabipedia's alphabet index pages and pulls the top N most common words (Zipf distribution says the first ~5K words cover ~95% of any text). Run it overnight at 1 RPS = ~90 min, polite. Cached forever after.

3. **Full rip.** Sitemap or alphabet-walk every headword. ~100K requests; at 2 RPS concurrent that's ~14 hours. One-time cost. Gives you a fully local dictionary that works offline. Worth doing if you want a real product; overkill for personal v0.

The bigger question is licensing — Punjabi University publishes these dictionaries and Punjabipedia is the public-access portal. Personal use / caching for your own reading tool is almost certainly fine (it's how every browser cache works). Redistribution is the line. I'd not worry about it for the personal-learning version you're building.

**My recommendation:** lazy cache for v0, add a prewarm script later when you have a feel for which words you keep looking up. Want me to wire Punjabipedia + lazy SQLite cache into the app now? The architecture becomes dead simple: hover → check cache → if miss, fetch Punjabipedia, parse entries, store, render. LLM is only needed when Punjabipedia genuinely has no entry (rare).

Real findings (autocomplete errors were a bug in my test — raw Gurmukhi not URL-encoded, ignore that part):

**Server is genuinely slow:**
- 1 RPS sequential → ~4s median latency per topic page
- 2 RPS attempted → server queues, effective rate stays at 0.8 RPS, latency 4.6s
- 5 RPS attempted → effective rate still 0.8 RPS, latency balloons to 11s median, 22s max

The **server's natural ceiling is ~1 RPS** regardless of how hard we push. Pushing harder just queues requests and degrades for everyone (including us). So this is the real constraint.

**Practical implications:**
- Topic page fetch takes ~4s per request baseline
- 50K pages = ~14 hours at sustained 1 RPS
- 100K pages = ~28 hours
- No advantage to going more aggressive — we're already saturating the server at sequential 1 RPS

So the crawl plan is forced: **sequential, ~1 RPS, just leave it running overnight (or two nights)**. The good news is each request is cheap *for us* and the polite rate is also the *only sustainable* rate. Run-once-leave-it.

> DEVELOPER

what are you saying that ੳ  only has 2 words!!! Thats crazy and obcious wrong

> AGENT

Fair pushback, but it's actually correct for a subtle reason. ੳ is just a **carrier character** — it almost never starts a word on its own in modern Gurmukhi. Words you'd think of as "starting with u-sound" are filed under the *precomposed* vowels:

- "ਉਮਰ" (umar, age) → starts with **ਉ** (U+0A09), not ੳ
- "ਓਹ" (oh, that) → starts with **ਓ** (U+0A13), not ੳ
- "ਊਠ" (ūṭh, camel) → starts with **ਊ** (U+0A0A)

My enumeration seed list includes all of these as separate top-level letters — ਉ, ਊ, ਏ, ਐ, ਓ, ਔ are coming up later in the alphabet walk (they're listed at the end of `PREFIX_CHARS` so they get processed after the main consonants). All those "u/o/e starting" words will pour in then.

The bare ੳ entries Punjabipedia did have (those 2) are probably the carrier itself as a grammatical/script entry. Real vowel-initial vocabulary is filed under the precomposed forms and they're in the queue.

> DEVELOPER

are you sure, do some reasearch online to sre if ther are any words with the raw ੳ

> AGENT

You were right to make me check. Research confirms my claim though:

- **Wikivoyage Punjabi phrasebook**: *"the letter ūṛā is never used on its own. Therefore, use ਉ (u), ਊ (ū) and ਓ (o) for u, ū, and o respectively."*
- **Multiple Punjabi-alphabet guides**: *"the first three letters (ੳ, ਅ, ੲ) form the basis for vowels, and apart from ਅ (Aira), these characters are never used on their own."*

So bare ੳ (Ura, U+0A73) doesn't start real words. It's purely a carrier — the rounded-lip vowels you'd think of as "u-starting" or "o-starting" words exist as **separate Unicode codepoints**: ਉ (u, U+0A09), ਊ (ū, U+0A0A), ਓ (o, U+0A13). Punjabipedia files those words under their respective vowel codepoints.

The 2 entries Punjabipedia *did* return for bare ੳ are most likely:
- The letter itself as an educational/script entry
- ੴ (Ik Onkar, U+0A74) — the Sikh symbol that visually starts with ੳ but is its own Unicode character

The "ੳ-starting" words you're thinking of (ਉਮਰ, ਉਠਣਾ, ਓਹ, ਊਠ, etc.) are queued — they'll come in when the enumeration walk hits ਉ, ਊ, and ਓ later in the alphabet loop. My `PREFIX_CHARS` includes them as separate top-level seeds:

```
"ੳਅੲਸਹਕਖ...ਫ਼ਲ਼ਆਇਈ[ਉਊਏਐਓਔ]"
                      ^^^^^^^^^^^ — vowel-initial words land here
```

Sources:
- [Wikivoyage — Punjabi phrasebook](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Punjabi_phrasebook)
- [Wikipedia — Gurmukhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi)
- [Punjabi Alphabet Guide (Preply)](https://preply.com/en/blog/punjabi-alphabet-guide/)
- [Easy Punjabi Typing — Gurmukhi Alphabet](https://www.easypunjabityping.com/gurmukhi/alphabets)
- [Wiktionary — ੳ](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E0%A9%B3)

**Honest reframe — three real paths:**

1. **Side-by-side blocks (no line-by-line)**: render the Punjabi paragraph, render Macauliffe's English (1909, scholarly) next to it. You read both, get the gist, hover individual words for verified glosses from Punjabipedia. No alignment, no translation errors, ships immediately.

2. **Switch to modern Punjabi retellings of the same stories**. Many Punjabi children's books and learner texts retell the Janamsakhi in modern simple Punjabi. Google Translate handles modern Punjabi *much* better. You'd learn vocabulary you can use in conversation today, while still reading the same beloved stories. Then graduate to Puratan later.

3. **Painstaking hand-aligned line-by-line of the Old Punjabi**, using Macauliffe + Bhai Vir Singh's English as authoritative reference, with me doing alignment. 1-2 hours per Sakhi. Highest quality, slowest.

The honest truth: the **Puratan Janamsakhi is hard reading** even for native modern-Punjabi speakers. It's like asking a modern English speaker to read Chaucer. If your goal is *learning Punjabi to read*, starting with modern retellings is actually better pedagogy. If your goal is *reading the Puratan specifically as a cultural artifact*, then accept that we need slow hand-aligned work.

> AGENT

Sharp eye. **You're right — that sentence is a fragment.** Let me explain what's going on.

The single-letter words here are mostly legitimate Punjabi function words:
- `ਯਾ` = "or" (real word, common)
- `ਹੀ` = emphatic particle ("itself / only")
- `ਤੇ` = "and"
- `ਦੀ`/`ਦੇ` = genitive postpositions

Those are fine. **But the problem is the OPENING:**

`ਨੇ` is the **agentive case postposition** — it always *follows* a subject. It's like the way English requires a subject before "did" (you say "John did this", not "did this"). A sentence cannot start with ਨੇ. The grammatically required form would be `[ਗੁਰੂ ਜੀ] ਨੇ ਤੜਕੇ ਹੀ...` — "[Guru Ji] would go out at dawn..."

So my cleanup script chopped off the subject ("Guru Ji" or "Aap") somewhere — probably mistakenly stripped a line that contained the subject because it looked too short or like a running header. Let me check the raw OCR.

> AGENT

Found it. The raw OCR had it right: line 3206 ended with `ਗੁਰੂ ਜੀ` and line 3207 continued `ਨੇ ਤੜਕੇ ਹੀ ਬਾਹਰ ਚਲੇ ਜਾਣਾ` — a perfectly coherent sentence broken across two lines. Let me see what my cleanup did to the connecting line.

> AGENT

> ...ਵੇਖਕੇ ਹੋਰ ਠਹਿਰ ਪਏ। ਹੁਣ ਇਉਂ ਦੀ ਵਰਤੋਂ ਹੋ ਗਈ ਕਿ ਗੁਰੂ ਜੀ **ਨੇ** ਤੜਕੇ ਹੀ ਬਾਹਰ ਚਲੇ ਜਾਣਾ ਤੇ ਓਥੇ...

("...He stayed longer. Now it became Guru Ji's practice that he would go out at dawn and there...")

It's perfectly coherent — the `ਗੁਰੂ ਜੀ ਨੇ` was wrapping across two lines because the source book has fixed-width print line wrapping (~50 chars per line). I showed you the snippet starting mid-paragraph which made it look broken.

**But this reveals a real issue with the output:** the text still has the print typesetting line breaks every ~50 chars. For a reader app you want **flowing paragraphs** with line breaks only between paragraphs, so the renderer can reflow naturally based on screen width. Let me add paragraph reflow to the cleanup.

Script ready. **Your turn — Google Cloud setup** (one-time, ~10 min):

```sh
# 1. Install the Python client (no GCP work yet)
pip3 install google-cloud-vision
```

```text
# 2. In your browser:
#    a) Go to https://console.cloud.google.com/
#    b) Create a new project (or pick an existing one). Note the project ID.
#    c) Enable the Cloud Vision API:
#       https://console.cloud.google.com/apis/library/vision.googleapis.com
#       → click "Enable" for your project
#    d) Create a service account:
#       https://console.cloud.google.com/iam-admin/serviceaccounts
#       → "Create Service Account" → name it "lipi-ocr" → skip the optional grants
#    e) Generate a JSON key for that service account:
#       Click the service account → "Keys" tab → "Add Key" → "JSON" → downloads a .json file
```

```sh
# 3. Save the key somewhere private and point env var at it:
mkdir -p ~/.config/lipi
mv ~/Downloads/lipi-ocr-XXXXX.json ~/.config/lipi/vision-key.json
chmod 600 ~/.config/lipi/vision-key.json
export GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS=~/.config/lipi/vision-key.json

# 4. Quick sanity test (single page — costs basically nothing):
cd /Users/sumeet/matrix/web/lipi-dict-rip
python3 scripts/ocr-vision.py 01-nanak --first 108 --last 108
cat sakhis/ocr-output/01-nanak/raw-pages-vision/page-0108.txt
```

**12 of 61 chapters missing from DB** — even chapter 39 "Milk or Blood" which we definitely saw OCR'd correctly. Let me find why.

> AGENT

Found the bug. The garbage filter uses `c.isalpha()` to count alphabetic characters, but Python treats Gurmukhi combining marks (vowel signs, halant, addak) as category `Mn` not `L`, so `.isalpha()` returns False for them. "੩੯. ਦੁੱਧ ਕਿ" has only 3 base letters (ਦ ਧ ਕ) — below the `alpha < 4` threshold — so the line gets dropped before chapter detection. Same problem strips "ਲਹੂ".

Let me fix `is_garbage_line` to count the full Gurmukhi range (letters + marks) as meaningful content.

> AGENT

Filter works correctly now. Let me re-run cleanup + DB build and verify the 12 missing chapters are recovered.

> AGENT

> AGENT

> AGENT

The remaining 4 misses are due to the build script's chapter regex needing a `।` to terminate the title — but when the original heading wraps and gets reflowed without a terminator, body content runs into the title. Let me check what's happening with 28, 48, 49 in the cleaned text.

> AGENT

Chapter 28's body genuinely doesn't exist in the OCR (only TOC entry + stray page-number "੨੮"s) — an upstream source issue. Let me check 48/49 raw structure to design the fix for the merge issue.

> AGENT

All three remaining (39/48/49) share one pattern: chapter heading line lacks a `।` terminator (wrapped across lines, or ended with footnote `*`). The build script's title regex needs that terminator. Fix: in the reflow loop, when a chapter heading lacks sentence punctuation, treat short following lines as title continuations and insert `।` before the body.

Done. Two things landed:

**1. Chapter coverage fix** (`scripts/clean-ocr.py`)
- `is_garbage_line` was counting characters with `.isalpha()`, which returns False for Gurmukhi combining marks (Mn category) — so "੩੯. ਦੁੱਧ ਕਿ" had only 3 "alpha" chars (ਦ ਧ ਕ) and got dropped. Switched to counting the Gurmukhi Unicode range directly.
- Added title-merge in the reflow loop: when a chapter heading lacks `।`/`?`/`!`, fold short following lines into the title and append `।` so the build splitter can find the title/body boundary. Catches both wrapped titles ("੩੯. ਦੁੱਧ ਕਿ" + "ਲਹੂ") and footnote-marker titles ("੪੮. ਕੁਰਛੇਤਰ ਫੇਰੀ*").

Result: Nanak coverage 49/61 → 60/61 (28 is an upstream OCR gap — body absent from source). Total sakhis across all 6 books: **376 → 490**, with Asht Guru Chamatkar nearly doubling (101 → 192).

**2. Reader UI** (Next.js 16, async params)
- `app/page.tsx` — guru library (filtered to gurus with sakhis)
- `app/guru/[id]/page.tsx` — sakhi list grouped by book, with ordinal + en title
- `app/sakhi/[id]/page.tsx` — reader with prev/next nav
- `components/SakhiReader.tsx` — paragraph-aligned PA/EN side-by-side, Gurmukhi tokens wired to existing `HoverWord` (morpheme popover stays untouched).

> DEVELOPER

what is ਨਾਉਂ is it a real word and has it been used correctly?

> AGENT

Yes, ਨਾਉਂ (nāuṁ) is a real word — it's an older/literary Punjabi word for "name", same meaning as the more modern ਨਾਮ (nām). Bhai Vir Singh (1872–1957) consistently uses ਨਾਉਂ throughout his writing; it's the dialect/era register he wrote in.

The three Punjabi words for "name":
- **ਨਾਮ** (nām) — standard modern Punjabi, from Sanskrit नाम
- **ਨਾਉਂ** (nāuṁ) — older literary form, what Bhai Vir Singh uses
- **ਨਾਂ** (nāṁ) — colloquial contraction

Used correctly everywhere in this sakhi:
- ਗੁਰੂ ਜੀ ਦਾ **ਨਾਉਂ** ਸ੍ਰੀ ਗੁਰੂ ਨਾਨਕ ਦੇਵ ਜੀ ਹੈ — "Guru ji's **name** is Sri Guru Nanak Dev Ji"
- ਉਸਦਾ **ਨਾਉਂ** ਸੀ ਰਾਇ ਭੋਇ ਦੀ ਤਲਵੰਡੀ — "its **name** was Rai Bhoi di Talwandi"
- ਉਸ ਦਾ **ਨਾਉਂ** ਸੀ 'ਨਾਨਕੀ' — "her **name** was 'Nanki'"

All grammatically correct (noun, used after possessive ਦਾ/ਦੀ). Worth keeping as-is for archival fidelity — modernizing it to ਨਾਮ would change Bhai Vir Singh's voice. The hover-word feature will be useful here so beginners learning Punjabi understand it means "name".

whats this word: ਘੱਲਿਆ

> AGENT

**ਘੱਲਿਆ** (ghallia) — past tense, masculine singular of the verb **ਘੱਲਣਾ** (ghallna) = "to send".

Translation: "**sent**" / "**dispatched**"

Used in sakhi 1: *"ਰਬ ਜੀ ਨੇ ਉਹਨਾਂ ਨੂੰ ਘੱਲਿਆ ਸੀ..."* = "The Lord **had sent** them..."

Verb forms:
- ਘੱਲਣਾ — to send (infinitive)
- ਘੱਲਿਆ — sent (he/it was sent, masc. sing.)
- ਘੱਲੀ — sent (fem. sing.)
- ਘੱਲੇ — sent (plural)
- ਘੱਲਣ — they may send (subjunctive, also used in sakhi: "ਰਬ ਜੀ ਮੇਰੇ ਲਈ ਵੀਰ ਬੀ ਘੱਲਣ" = "may the Lord send me a brother too")

It's a native Indo-Aryan verb (not a Sanskrit/Persian loan), common in colloquial Punjabi for "send" — though modern standard prefers ਭੇਜਣਾ (bhejna). Bhai Vir Singh's old-register diction.

Yes — `lipi/lipi.db`, table `morpheme_cache`. Schema I just upgraded:

```sql
CREATE TABLE morpheme_cache (
  script TEXT NOT NULL,           -- "gurmukhi"
  surface TEXT NOT NULL,          -- the word, e.g. ਆਤਮਾ
  morpheme_json TEXT NOT NULL,    -- full Morpheme blob (gloss, romanization,
                                  -- dictionary_entries from Punjabipedia, components, etc.)
  processed INTEGER DEFAULT 0,    -- 0 = auto-fetched, 1 = manually verified
  updated_at TEXT,
  PRIMARY KEY (script, surface)
);
```

Each lookup goes Cache → Punjabipedia (live, on miss) → nano-gpt → write to cache. So every word ever hovered IS stored.

Quick clarification before I build it:

What counts as "the dictionary thing done"? Three readings:

1. **Strict Punjabipedia hit** — only words where `dictionary_entries.length > 0` can ever be marked processed. Words like ਘੱਲਿਆ or ਨਾਉਂ (not in Punjabipedia's index because they're inflected/archaic) could never be marked verified.

2. **Dictionary entry exists somehow** — either from Punjabipedia OR a manually-entered fallback you've curated. If Punjabipedia misses, you'd manually add a dictionary entry before being allowed to verify.

3. **Punjabipedia attempted + result is what it is** — we tried, and whatever Punjabipedia returned (entries or none) is fine, as long as you've manually checked the gloss/romanization/decomposition makes sense.

All three changes applied. Reload http://localhost:3000/sakhi/1:

**Hover behavior**
- **Mouse over** a word → popover opens after a 200ms delay (no accidental triggers on quick mouse-throughs)
- **Mouse leaves** word AND popover → closes after 250ms
- Move the mouse INTO the popover and it stays open (you can read entries, scroll, click links)
- **Click** the word → "sticky" mode: stays open until you click outside or click the word again

**Smart positioning** (via React portal)
- Popover rendered to `document.body`, positioned with `position: fixed`
- Measures the trigger word's `getBoundingClientRect()` on open
- Centers under the word, but **clamps** to viewport so it never bleeds off the left/right edges
- **Flips above** the word if there's not enough room below
- Re-positions on scroll/resize

**Punjabipedia entries collapsed**
- The dictionary entries (often 9+ paragraphs of dense Punjabi) are hidden behind a `▸ Show N Punjabipedia entries` toggle
- Click to expand → scrollable panel (max-h-64) so the popover stays compact
- Default view is just the headline: word, romanization, gloss, lemma note, decomposition components, verify badge — clean and short

WHen going from one popup to another it somestiems does not over the popup

> AGENT

That's a race condition — when you hover from word A to word B, A's 250ms close timer fires *after* B's 200ms open timer, so A's close blanks out B. Let me fix it so each word can only close itself if it's still the active one.

> AGENT

Also cancel pending timers when the word is deactivated externally (parent opened a different popup):

> AGENT

Fixed two ways:

1. **Parent-side guard** — `setActiveWord((prev) => prev === key ? null : prev)`. When word A's late close-timer fires, it only blanks the active word *if A is still the active one*. If B has already taken over, A's close is a no-op.

2. **Local timer cleanup in `HoverWord`** — when `isActive` flips externally (B took over), any pending close-timer on this component gets cancelled immediately, so it never fires a stale `onClose` at all.

Hover from word to word should be smooth now — moving the mouse onto a new word always opens its popup, never gets blanked out by the previous one closing.

**How it works now:**
- Click 🔊 in the popover → browser hits `/api/tts?word=...`
- First call: server fetches MP3 from Google Translate's TTS endpoint (`tl=pa` = Punjabi voice), caches it in `lipi.db.audio_cache` as a BLOB
- Subsequent calls (anyone, anywhere): served instantly from cache, ~7ms
- Audio plays via a regular `<audio>` element — no client-side voice install required

Reload http://localhost:3000/sakhi/1, hover any word, click 🔊 — you should hear it. While it's playing the icon changes to 🔉 and the button disables so you can't double-trigger. Clicking another word's 🔊 stops the previous one first.

Audio cache persists across server restarts (it's SQLite). Wipe with `DELETE FROM audio_cache;` if you ever want to refetch.

**Re: "why does it not work with native Punjabi?"**

It's a genuine quirk of how Google's voices were trained, not something we caused. Two contributing reasons:

1. **Word-final schwa deletion is "more aggressive" in the pa-IN model.** Punjabi natively drops the inherent schwa /ə/ from most final syllables — ਪਿੰਡ is /pɪɳɖ/ not /pɪɳɖə/. The pa-IN voice was trained to do this, but its prosody model goes one step further on isolated single words: it treats the final stop as unreleased (lips/tongue close but no audible burst), which is what fluent speakers do mid-sentence but not when citing a word in isolation. So the consonant is technically "there" in the articulatory model, but inaudible.

2. **Hindi keeps the schwa more often, and Devanagari training data has fewer "isolated single word" examples.** When you feed Devanagari पिंड to the *same Punjabi voice*, the model maps it through Devanagari rules first (which more often expect a released final), then the Punjabi voice articulates it — so you get the Punjabi accent but with the final ḍ properly released.

Basically Google's pa-IN model over-applies a sentence-internal rule (unreleased final stops) to citation-form single words. Devanagari input bypasses that rule. The voice/accent is still Punjabi — only the script-side phonological pipeline differs.

If you ever want to revert when Google fixes their model: delete the `gurmukhiToDevanagari(word)` line and pass `word` directly.

> AGENT

Congrats! Here's the cheapest path that scales sanely. I'll recommend a stack, then explain what changes in your current code.

## Recommended stack: Vercel + Supabase + Cloudflare R2

| Layer | Service | Free tier | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hosting** | Vercel | Hobby plan free; 100GB bandwidth/mo | Zero-config for Next.js 16, instant GitHub deploys |
| **Database + Auth** | Supabase | 500MB Postgres + 50k monthly active users + 1GB file storage | One signup gives you DB + accounts + email/Google login |
| **Audio storage** | Cloudflare R2 | 10GB storage + **no egress fees** | Audio streaming bandwidth is what kills you at scale on S3/Supabase; R2 has zero egress charge — critical for this app |
| **TTS / OCR** | Your existing GCP project | Chirp 3 HD: 100k chars/month free | Cached per-word forever, so it stays near-free |
| **Domain + DNS + CDN** | Cloudflare (already!) | Free | Point akhar.io at Vercel |

**Total monthly cost at zero traffic: $0.** Even at a few thousand readers a month: still likely $0. When you outgrow Supabase free, it's $25/mo, Vercel Pro $20/mo — that's roughly when you'd be ready to think about revenue.

## What changes in your current code

You've built almost everything in a way that ports cleanly. The work splits into 4 chunks:

1. **`lipi.db` (SQLite, local) → Supabase Postgres.** Same schema, just different driver. Replace `better-sqlite3` with `pg` or `@supabase/supabase-js`. The `morpheme_cache` and `audio_cache` tables move 1:1. The read-only `data/sakhis.db` (the corpus) is interesting — that's static archival data, could stay as a bundled SQLite file in the Vercel deploy (read-only), or move to Postgres too.

2. **`audio_cache` BLOBs → R2 objects.** Don't store MP3s in Postgres at scale — bandwidth is the killer. Each cached audio becomes a key in R2 (`audio/<voice>/<sha1(text)>.mp3`); the DB just records "exists, generated at X." R2 streams them with zero egress cost.

3. **Add Supabase Auth.** Drop in `@supabase/ssr` — gives you `signUp`, `signIn`, magic links, Google OAuth in maybe 50 lines. Wrap protected routes (your reader could be public; bookmarks/notes/progress would be per-user).

4. **Deploy: connect your GitHub repo to Vercel, set env vars, point akhar.io DNS.** ~15 minutes.

## What to do first (a concrete first day)

If you want me to start, the order I'd go:

1. Push the current code to a GitHub repo (private is fine — Vercel hobby supports it)
2. Create a Supabase project, get the Postgres URL + service key
3. Migrate the schema (`morpheme_cache`, `audio_cache`) to Postgres
4. Create a Cloudflare R2 bucket, get an API token
5. Move `audio_cache` from BLOB-in-DB to "key in R2, metadata in Postgres"
6. Add a minimal Supabase Auth login page
7. Deploy to Vercel, point akhar.io at it

Want me to start with step 1 (initializing the repo + dependencies) or do you want to talk through any of these choices first? If you'd rather go full-Cloudflare (Pages + D1 + R2) for ideological consistency with the domain registrar, that's a viable alternative — slightly cheaper at huge scale, slightly more friction for Next.js right now.

just a question could I get in trouble for charging for this app?

> AGENT

Short answer: **probably fine for the core idea, but specific content sources matter, and there's a separate community-perception risk that's worth thinking about even when there's no legal one.**

The actual risk areas:

**1. Bhai Vir Singh's Punjabi originals** — He died in 1957. Under Indian copyright (life + 60 years), his works entered public domain on Jan 1, 2018. So the Punjabi text is fine to use commercially in India. Western countries use life + 70, so they're protected in the UK/US/EU until ~2027–28. In practice his works are reprinted everywhere without enforcement and the foundation that manages his estate (Bhai Vir Singh Sahitya Sadan) is mission-driven, not litigious — but technically there's a window where US users could be a legal grey area.

**2. The English translations** — This is the bigger one. If you OCR'd English translations from sikhbookclub.com or similar, those translators/publishers hold separate copyright on the translation even when the original is PD. The safe play: rewrite the English yourself (which you're already doing in body_en hand-cleanup) or commission/generate your own. Don't ship verbatim someone else's translation behind a paywall.

**3. Punjabipedia** — You're scraping their entries into your popovers. Check their TOS; most wiki-style projects are CC-BY-SA which requires attribution AND that derivatives stay openly licensed. You already show ਸਰੋਤ markers in the popup which is most of the attribution work; the share-alike clause could complicate selling the dataset itself, but probably doesn't affect selling app access.

**4. Google Cloud TTS audio** — GCP's TOS explicitly allow commercial use of outputs. No issue.

**5. Trademark on "akhar"** — Common Punjabi word, unlikely to be trademarked but worth a 10-min USPTO/IPO search before printing merch.

**The non-legal risk** worth flagging: Sikh community norms around free access to spiritual content are strong (seva, langar). Every major Sikh app — SikhNet, SikhiToTheMax, Gurbani Now — is donation-based, not paid. Charging for sakhi access (vs. e.g., charging for *user features* like cross-device bookmarks, downloadable audio, personal notes, progress tracking) tends to get blowback. The common pattern is: **core reading free, "pro" features paid.** This sidesteps both the "you're selling sacred content" criticism and most of the copyright grey-area pressure.

If you go that route, you're effectively only monetizing your *engineering*, not the texts — which is on much firmer ground both legally and reputationally. And it lets you grow the user base faster, because free is the lowest-friction onboarding.

**Bottom line:** Won't get sued at small scale. Get a lawyer before raising money or hitting US scale. And consider the freemium model regardless of legal risk — it's a better fit for the space.

- **Sakhis** are narrative literature *about* the Gurus, written by authors. They've been commercially published as books for over a century. Bhai Vir Singh himself made his living from publishing and selling these. Singh Brothers Amritsar, Bhai Vir Singh Sahitya Sadan, Sikh Book Club, Amazon — all sell sakhi books at full retail.

So **selling akhar.io is the digital equivalent of selling a sakhi book — totally normal and well-established commercial territory.** No different from a Penguin Classics paid ebook of, say, the Mahabharata translations.

The actual legal points I raised still stand:
1. Bhai Vir Singh's Punjabi text — PD in India since 2018, grey in US/UK until ~2027 (low practical risk)
2. Don't ship someone else's verbatim English translation — write your own (you're already doing this)
3. Punjabipedia attribution per their license (you already display ਸਰੋਤ markers)

But the "community will object to monetization" concern I raised was overblown for sakhi content specifically. Apologies for the overcorrection — your instinct is right. A paid akhar.io is a normal product, not a transgression.

Freemium is still a *good* business choice (lower friction, bigger top of funnel, lets you charge for engineering features like cross-device sync, audio downloads, bookmarks), but for product/growth reasons — not because charging would be wrong.

Big opportunity — and there's a real gap. Quick market scan:

**No premium Punjabi learning app exists at scale.** Duolingo has 40+ languages but still no Punjabi (~100M+ speakers globally — by speaker count it's bigger than Korean, Italian, Polish — but underserved because it's "ethnic" not "tourist"). Pimsleur has audio-only Punjabi at $15/mo. The rest is YouTube + low-quality Indian apps. The diaspora demand (UK / Canada / US / Australia Sikh families wanting kids to keep the language) is high-willingness-to-pay and currently un-monetized.

**Your existing build is already 70% of a premium learning platform**, you just haven't framed it that way:
- Parallel text reader = LingQ / Beelinguapp model (narrative-driven learning, currently in vogue vs. Duolingo-style drills)
- Hover popover with romanization + etymology + Punjabipedia = best-in-class lookup
- Sentence-level Chirp 3 audio = built-in shadowing/listening practice
- Bhai Vir Singh sakhis = differentiated content (cultural+religious vs. generic textbook material — and parents/kids both engage with it)

**What you'd add to position as "learning platform" v1:**
1. **Accounts** (already in the plan — Supabase Auth)
2. **SRS deck**: clicking any word during reading adds it to a personal Anki-style spaced-repetition deck. Free tier = 50 words, paid = unlimited. Huge perceived value.
3. **Progress tracking**: "you've read 12 sakhis, know 340 words, listened to 2.1 hours." The dashboard is what justifies subscription pricing.
4. **Difficulty curve**: tag content by level (simple folk tales → Bhai Vir Singh's literary register). Start beginners gently.
5. **Mobile-first PWA**: most learners read on phones. Next.js PWA is straightforward.
6. **Listening mode**: shuffle through sentences with audio + show meaning after delay (passive practice).

**Pricing benchmark**: $8–12/mo, $60–80/year is the niche-language-learning sweet spot. Diaspora parents pay $30+/mo for kids' heritage-language tutoring on iTalki / OutSchool — so $10/mo for an app is easy.

**Freemium structure that works for this market:**
- Free forever: read the whole corpus, basic hover lookups, listen to audio
- Premium ($10/mo): SRS deck unlimited, progress tracking, sentence-level "shadow this" exercises, offline downloads, advanced grammar notes

The architecture we just started (Vercel + Supabase + R2) handles all of this with zero changes. Accounts → Supabase Auth. SRS state → Postgres table. Audio → R2.

## 3. Add the new account to your `gh` CLI

So you can run `gh` commands as `reliq-digital` (creating PRs, repos, etc.) without juggling browser logins. `gh` supports multiple accounts:

```
gh auth login             # walk through it, choose GitHub.com → HTTPS or SSH → login via browser → sign in as reliq-digital
gh auth status            # should show both accounts
gh auth switch            # toggle between them
```

For day-to-day on this repo, since the remote is SSH (`git@github.com:...`), it'll work regardless of which gh account is "active" — SSH uses your `~/.ssh` keys, not `gh`. So as long as your SSH key is added to **either** GitHub account, push/pull just works.

  - `service_role` `secret` — also `eyJ...` but click "Reveal" first — **copy this** (server-only, will become `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`)

## 2. Database connection string

**Project Settings → Database** (left sidebar → ⚙️ → "Database" tab)

Scroll down to **"Connection string"**. There are tabs: PSQL, URI, .NET, JDBC, Golang… **stay on "URI"**.

Above the connection strings there's a dropdown labeled **"Connection mode"** with three options:
- ❌ Direct connection (don't use — Vercel can't reach it)
- ❌ Transaction pooler (limited, no prepared statements)
- ✅ **Session pooler** — use this one

Copy the string. It'll look like:
```
postgresql://postgres.abcdefghij:[YOUR-PASSWORD]@aws-0-eu-west-2.pooler.supabase.com:5432/postgres
```

The `[YOUR-PASSWORD]` is a literal placeholder — **replace it** with the database password you set when creating the project. (If you didn't save it, you can reset it under Database → "Reset database password" — but that'll invalidate any existing connections.)

There is a new method no? https://github.com/orgs/supabase/discussions/29260

> AGENT

Good catch — Supabase migrated to a new API key system. The new ones are:

- **`sb_publishable_<random>_<checksum>`** — replaces the old `anon` key (client-side, safe to ship in browser)
- **`sb_secret_<random>_<checksum>`** — replaces the old `service_role` key (server-only)

Benefits: opaque format with built-in checksum (faster leak detection), better key rotation, and the platform supports multiple publishable keys per project (so you can revoke just one without taking down everything).

The MCP can fetch them, but the auto-mode classifier blocks anything that smells like "extract keys". I'll need you to paste them.

**Where to find them in your dashboard:**

**Project Settings → API Keys** (this is the new tab — separate from the legacy "API" tab that has the old anon/service_role JWTs)

You'll see two sections:
- **Publishable key** — one row, starts `sb_publishable_...` — paste this
- **Secret key** — click **"Reveal"** — starts `sb_secret_...` — paste this

Once you give me both, I'll:
1. Write them to `.env.local` (gitignored) under `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` + `SUPABASE_SECRET_KEY`
2. Update the `lib/supabase/server.ts` and `client.ts` code to read the new var names (the SDK accepts the new key format as a drop-in)

Here's the full walkthrough — should take ~5 minutes:

## 1. Open R2 in Cloudflare

Go to **https://dash.cloudflare.com** → in the left sidebar look for **"R2 Object Storage"** (might just say "R2"). Click it.

If you've never used R2 before, you'll see a "Purchase R2" or "Subscribe to R2" screen first:
- Click through to enable it
- You'll need to add a payment method (debit/credit card)
- **Free tier**: 10 GB storage + zero egress fees + 1M class-A ops/mo + 10M class-B ops/mo. We're nowhere near these limits — won't get charged.

## 2. Create the bucket

Once R2 is enabled, you'll be on the R2 overview page. 

- Click **"Create bucket"** (top right)
- **Bucket name**: `akhar-audio`
- **Location**: leave on "Automatic" (Cloudflare picks closest)
- **Storage class**: Standard
- Click **"Create bucket"**

## 3. Grab the Account ID

On the R2 overview page, look at the **right sidebar** — there's a section labeled **"Account details"** with **"Account ID"** below it. It's a hex string like `abc123def456...`. **Copy this** — that's value 1 of 4.

## 4. Create API token

From the R2 page, click **"Manage R2 API Tokens"** (top right, near "Create bucket"). Then:

- Click **"Create API Token"**
- **Token name**: `akhar-prod`
- **Permissions**: select **"Object Read & Write"**
- **Specify bucket(s)**: pick **"Apply to specific buckets only"** → select `akhar-audio`
- **TTL**: "Forever"
- Click **"Create API Token"**

## 5. Copy the credentials (IMPORTANT — only shown once)

After creating, you'll see a screen with:
- **Access Key ID** → copy this (value 2)
- **Secret Access Key** → copy this (value 3) — this is the **last time you'll ever see it**, save it now
- **Use jurisdiction-specific endpoints for S3 clients** section, which shows:
  - **Endpoint**: `https://<your-account-id>.r2.cloudflarestorage.com` → copy this (value 4)

If you close that page without copying, the secret is gone forever and you'll have to create a new token.

Honestly, you're right to push back on this. Let me think out loud about it.

**What actually needs to be cloud vs what doesn't:**

| Thing | Needs cloud? | Why |
|---|---|---|
| User accounts | **Yes** | Auth, multi-device persistence |
| User's SRS cards / bookmarks / progress | **Yes** | Same — they switch devices |
| Sakhi corpus (the texts) | **No** | Static, ~4.4MB, doesn't change at runtime |
| Morpheme cache (gloss/dictionary entries) | **No** | You author this on your machine, prod is read-only |
| Audio MP3s | **Yes-ish** | Will hit ~700MB at full corpus — too big to ship in deploy bundle. Needs R2. |

**The crucial insight: production is INHERENTLY read-only for caches.** You explicitly said audio shouldn't generate on first hover for new words. Same logic applies to morphemes — new words shouldn't trigger Punjabipedia + nano-gpt calls on user hover. So why migrate them to a DB Postgres can write to? Just ship them as bundled data.

## Simpler architecture (your instinct)

1. **`data/sakhis.db`** — keep as SQLite, commit to repo. Vercel reads it via `better-sqlite3` (Vercel supports it). 4.4MB.
2. **`data/morphemes.db` (or `morphemes.json`)** — same. Commit your local cache to the repo. ~5-10MB at full corpus. Prewarming pushes new words to your local file → git commit → deploy. Prod can't add new words but doesn't need to.
3. **Audio in R2** — only because of size. ~2KB per word, will grow to ~700MB total. Too big to bundle.
4. **Supabase** — only for the things that genuinely need it: user accounts + SRS + bookmarks + progress.

**Pros over what I was building:**
- Drops the morpheme_cache + audio_meta Postgres migration entirely
- Hover lookups in prod = zero DB calls, instant (read from bundled file)
- Prewarm workflow stays simple: local SQLite, git commit
- Reproducible: same `git checkout` = same exact cache state
- Easier to version, diff, audit

**Cons:**
- Each `git commit` after prewarming adds DB diffs (binary diff is messy for SQLite, but JSON would be diffable)
- Vercel bundle gets a bit larger with each deploy (negligible)
- New words still need R2 setup before deploy

## Trade-off question

R2 for audio is unavoidable if you ever process all 405 sakhis (~700MB). But for **right now** (sakhi 1 = ~1.8MB of audio), you could even ship audio bundled too — `public/audio/<voice>/<hash>.mp3` as static assets — and add R2 later when corpus grows.

**My revised recommendation:**
- Keep morpheme_cache + sakhi corpus as bundled SQLite (no migration)
- For now, ship audio as `public/audio/...` static files too (no R2 needed yet)
- Supabase ONLY for user accounts + learning state
- Add R2 later when audio bundle gets > ~50MB

This means **you can deploy today without R2** and don't pay anything extra. Want me to pivot to this?

> AGENT

Two separate things — let me untangle them:

**Where the audio LIVES on the server** ≠ **whether USERS can download it for offline use**.

For users:
- **Offline playback (downloadable) is a great premium feature.** Every comparable app gates it behind paid — Pimsleur, Spotify, Duolingo Super, Apple Music, Netflix all do this. Big diaspora use case: parents playing sakhis during car rides / school runs / walks where data is spotty.
- **Implementation later**: it's a Service Worker / PWA feature — a "Download this sakhi" button that pre-caches the MP3s in the browser's Cache API. Doesn't change the server-side architecture at all; it's just a frontend feature that pre-fetches and stores.
- **Don't build it now** — ship free streaming first, add downloads when you have paying users asking for it.

For the server architecture (current decision):
- **Free users get streaming from server.** Tap play → fetch MP3 → play.
- **Where the MP3 lives** is the question: bundled in `/public` or in R2.

So the question becomes: where do MP3s live on the server today?

**My recommendation: ship in `/public/audio/<voice>/<hash>.mp3` for now, migrate to R2 only when needed.**

Numbers:
- Sakhi 1 fully cached audio = 1.8 MB
- All 405 sakhis estimated = ~700 MB
- Vercel deploy size limits: 100 MB per static asset (none of our files hit that), but **total deploy size** practically maxes around 500 MB before deploys get slow/painful
- So bundled `/public` works fine for the first ~100 sakhis or so

This means:
- **Today**: bundle audio in `/public/audio/...`, deploy to Vercel. Zero R2 needed. Zero new accounts. `akhar.io` lives today.
- **When `/public/audio` hits ~100 MB** (~50–80 sakhis processed): migrate to R2. Single config change in `lib/audio-cache.ts`. ~10 min of work + R2 setup. Trivial future cost.
- **When users ask for offline**: build the PWA download button as a premium feature.

**Bottom line: skip R2 for now.** Way faster to ship. We'll add it when there's actually a reason to.

Want me to proceed on this path? It means:
1. Cache stays local SQLite (no Supabase migration for caches)
2. Audio files get moved from SQLite BLOBs → `public/audio/<voice>/<hash>.mp3` (so they're served as Vercel static assets, CDN-cached automatically)
3. Supabase only for user accounts + SRS + bookmarks
4. Push + Vercel deploy + DNS

Way fewer moving parts.

Now: point **akhar.io** at Vercel via Cloudflare DNS.

## 1. Add the domain in Vercel

1. In your Vercel project dashboard → **Settings → Domains**
2. Type `akhar.io` → click **Add**
3. Vercel will show you a record to add. It will be **one** of:
   - **CNAME** → `cname.vercel-dns.com`, OR
   - **A record** → `76.76.21.21` (Vercel's anycast IP)

For an apex domain like `akhar.io`, Vercel usually asks for the **A record `76.76.21.21`**. Note exactly what it tells you — copy the value verbatim.

Also add `www.akhar.io` while you're at it (so both work).

## 2. Add the DNS records in Cloudflare

1. **https://dash.cloudflare.com** → click on `akhar.io`
2. Left sidebar → **DNS → Records**
3. Click **Add record**:
   - **Type**: `A`
   - **Name**: `@` (means the apex, akhar.io itself)
   - **IPv4 address**: `76.76.21.21` (or whatever Vercel told you)
   - **Proxy status**: **DNS only** ⚠️ (the orange cloud OFF — Vercel manages CDN itself, Cloudflare proxy in front breaks SSL handshake)
   - **TTL**: Auto
   - Save
4. Add another record:
   - **Type**: `CNAME`
   - **Name**: `www`
   - **Target**: `cname.vercel-dns.com`
   - **Proxy status**: **DNS only**
   - Save

## 3. Wait + verify

DNS propagation: usually 1–5 minutes through Cloudflare. Vercel polls automatically and will turn the domain status green once it sees the records. SSL cert (via Let's Encrypt) provisions automatically right after — another minute or two.

Tell me when:
- `akhar.io` shows as **Valid Configuration** ✅ in Vercel, AND
- Hitting **https://akhar.io** in a browser loads the reader

If the cert takes ages or you see errors, paste them and I'll debug.

```sh
# 1. Iterate locally

git push

git merge dev
git push

```

so what I think is really important is to hae a a punjabi alphabet learning section. Let's talk aboyt this and how we could do it

> AGENT

Great instinct — for a Punjabi learning app, the alphabet is the literal first thing 90% of learners need. Let me think through this carefully.

## Why this is a strategic feature, not just a feature

1. **It's the funnel top.** Most people searching "learn Punjabi" don't know the alphabet. Without it they bounce. Duolingo's whole onboarding starts with the alphabet for every new script.
2. **It's the SEO play.** "Punjabi alphabet", "Gurmukhi letters", "paintee akhar" — high-volume keywords with weak existing content.
3. **It naturally cross-promotes the reader.** Every example word in the alphabet section can link to a sakhi where the word appears in real classical context. No other app does this.

## What's in scope (Gurmukhi script)

- **35 base letters** (paintee akhar) — the canonical alphabet
- **6 nukta letters** (ਸ਼ ਖ਼ ਗ਼ ਜ਼ ਫ਼ ਲ਼) for borrowed sounds
- **10 vowel signs (matras)** that attach to consonants
- **Special symbols** — tippi (ੰ), bindi (ਂ), addhak (ੱ), halant (੍), bihari (ੀ), etc.
- **Numerals** (੦–੯)

## The thing nobody else does

**Use sakhi vocabulary as the example words.** Most apps teach "ਘ = ਘੋੜਾ (horse)" — generic textbook content. We'd teach "ਘ = ਘੱਲਿਆ (sent), from sakhi 12, where Guru Nanak says..." Every letter becomes a doorway into the corpus, not a flashcard in isolation.

## What I'd build (phased)

### Phase 1: Reference (1 day) — `/alphabet`

- Grid of 35 letters, click any → detail page
- Detail page per letter:
  - The character (large, calligraphic)
  - Its name in Gurmukhi + romanized ("ਜੱਜਾ jajja")
  - Phonetic value (IPA + romanized: /dʒə/, "ja")
  - Audio: play the letter name (Chirp 3, cached in R2)
  - 3-5 example words from sakhis we've processed, each linking to where it appears
  - Optional: stroke order / origin (Brahmi → Sharada → Gurmukhi)

Just *reading content* — no quizzes. Already shippable, already SEO-valuable.

### Phase 2: Recognition practice (2-3 days) — `/alphabet/practice`

- Flashcards: shows letter, you say name in your head, click reveal, mark "knew it / didn't"
- Audio mode: plays letter name, you pick the right letter from 4 options
- Tracks per-user "letters known" via Supabase (`alphabet_progress` table)

### Phase 3: Integration with reader

- Hover any word in a sakhi → popover gains a "letter breakdown" line showing each character with link to its page
- Reading view has a "show letter names" toggle that overlays small romanized letter names above each Gurmukhi character

### Phase 4 (later): Writing practice — `/alphabet/write`

- Touch/mouse trace the letter with correct stroke order
- Premium feature candidate

## Questions for you (architecture)

> DEVELOPER

Before we get started, something that needs to happeb is that we gotta talk about movile. I want tehse to be proper mobile apps. Is tehre a way we can do the web veriosn and movile apss as well>

> AGENT

Yes — multiple paths, with different tradeoffs. Let me lay them out.

## The three real options

### Option A: PWA (Progressive Web App)
Add a manifest + service worker to the existing Next.js app. Users get an "Add to Home Screen" prompt, the app installs like a native app, runs in fullscreen, can work offline, can send push notifications (Android freely, iOS more limited).

- **Cost**: $0 (no app store fees)
- **Time**: ~1-2 days
- **Codebase**: same as web (zero extra code beyond the manifest)
- **Distribution**: people install from akhar.io, NOT from App Store
- **Limitation**: NOT in App Store / Play Store → lower trust, lower discoverability, no "search for Punjabi app" findability

### Option B: Capacitor (web wrapped as native) ⭐ **What I'd recommend**
Capacitor wraps your existing Next.js web app in a native shell (WebView) and ships to both App Store and Play Store. Same codebase as web — no rewrite. Plus native APIs for push notifications, offline downloads, app badges, biometric auth, etc.

- **Cost**: $99/yr (Apple Dev) + $25 one-time (Google Play)
- **Time**: ~1 week first time, hours per update after
- **Codebase**: same as web (one repo, one product)
- **Distribution**: real apps in both stores, akhar.io still serves the web
- **Native features**: progressively add as needed (push for SRS reminders, offline sakhis for premium, etc.)
- **Used by**: Discord (early days), Microsoft Teams, Sworkit, Spotify Lite

### Option C: Expo / React Native (truly native)
Separate native app codebase, share types/logic with web via packages. Real native performance, real native components. This is what you'd build if you were Notion or Linear — eventually they all end up here.

- **Cost**: store fees + significant dev time
- **Time**: ~3-4 weeks to reach web parity, then ongoing parallel development
- **Codebase**: two render targets (web + native), most logic shared
- **When it's right**: when you have users telling you the WebView is too slow

## My honest recommendation: **B (Capacitor)**

For akhar.io specifically:

1. **You already have a Next.js web app that's nearly launch-ready.** Capacitor reuses 100% of it. Option C would mean rebuilding it.
2. **The performance penalty of WebView is invisible for a reading app.** Akhar is text + audio playback + hover popovers — none of these need native rendering speed. (If we were building a Punjabi *video chat* app, different story.)
3. **The store presence is real value.** "Punjabi learning app" → if you're not in App Store/Play Store, you're invisible. Diaspora parents searching for kids' learning apps look in the App Store first, Google second.
4. **You can escape later.** If years from now you want to rewrite specific screens in true native, Capacitor lets you mix WebView screens with native React Native screens in the same app. No lock-in.

## The path I'd actually take

1. **Now → next 1–2 weeks** — make the web responsive and touch-friendly. This benefits desktop AND mobile web AND future native apps. Concretely:
   - Hover popovers → tap-and-hold or tap-to-open on touch devices
   - Sentence play buttons sized for fingers
   - Reader layout works on phone widths (320px+)
   - Bottom-nav pattern on mobile (Home / Library / Deck / Profile)
   - This is needed regardless of native or not

2. **PWA basics** (~1 day after that)
   - manifest.json, app icons, splash screen
   - Service worker for offline caching of read sakhis
   - "Install akhar" prompt on mobile

3. **When ready to launch in stores** (~1 week)
   - Add Capacitor
   - Build for iOS + Android
   - Submit to both stores (1-3 day review)
   - Same web codebase keeps updating both

4. **Later** (post-launch, only if needed) — rewrite hot paths in React Native if WebView feels slow.

## Decision

> AGENT

Yes, Capacitor is the right call for both of your goals. Let me address them specifically:

## "Will the app be accepted on the App Stores?"

**Yes, with one caveat that's easy to satisfy.**

The historical concern: Apple Guideline 4.2 — "apps must include features that go beyond a repackaged website." A bare WebView wrapping akhar.io would get rejected as "just a website."

The fix: add native features that justify the app's existence. For akhar specifically, we'd add:

1. **Push notifications** — "Time to review your Punjabi flashcards" (SRS reminders)
2. **Offline sakhi downloads** — read on flights, on the subway (also a premium tier hook)
3. **Native Sign in with Apple** — mandatory on iOS if you offer any other auth method (we do — email magic links)
4. **Native audio session** — proper background audio playback (continues when phone is locked, integrates with iOS lock-screen controls)
5. **Native share sheet** — "Share this sakhi with a friend"

Each is one Capacitor plugin (`@capacitor/push-notifications`, `@capacitor/filesystem`, `@capacitor/share`, etc.). Apple accepts apps doing this all day. Real examples: Burger King (US), Southwest Airlines, Sworkit, MarketWatch — all Capacitor apps in the App Store.

## "Will the website work mirror perfectly on the mobile app?"

**Yes, that's literally Capacitor's whole point.**

The mobile app *is* the web app — rendered inside a WebView. Two flavors:

- **Bundled mode**: web app is shipped inside the .ipa/.apk. Offline-first. Need a store release to ship new features.
- **Live mode** (recommended for akhar): app loads `https://akhar.io` at runtime. Every web deploy → mobile app updates instantly with no store submission. Store updates only needed when you change native plugins.

For akhar, **live mode is the sweet spot**:
- Update the reader UI → users see it next time they open the app
- Add a new sakhi → in the app immediately
- Change copy on the landing page → live
- Add a new native plugin → THAT needs a store release

This is exactly the workflow you described — "work we do in the website mirrored perfectly on the app store."

**Caveat**: live mode has tradeoffs. If the user is offline, the app shows a blank screen. Solution: cache a basic offline shell + the user's saved sakhis. Capacitor + service worker handles this.

## What this means concretely for the order of work

1. ✅ Now: build the alphabet feature (web only)
2. ✅ Soon: finish auth polish, save-to-deck UI, /deck review page
3. Next: **responsive/touch UX pass** across the whole web app (mobile-friendly first)
4. Then: **PWA basics** (manifest, icons, service worker for offline shell)
5. Then: **Capacitor wrap + store submissions** (App Store + Play Store)
6. Then: **native plugins as needed** (push, downloads, share, Sign in with Apple)

You're approving this path. Now back to the alphabet — you picked:
- **Signed-in only** (gates SEO opportunity but you said you want everyone signed in anyway, consistent with the rest of the app)
- **Both textbook + sakhi examples** (beginner-friendly + the corpus-integration angle)
- **Reference page first, no practice** (ship in a day, validate before adding interactive)

I've read the whole book. Here's what makes it work pedagogically and how I'd map it to akhar:

## The book's actual methodology (49 pages, distilled)

It's a **sequential curriculum**, not a reference. Every page introduces ONE concept, shows examples, then has a reading exercise.

| Section | Pages | What's taught |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Pronunciation primer** | 5–7 | The 10 vowel sounds with English equivalents (u/ū/o, a/ā/æ/ɔ, i/ī/e), diacritic conventions (bar = long, dot = retroflex, h = aspirated), tongue-position diagrams (dental vs retroflex t/d) |
| **2. Letter flashcards** | 8–21 | One letter per page in a big frame: large character + name + 1–2 illustrated example words with romanization + English. NOT a grid — every letter gets its own dedicated page. |
| **3. Letter chart** | 22–23 | All 35 letters in one reference grid + the 5 nukta letters |
| **4. Recognition test** | 24 | Same letters shuffled — "Can you identify these now?" |
| **5. Mukta** (inherent 'a') | 25–26 | Words with NO vowel sign + reading sentences |
| **6. Vowel signs (matras)** | 27–37 | One lesson per matra: Kannā ā, Sihārī i, Bihārī ī, Aunkaṛ u, Dulæṅkaṛ ū, Lāvāṅ e, Dulāvāṅ æ, Hoṛā o, Kanɔṛā ɔ. Each shows construction (ਕ + ਾ = ਕਾ), 15–25 example words, then sentences using them. |
| **7. Gemination** | 38–39 | Adhikk ੱ — doubles the next consonant |
| **8. Nasalization** | 40–43 | Tippī ੰ (above-the-line) and Bindī ਂ (above-the-curve) |
| **9. Subscript Rārā** | 44 | Pairī Rārā ੍ਰ — conjuncts |
| **10. H at end/foot** | 45 | Tone marks, subscript h |
| **11. Glossary** | 46–49 | Every word from every lesson, with page refs |

**The structural insight**: every lesson teaches one thing, then **immediately uses it in real sentences**. By page 26 the learner is already reading sentences like "ਬਸ ਫੜ ਘਰ ਵੱਲ ਚੱਲ" (catch the bus, go home). Confidence-building through reading practice from day one.

## What's wrong with my current `/alphabet`

It's a reference grid. The book is a curriculum. Two completely different things.

A reference page is for someone who already knows the alphabet and needs to look one up. A curriculum is for someone learning from scratch — which is our actual user.

## How I'd restructure

**Restructure `/alphabet` from "grid" to "lesson path":**

```
/alphabet                       — Lesson index (1, 2, 3... with progress dots)
/alphabet/pronunciation         — Lesson 0: vowel/consonant system overview
/alphabet/letters/ੳ             — Lesson 1: letter ੳ (Ūṛā) — single letter, big, examples, audio
/alphabet/letters/ਅ             — Lesson 2: letter ਅ
...
/alphabet/letters/ੜ             — Lesson 35: letter ੜ
/alphabet/nukta                 — Lesson 36: the 5 nukta letters as a group
/alphabet/chart                 — Reference: all letters in one grid (the page you wanted to scrap, but as a separate reference, not the entry point)
/alphabet/recognition           — Exercise: shuffled letters, click to name
/alphabet/mukta                 — Lesson: inherent 'a' (reading words without vowel signs)
/alphabet/matras/kanna          — Lesson: vowel sign ਾ (ā)
/alphabet/matras/sihari         — Lesson: vowel sign ਿ (i)
...
/alphabet/adhikk                — Lesson: gemination ੱ
/alphabet/tippi                 — Lesson: nasalization ੰ
/alphabet/bindi                 — Lesson: nasalization ਂ
/alphabet/pairin-rara           — Lesson: subscript ਰ
/alphabet/tone-h                — Lesson: tone-marking h
```

Each lesson is a focused, single-concept page with:
- Big visual of the new concept
- Audio for the concept and the examples
- 5–15 real Punjabi example words from the book's wordlists (manually curated, you wanted this)
- A short reading-practice section: 3–5 sentences using only what's been taught so far
- "Mark complete" button → writes to a new `alphabet_progress` per-user table (so you can resume + see overall completion %)
- Prev / next lesson nav

Total: ~50 lessons (35 letters + 5 nukta-as-group + 10 matras + 5 other concepts). Roughly the full book translated into the medium.
