Doc lives at `docs/exam-prep-research.md`. Here's the strategic summary so it's all in one place:

## The market gap is real

**There is no online prep platform for GCSE or A-Level Panjabi.** UK supplementary schools use the JS Nagra books + photocopied past papers. Heritage families pay £15–40/hr for iTalki tutors. That's the entire market.

## What the three books are actually for

| Book | Maps to | What it teaches |
|---|---|---|
| **Book 1** (49 pp) | Already in akhar | Script — alphabet, vowels, diacritics |
| **Book 2** (69 pp) | Future feature | Beginner readers — character-driven narratives (Manpreet, Gurpal, Charanjit...) with comprehension Qs + mirror tasks |
| **Book 3** (93 pp) | Future feature | Intermediate readers — longer narratives + animal fables (greedy dog, meeting of mice) |

The format we'd steal: every lesson ends with **"Like Manpreet, write 10 sentences about yourself."** Mirror tasks force productive output, which is exactly what GCSE Papers 2 + 4 (Speaking + Writing) test.

## GCSE Panjabi (AQA 8683)

- **4 papers** × 25% each: Listening (35–45 min), Speaking (NEA, 7–12 min), Reading (45–60 min, includes PA→EN translation), Writing (1–1h15, includes EN→PA translation)
- **Foundation tier** (grades 1–5) or **Higher tier** (grades 4–9)
- **3 themes / 12 topics** — family, tech, free time, customs, town, social issues, global issues, travel, studies, school, post-16, jobs
- **Full grammar list** captured in the doc (subjunctive, imperatives, participles, future, passives, postpositions, conditionals, etc.)

## A-Level Panjabi (AQA 7682)

- **3 papers**: Reading+Writing (42.5%), literary essays (20%), Listening+Reading+Writing (37.5%)
- **5 prescribed texts/films**: *Noori* (B.S. Shaad), *Ristian de Rang* (G.S. Bainiwal), *Savee Pattar* (M. Singh), *Asa Nu Maan Watna Da* (2004), *Jatt and Juliet 2* (2013)
- **4 research topics**: women's role / social issues / tourism / Partition 1947
- **No dictionaries** in any paper. **300-word essays.**

## Positioning + monetisation

| Tier | Price | What's in it |
|---|---|---|
| **Free** | £0 | Everything we have today: alphabet curriculum + sakhi reader |
| **akhar Pro** | £8–12/mo | GCSE topic packs, writing/translation drills with AI marking, past-paper practice, unlimited SRS, progress tracking |
| **akhar A-Level** | £15–20/mo or £150/yr | Annotated prescribed-text readers, film transcripts, research-topic Panjabi-language source library, essay marking |

**The killer feature**: AQA-rubric-aligned AI marking of writing + translation answers. Feed student response + AQA mark scheme to an LLM, return scored rubric breakdown + error highlights + suggested rewrites. Replaces the most mechanical (and expensive) part of private tutoring.

## Recommended build order from here

1. **SRS review UI** — cards already save to Supabase via the deck button, but there's no review flow yet. Smallest gap to close, biggest learning-platform unlock.
2. **GCSE topic packs** — start with Theme 1 Topic 1 (Me, family, friends). Vocab + example sentences, AQA-aligned.
3. **Reading lessons** — Book 2/3 format, our own narratives written around GCSE themes.
4. **Writing drills with AI marking** — the killer feature, ships the Pro tier.
5. **Translation drills** — both directions, auto-marked.
6. **Listening practice** — convert reading lessons to audio + comprehension Qs (we already have the TTS).
7. **A-Level tier** — annotated texts/films + research source library.

Sources:
- [JS Nagra, Panjabi Made Easy Book 1](https://archive.org/details/PanjabiMadeEasy-Book1)
- [JS Nagra, Panjabi Made Easy Book 2](https://archive.org/details/PanjabiMadeEasy-Book2)
- [JS Nagra, Panjabi Made Easy Book 3](https://archive.org/details/PanjabiMadeEasy-Book3)
- [AQA GCSE Panjabi 8683 specification](https://www.aqa.org.uk/subjects/panjabi/gcse/panjabi-8683/specification)
- [AQA A-Level Panjabi 7682 specification](https://www.aqa.org.uk/subjects/panjabi/a-level/panjabi-7682/specification)

Doc lives at `docs/market-research.md`. Here's the summary you actually need:

## The opportunity in three numbers

- **~400–450k addressable households** globally (diaspora families willing to pay for heritage Punjabi)
- **963 GCSE + 167 A-Level** candidates per year (UK, AQA 2024) — small but stable, no digital competition
- **£2B/yr** UK private tutoring market (we're a fraction of that, but a real fraction)

## The competition (one-line verdict on each)

Reviewed every Punjabi app: Pimsleur, Mango, Ling, Mondly, Memrise, Drops, Language Curry, uTalk, HelloTalk, eLearnPunjabi, Punjabi By Choice. Plus the tutor marketplaces (Preply, italki).

**Nobody does script teaching + reading practice + exam prep + AI marking in one product.** Every existing app is either:
- Audio-only (Pimsleur)
- Generic phrase drills (Mondly, Ling, Mango)
- Tutor marketplace (Preply, italki)
- Religious/donation-funded (Punjabi By Choice)
- Government-funded but dated (eLearnPunjabi)

The gap is real and structural — Duolingo won't enter this market because it's too small for them.

## Benchmark pricing (so we know where to land)

| App | Monthly | Annual |
|---|---|---|
| Babbel | $13.95 | $99 |
| LingQ | $14.99 | $108 |
| Mango (single) | $11.99 | $80 |
| Memrise | $8.99 | $60 |
| Mondly | $9.99 | $60 |
| Pimsleur | $19.95 | $170 |
| Speak (AI tier) | $19.99 | $100 |
| Duolingo Super | $12.99 | $84 |
| Duolingo Max (AI) | $29.99 | $168 |

**Sweet spot: £8/mo annual (£80/yr) for the standard tier, £20/mo for AI-enhanced.**

## Realistic revenue scenarios

Conservative 6% conversion, blended ARPU £80/yr:

| Scenario | MAUs | Paid subs | Annual revenue |
|---|---|---|---|
| Year 1 modest | 30k | 1,200 | **£96k** |
| Year 3 plausible | 250k | 15,000 | **£1.2M** ← supports a real team |
| Year 5 optimistic | 500k | 40,000 | **£3.2M** |
| Year 5 best case (Duolingo-level penetration) | 1M | 90,000 | **£7.2M** |

For context: Duolingo gets 9% conversion at $94/yr ARPU. Our model bakes in worse-than-Duolingo on both axes and still pencils to a real business.

## Recommended monetization stack

| Tier | Price | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **Free** | £0 | Everyone — alphabet, sakhi reader (current product) |
| **akhar Pro** | £8/mo or £80/yr | Heritage learners, casual students |
| **akhar GCSE Prep** | £15/mo or £120/yr | Exam-aligned content + AI writing/translation marking |
| **(Later) A-Level** | £25/mo or £200/yr | Prescribed texts + film transcripts + essay marking |
| **(Year 2) School licensing** | £200–500/yr per school | UK supplementary/Saturday schools, ~50 known |

**The killer feature**: AQA-rubric-aligned AI marking. Saves parents £400–800/yr per child in private tutor fees. Clear ROI, easy sell.

## What to copy from others

- **Daily streak + push notifications** (Duolingo) — proven retention
- **Family Plan** (Duolingo) — perfect for heritage market (parents buying for kids)
- **7-day free trial** (everyone) — 22% trial-to-paid conversion benchmark
- **Reading + vocab tracker** (LingQ) — already our core mechanic, just need the "known words" UI
- **Listen-only mode** (Pimsleur) — commute use case, we already have the audio
- **FSRS spaced repetition** (Anki) — review UI gap to close on the existing srs_cards table

Three docs pushed to `dev`. Here are the punchlines from each so you don't have to read all 900 lines unless you want to:

## `docs/content-architecture.md` — what is this thing?

**akhar = two wings on one foundation.**

- **Foundation**: the alphabet curriculum (already built). Script-learning is the universal entry point.
- **Wing 1 — Read**: the sakhi library. Classical Punjabi, 405 stories, hover-translation, audio. For heritage/devotional readers.
- **Wing 2 — Learn**: alphabet + beginner readers + GCSE topic packs + A-Level (later). Modern Punjabi, structured curriculum, exam-aligned.

They're **different products in the same app**. Top nav after sign-in: **Read · Learn · Deck · Profile**.

**Why two wings, not one funnel**: sakhis are classical literary prose; GCSE tests modern conversational Punjabi about family/school/jobs. Folding them confuses both audiences. Keeping them separate lets a heritage parent never touch GCSE and a GCSE student never read a sakhi — yet they're paying for the same product.

**How the sakhi corpus gets dished out**: browse by Guru / book / theme / difficulty + daily pick + continue reading + bookmarks. Not the full 405 in a list — that's overwhelming. Default home = "continue + recommended."

**How the wings cross-link**: shared SRS deck, shared dictionary, "suggested sakhi for this GCSE topic" pointers, and a `word_encounters` counter that flows both ways. So vocabulary learnt in one wing makes recommendations smarter in the other.

## `docs/tiers.md` — what's free, what's paid

| Tier | Price | What you get that the tier below doesn't |
|---|---|---|
| **Free** | £0 | Alphabet + 10-sakhi taster + GCSE Theme 1 Topic 1 taster + 50-card deck. Genuinely useful (more complete than 90% of Punjabi apps) — drives signups. |
| **akhar Pro** | £8/mo or £80/yr | Full 405 sakhi library, unlimited deck, all beginner readers, **GCSE vocab + reading components** (not writing/AI marking), audio downloads, cross-device sync |
| **akhar GCSE** | £15/mo or £120/yr | AI-marked writing & translation against AQA rubrics, listening exercises, past papers, mock exams, grammar drills. **The killer tier.** Saves parents £400+/yr in tutor fees. |
| **akhar A-Level** | £25/mo or £200/yr | (Later phase) Annotated *Noori* / *Ristian de Rang* / *Savee Pattar*, film transcripts, research-topic source library, essay marking |
| **Family Plan** | £150/yr | (Later phase) 5 users on Pro features. Heritage-parent killer. |

**Gating rule**: if it costs us per-use (LLM tokens, Whisper, heavy bandwidth) → paid. If it's cached / free at scale (text reads, hover lookups, played audio) → free forever. Means the free tier is actually sustainable at any scale.

**Explicitly NOT building**: ads, hearts/lives, certificates, VR, sacred-text monetization. Avoiding Duolingo's worst mistakes.

## `docs/build-order.md` — what we build, in what order

Eight phases, each with a shippable goal:

| Phase | Goal | Estimate |
|---|---|---|
| **0** | Mobile pass, onboarding, bookmarks, akhar.io DNS, Capacitor submission. **Free tier live.** | 2 wks |
| **1** | SRS review UI, process 50+ sakhis (LLM-assisted), tagging, recommendations | 3 wks |
| **2** | Stripe, tier gating, free trial, audio downloads. **Pro tier live.** | 2 wks |
| **3** | Beginner readers + ONE GCSE topic pack proving the format end-to-end | 4 wks |
| **4** | AI marking pipeline. **GCSE tier live — the moment akhar becomes real exam prep.** | 3 wks |
| **5** | Remaining 11 GCSE topic packs + past papers + mock exams | 4 wks |
| **6** | Native mobile polish + Family Plan | 3 wks |
| **7** | A-Level tier (prescribed texts, films, research) | 6+ wks |
| **8+** | Speaking + AI tutor + B2B schools + other languages | Later |

**Total to "complete GCSE prep product"**: ~21 weeks (~5 months) from now if focused. Revenue from Pro starts at end of Phase 2 (~7 weeks in). Revenue from GCSE tier starts at end of Phase 4 (~14 weeks in).

The build order is opinionated but not rigid — when we get real users we adjust based on data, not vibes.

> AGENT

Honest opinion: **I'd push back on "limited free that forces upgrades."** Not because forcing upgrades is wrong in general — it's because it's wrong *for akhar specifically*. Let me explain, then propose a tight MVP.

## Why I'd avoid a hard paywall

Three things specific to akhar:

**1. Tiny addressable market.** We're chasing ~400k households globally, not millions. Every visitor who bounces because the free tier feels stingy is one we'll never get back — there's no SEO long tail of "Punjabi learning app" searches refilling the funnel like there is for "Spanish learning app." Generosity now compounds.

**2. Zero brand recognition.** Pimsleur and Babbel can afford hard paywalls because everyone already knows their name. We're starting cold — people need to discover akhar exists, try it, talk about it, share it with their kids' Saturday school WhatsApp group. None of that happens if the first thing they hit is a paywall.

**3. Heritage learning is emotional, not transactional.** Punjabi diaspora parents don't convert because a paywall forced them; they convert because they fell in love with watching their kid read ਘੋੜਾ for the first time. That feeling needs space to develop — usually a week or two of free use.

**The counterexample to study is Pimsleur**. Audio-only, hard paywall after free trial, 90-day money-back guarantee. They have ~$100M/yr revenue at high ARPU but their *growth* is anaemic because there's no viral loop. Duolingo at $271M/quarter with 135M MAUs is the alternative — generous free + ads + Pro upgrade. Different scale entirely.

For akhar's stage, **Duolingo's playbook fits, not Pimsleur's** — even though our ARPU target is higher than Duolingo's.

## What I would do instead

**Generous free tier for the foundational stuff** (alphabet, dictionary, a meaningful taste of the library) → **clear paid value for depth + convenience + AI**. Free should feel sustainable to someone who never pays; paid should feel like an obvious upgrade once they're hooked.

Specifically:
- Free **must** stay generous for the *script + dictionary* layer — that's the SEO and word-of-mouth engine. Gating "how do I read ਘੋੜਾ?" is suicidal for our funnel.
- Pro can comfortably gate the *library depth*, *deck depth*, *audio downloads*, *cross-device sync*. These are clearly "I'm serious now" features.
- GCSE tier should gate the things with real per-use cost (LLM marking) — those are also the things people pay tutors £30/hr to get.

## My MVP proposal

Cut everything not on this list. Ship in 6–8 weeks. Validate the core hypothesis: *"heritage Punjabi learners will pay £8/mo for a thoughtfully built reader."* Don't validate the GCSE thesis yet — that's MVP+1.

### MVP Free tier
- **Full alphabet curriculum** — already built. 58 lessons, audio, glossary.
- **5 sakhis** fully open with all features (hover, audio, save-to-deck). Reduce from my earlier "10" — that's enough to fall in love.
- **SRS deck capped at 100 cards** — enough to develop the habit, not enough to never need Pro.
- **No cross-device sync** — sign in on one device.
- **No audio downloads** — streaming only.

### MVP Pro tier — £8/mo or £80/yr
- Everything in Free
- **All 50+ processed sakhis** (target for MVP: ~50 sakhis processed via LLM-assisted pipeline)
- **Unlimited deck**
- **Cross-device sync**
- **Audio downloads (PWA offline cache)**
- **Reading goals + streak tracking**

### What's NOT in MVP (defer to post-launch)
- GCSE tier (build only after Pro has paying users)
- AI marking (the killer feature, but expensive to build right — wait for revenue to justify)
- A-Level tier
- Beginner readers (the Book 2/3 stuff — build only if MVP validates and we need more depth)
- Native iOS/Android apps (web + PWA is enough — Capacitor wrap as Phase 2)
- Family Plan (build when we have ≥1,000 Pro users)
- Topic packs / writing drills / translation drills / listening exercises

### MVP success criteria

After 90 days from launch:
- **1,000+ free signups** (proves the funnel works)
- **30+ Pro subscribers** (3% conversion at this stage = validation)
- **£2,400+ MRR** by month 3 (covers infra cost and proves willingness-to-pay)

If those hit, build GCSE tier next. If they don't, the issue isn't paywall — it's product/market fit, and we need to fix that before adding more tiers.

## The deeper principle

There's a saying in subscription businesses: **"Free isn't your marketing — it's your product."** People who would pay convert in their own time when they're ready. The free experience's job is to keep them around long enough for that moment to arrive.

A hard paywall on day one says: "Decide right now whether I'm worth £8/mo, with almost no information." A generous free tier says: "Use this for a month, build a habit, then decide." Heritage learners need the second version.

Agree on the first, would push back on the second. Let me explain.

## Cross-device free — yes, you're right

It's 2026 — multi-device sync is an expectation, not a feature. Heritage households are exactly the case: kid on iPhone, mum on iPad, dad on a laptop at work, all sharing one akhar account in spirit. Gating that creates friction with no upside (Supabase syncs automatically the moment they're signed in — costs us nothing to enable).

Withholding it would feel punitive in a way that "audio downloads" or "AI marking" don't. Make it free.

## Save-to-deck paid — I'd split the difference

You're directionally right that "save" is a strong engagement signal — but the actual learning value isn't in *saving* a word, it's in **reviewing** it with spaced repetition. The thing tutors and language schools charge for isn't the word list — it's the active recall loop.

Three reasons to make **saving free but reviewing paid**:

1. **Saving is a habit-formation hook.** A user who saves their first 10 words is dramatically more likely to keep using the app than one who doesn't. Free saves = more engaged users = better conversion later. Paywalling the save itself prevents the very behaviour that builds paying users.

2. **Heritage learners save sentimentally.** "I want to remember ਨਾਨੀ because grandma used to say it." Putting a paywall on that emotional moment is a really bad first impression. They'll bounce.

3. **The killer feature is the review loop, not the list.** Anki has unlimited free saving — it's a giant market leader despite that. What people actually pay for is the *structured review experience*: FSRS scheduling, daily review session, audio playback during review, performance analytics, custom decks grouped by theme. That's where real learning happens, and that's what justifies £8/mo.

**My counter-proposal:**

| Action | Tier |
|---|---|
| See a saved-words list (passive) | Free |
| Save words from any sakhi or lesson | Free |
| Hear audio for a saved word | Free |
| **Daily SRS review session with FSRS scheduling** | **Pro** |
| Custom decks (group by theme, sakhi, etc.) | Pro |
| Performance analytics (retention curve, weak words) | Pro |
| Audio-only review mode for commutes | Pro |
| Export deck to Anki | Pro |

So the free user gets a "vocabulary scrapbook." The paid user gets a "personal Punjabi memory system." Different products, both valuable, clear upgrade reason.

## Updated MVP

### MVP Free
- Full alphabet curriculum (58 lessons)
- 5 sakhis fully open (hover, audio, save-to-deck)
- **Cross-device sync** ✅
- **Save unlimited words to a personal list** ✅
- Streaming audio
- Daily streak tracking

### MVP Pro — £8/mo or £80/yr
- All ~50 processed sakhis
- **Daily SRS review session** (the active learning loop)
- Custom decks
- Audio downloads (PWA offline cache)
- Deck analytics

### Out of MVP
- GCSE / A-Level tiers
- AI marking
- Beginner readers
- Native apps
- Family Plan
- Topic packs / writing / translation / listening

## Alternative if you genuinely want save = paid

If you feel strongly that the "moment of intent" is the right conversion point (Headspace/Calm-style), we could go more aggressive:

### Alternative MVP Free
- Alphabet curriculum
- 5 sakhis with hover + audio (no save)
- No deck at all

### Alternative MVP Pro
- Everything above + save-to-deck + SRS review + 50 sakhis + offline

This is simpler to message ("Pro = remember what you learn") and might convert faster but loses the habit-formation hook. Worth A/B testing once we have volume, not at launch.

## My honest recommendation

Go with the **split** (save free, review paid). It maps cleanly to how Anki vs Memrise vs LingQ differentiate. The save-to-deck button stays as a moment of delight on the free tier; the SRS review at `/deck` is gated with a clear "Start your daily review" upsell.

> AGENT

Fair challenge. Let me reconsider — and I think there's a clean split that respects both instincts.

## You're right that 58 lessons is a lot

The whole alphabet curriculum took 5 days of work and is genuinely high quality. Giving it away entirely is the Duolingo move, which made sense when I was thinking purely about SEO. But you're correct that for a £8/mo product with a small market, we need *something* to gate even within the script section.

## The clean split: "recognise" free, "read" paid

The 58 lessons fall into two pedagogical halves:

**Half 1 — "I can identify Punjabi letters" (~39 lessons)**
- Pronunciation primer
- 35 paintee akhar (the letters themselves)
- 5 nukta letters
- Reference chart
- Recognition exercise

After this, a learner can *look at any Punjabi text and name each letter*. They can't read it yet — because they don't know vowel signs.

**Half 2 — "I can actually read Punjabi" (~19 lessons)**
- Mukta (inherent 'a')
- 9 matras (Kannā ā, Sihārī i, Bihārī ī, Aunkaṛ u, Dulæṅkaṛ ū, Lāvāṅ e, Dulāvāṅ æ, Hōṛā o, Kanɔṛā ɔ)
- 5 diacritic concepts (Adhikk, Tippī, Bindī, Pairī Rārā, Tone-H)
- 4 story lessons (Kulbir's day, Flying birds, Clever crow, Vaisakhi)
- Glossary

This is the half that makes you a reader. **Gate it behind Pro.**

## Why this split is honest, not arbitrary

1. **It maps to what people Google.** "Punjabi alphabet" → free (we own that SEO term). "Punjabi vowel signs" / "how to read Gurmukhi" → paid. Both terms exist, both have intent, but the second one has stronger conversion intent.

2. **It mirrors how the original book is sold.** JS Nagra's Panjabi Made Easy Book 1 is £8 at the SikhBookClub. We're giving away the equivalent of pages 5–24 (the letter pages) free and gating pages 25–48 (matras + diacritics + stories). Honest framing: "the first half of the book is free."

3. **Heritage learners who already read some Punjabi don't need the matra lessons** — they're reading by recognition already. They land on free, browse the alphabet refresher, then go straight to the 5 free sakhis. Their entry point is reading, not the curriculum.

4. **Beginners who are learning from scratch hit a clear wall**: "you've learned the letters; to actually read words, unlock vowel signs with Pro." This is the cleanest possible conversion moment — they're invested, they understand the gap, they see the path forward.

5. **One teaser matra free.** Unlock Kannā (the long ā) free so beginners *understand what a matra is and how it works* before they're asked to pay. Then the other 8 + diacritics are gated. Babbel and Memrise both do this — first lesson of each course free, rest paywalled.

## Updated MVP free tier

- Pronunciation primer
- 35 letters + nukta + chart + recognition exercise
- **1 matra lesson** (Kannā, the long ā) — the teaser
- 5 sakhis fully open (hover, audio, save-to-deck)
- **Cross-device sync** ✅ (from last round)
- **Save unlimited words to personal list** ✅ (from last round)
- Streaming audio
- Daily streak

## Updated MVP Pro — £8/mo or £80/yr

- All 50+ processed sakhis
- **Remaining 8 matras + diacritics + stories + glossary** (the rest of the alphabet curriculum)
- Daily SRS review session
- Custom decks
- Audio downloads (PWA offline cache)
- Deck analytics

## What about the 5 free sakhis if matras are paid?

Important consequence: someone who uses *only* the free tier (just letters, no matras) can't actually read those 5 sakhis from scratch — they don't know the vowel signs. Two ways to handle:

**A. Generous hover lookup as the bridge.** The hover popover already shows romanization + meaning for every word. So a learner who knows letters but not matras can still navigate a sakhi by hover-translating each word. They don't fully decode it, but they understand it. This is genuinely useful and might be enough for many heritage learners.

**B. Pick the 5 free sakhis carefully.** We curate 5 that are short, narratively rich, and have the most consistent vocabulary — so the hover-only experience feels rewarding even without full reading skill. The other 400+ are gated.

I'd do both. The 5 freebies are the "fall in love with reading even before you can read" moment.

## Bottom line

You were right. **Gate the second half of the alphabet behind Pro.** Free becomes "letters + a taste of vowel signs + 5 sakhis with hover help" — a real product, but with an obvious upgrade path to "actually read Punjabi."

> AGENT

Two genuinely important questions. Let me answer each properly.

## How we gather metrics

### Recommended stack

| Tool | What it does | Cost | Why this one |
|---|---|---|---|
| **PostHog** (cloud free tier) | Product analytics — events, funnels, retention cohorts, session replay | Free up to 1M events/mo | Open-source, self-hostable if we ever care, excellent free tier, GDPR-friendly defaults |
| **Sentry** | Errors + performance monitoring | Free up to 5k errors/mo | Industry standard, catches the bugs we don't catch |
| **Stripe** | Revenue, MRR, churn, refunds | Free (built-in dashboard) | Already in the plan |
| **Supabase logs** | DB query slowness, auth errors | Free | Already in the plan |
| **First-party events table** in Supabase | The 10–20 business-critical events we always want to query ourselves | Pennies | Lets us write SQL against our own data without depending on a third-party |

**Don't add Google Analytics.** GA4 is bloated, tracks more than we need, makes us look like every other surveillance-ad-tech site, and we'd be hated by privacy-conscious heritage learners (who, anecdotally, are getting more wary every year).

### The events that actually matter

Don't track everything. ~20 events tell you everything:

**Acquisition**
- `page_viewed` (with referrer + path)
- `signup_started` / `signup_completed` (with source)

**Activation** — *did they "get it" in first session?*
- `first_alphabet_lesson_completed`
- `first_sakhi_opened`
- `first_word_saved`
- `first_audio_played`

**Engagement** — *do they come back?*
- `lesson_completed` (which lesson, time spent)
- `sakhi_completed` (which sakhi)
- `srs_review_session_finished` (cards reviewed, accuracy)
- `audio_played` (rough engagement proxy)
- `streak_milestone_hit` (7-day, 30-day, etc.)

**Monetization**
- `paywall_seen` (which paywall — alphabet matra vs sakhi 6 vs deck review)
- `pricing_page_viewed`
- `checkout_started` / `checkout_completed`
- `trial_started` / `trial_converted` / `trial_canceled`
- `subscription_canceled` (with optional reason)

**Drop-off** — *where do they leave?*
- `session_ended` (auto)
- `alphabet_abandoned_at_lesson_N`
- `sakhi_abandoned_at_paragraph_N`

That's the whole list. PostHog handles all of it via a single `posthog.capture()` call.

### What we actually do with the data

The metrics question only matters if we *use* the data. Realistic cadence:

- **Weekly review** (15 min, every Monday): funnel from signup → first sakhi → save → review → paywall → checkout. Look for the biggest drop-off; that's next week's priority.
- **Monthly cohort review**: are day-30 retention numbers improving as we ship features?
- **Per-feature ship**: define one metric before shipping. "If this AI-marking feature is working, the percentage of GCSE-tier trial users who convert should rise from X% to Y%." If it doesn't, kill the feature.

The temptation is to track everything and analyse none of it. Resist that.

---

## How we don't appear scammy

This is genuinely more important than the metrics question, and the things below are decisions we should commit to in writing now — not figure out later.

### Where scammy comes from

Apps feel scammy because of specific dark patterns:

1. **"Free trial" that requires a credit card with hidden auto-renewal**
2. **"Cancellation" flows that take 7 clicks with manipulative copy** ("Are you SURE? You'll lose your streak!")
3. **Fake urgency** ("50% off — 48 hours only!" when the price is always that)
4. **Aggressive paywalls** popping up every 30 seconds mid-lesson
5. **Selling user data** to third parties
6. **Email spam** after signup
7. **Push notification harassment** ("You've abandoned your streak! Come back!")
8. **Bait-and-switch** (free becomes paid after content addiction)
9. **Hidden costs / surprise charges**

### Concrete commitments akhar makes

**On free trials and pricing:**
- **Free tier without sign-up wall** — you can try the alphabet curriculum first 3 lessons without an account. Sign-up is for saving progress, not for evaluation.
- **Free trial without credit card** when possible. Industry says we lose 22% conversion vs requiring card; we accept that for trust.
- **One pricing page, all tiers visible, monthly and annual side by side.** No "contact us for pricing." No bundles requiring 3-year commitments.
- **Annual plans clearly marked as annual** with renewal date shown at every billing event.
- **Email 7 days before renewal** for annual plans so nobody is surprised.
- **No fake-urgency anywhere.** No "limited time offers" unless they're actually limited time. No countdown timers.

**On cancellation:**
- **One-click cancel from account settings.** No "are you sure?" loops with retention copy.
- **Refunds within 14 days for any reason.** UK law requires this for digital products bought online (Consumer Contracts Regulations 2013). Be visible about it.
- **Data export on cancel** — they can take their saved words + reading history with them in JSON or CSV.

**On notifications:**
- **All notifications opt-in.** Default OFF after signup; user chooses what they want.
- **Configurable times.** Heritage parents reading at 8pm not 8am.
- **Max one email per week** as marketing. Transactional emails (receipts, password resets) excepted.
- **No guilt-trip notifications.** No "You've abandoned your streak!" — just "Your daily review is ready when you are."

**On privacy:**
- **GDPR-compliant** by default — cookie consent that actually works, data deletion on request honoured within 30 days.
- **No selling data to third parties.** Ever. State it prominently.
- **No third-party trackers** beyond PostHog (privacy-friendly) and Sentry (error tracking only). No Facebook Pixel, no Google Ads, no TikTok.
- **Cookie banner** with a real "Reject all" button (not buried).

**On trust signals:**
- **Real "About" page** with the founders' names and faces. Sumeet Saini, who built this because his own kids deserved better. Honesty is the moat.
- **Public changelog** showing what we shipped each week.
- **Public roadmap** so users can see what's coming and vote.
- **Open-source core** consideration — the alphabet curriculum data + the sakhi reader UI could plausibly be open source. Builds trust + lets the community contribute corrections.
- **Real customer support** — a `hello@akhar.io` that a human replies to within 24 hours.
- **A "Sikh community statement"** addressing the elephant in the room: "We are not a religious organisation. We're not affiliated with any Gurdwara, school, or Sikh institution. We built this because the diaspora needs better tools. We treat the sakhi content with respect: we don't paywall reading the texts themselves — we charge for engineering features (cross-device, audio downloads, SRS, AI marking). The texts are our shared cultural inheritance."

### The specific worry for a Punjabi/Sikh learning product

The diaspora community is tight-knit and reputation spreads fast — in Gurdwara WhatsApp groups, in supplementary-school staff rooms, on Sikh subreddits. **One scammy interaction will sink us within weeks.** One genuinely good interaction will get us recommended for years.

This means the right strategy is **be visibly more ethical than we need to be**, not "ethical enough." The cost of going slow and trustworthy is paid in conversion percentages. The cost of going fast and scammy is paid in total addressable market we can never reach.

### What's NOT scammy

To be clear, none of these are scammy and we shouldn't worry about them:

- **Charging £8/mo** for a quality product is not scammy. It's fair value.
- **Paywalling content behind a tier** is not scammy if the tier is clearly priced and the gate is visible upfront.
- **Marketing the product** via email/social is not scammy if frequency is reasonable.
- **Asking for reviews** in-app after a positive moment is not scammy.
- **A/B testing** copy or pricing is not scammy (but be ethical: don't test dark patterns).
- **Showing "X people signed up this week"** is not scammy if it's true.

> AGENT

Yes, all of it works on mobile — but there are some specific mobile considerations worth knowing upfront.

## Metrics: clean carryover

**PostHog** has a JavaScript SDK that runs natively inside Capacitor's WebView. Since our Capacitor app is essentially the web app wrapped in a native shell, **the same `posthog.capture()` calls fire on web and mobile** — no separate tracking code, no two-code-paths-to-maintain problem.

Better still: because users sign in with the same Supabase Auth on web and mobile, **events from both surfaces attribute to the same user.** A user who reads sakhi 1 on mobile and saves a word on web shows up in PostHog as one journey. This is the cleanest possible setup.

**Sentry** works the same way — JS SDK in WebView covers everything we care about. There's an additional Capacitor-native Sentry plugin for catching crashes that happen at the native shell level (rare but worth having for the store submission).

**Mobile-specific events** to add on top of the web event list:
- `app_installed` (first launch detection)
- `push_notification_permission_granted`
- `push_notification_opened` (tracks re-engagement)
- `offline_mode_used` (did they read while no internet?)
- `native_share_used` (system share sheet — "share this sakhi to WhatsApp")
- `app_store_review_prompted` / `_completed`

PostHog handles all these via the same JS SDK calls.

## Stripe + the 30% problem

Here's the wrinkle nobody warns you about: **Apple and Google take 30% of any digital subscription purchased through their in-app billing systems.** It drops to 15% in year 2, or 15% from day one if you're under $1M/yr revenue, but it's still significant compared to Stripe's ~3%.

Three ways to handle it:

### Option A: Web-only purchases (recommended for launch)

- Mobile app: free tier fully usable, sign-in works
- "Upgrade to Pro" button → opens system browser → `akhar.io/pricing` → Stripe Checkout → user pays via Stripe → returns to app, now logged in as Pro
- **Cost: 3% to Stripe, 0% to Apple/Google**
- **Cost: ~10-15% conversion friction** (some users abandon at the browser handoff)

This is what Spotify, Netflix, YouTube, and the Kindle app all do. Apple's 2026 rules (post-*Epic v Apple* ruling) explicitly allow linking out to external purchase pages with disclosure copy. We're allowed.

### Option B: Native In-App Purchase (IAP)

- Pro/GCSE subs purchasable inside the mobile app via Apple/Google billing
- Smoother UX, much higher mobile conversion
- **Cost: 30% (year 1) / 15% (year 2+) to Apple/Google**
- Need to keep Stripe for web purchases simultaneously
- Sub state has to be synced between Stripe + RevenueCat (or equivalent) + Supabase — annoying but standard

### Option C: Hybrid (probably year-2 move)

- Free tier: full access on web + mobile
- Pro purchase: both options available
  - Default CTA → web checkout (cheaper for us)
  - Secondary "Subscribe in App" button → IAP (smoother for them)

### My recommendation

**Launch with Option A (web-only).** Reasons:
- Heritage parents researching a £8/mo subscription will use a laptop anyway, not a phone
- The 30% Apple tax on a £8/mo sub = £2.88 lost per user per month. At our small scale that compounds fast
- Avoids the engineering complexity of RevenueCat/IAP wiring at MVP stage
- Can add IAP later when we have data showing how many mobile users would have converted with a native flow

Switch to Option C in year 2 if metrics show mobile users dropping off at the browser handoff.

## Trust principles: identical, with mobile-specific additions

All the not-scammy commitments carry over. **Plus** the app stores impose their own compliance requirements:

### iOS App Store

- **Privacy nutrition labels** (mandatory) — explicit declaration of what data you collect, linked to your privacy policy. PostHog events fall under "Usage Data" and "Diagnostics."
- **Sign in with Apple** (required) if you offer any other auth method. We use magic links → we must add Sign in with Apple. Apple gives users an option to hide their real email; we honour the relay.
- **App Tracking Transparency (ATT)** if we ever track users across other apps/websites. PostHog by default doesn't do this — we're fine.
- **No bait-and-switch on subscriptions** — pricing must be clear in the app, charges must match what's displayed, renewals must be honoured per Apple's rules.

### Google Play Store

- **Data safety section** — equivalent to Apple's privacy labels. Same declarations.
- **Refund policy** — clearly disclosed.
- **No deceptive ads** — same standard as everywhere.

### What this means in practice

Three additions to the trust commitments doc, just for mobile:

1. **Add Sign in with Apple** alongside email magic links, before iOS submission
2. **Privacy nutrition labels match privacy policy match PostHog reality** — these three documents stay in sync, audited at every release
3. **Push notifications strictly opt-in** with system prompt — never request permission until the user has already decided they want them (after they enable "daily review reminder" in settings, for example, not at first launch)

## Updated takeaway

Yes, our analytics + trust stack works on mobile with minimal extra work. The one real decision is **payment routing**: web-only Stripe (cheap, lower mobile conversion) vs native IAP (expensive, higher conversion). Launch with web-only, layer IAP in when data justifies it.

The one thing to **do early** that I haven't called out: **submit to App Store + Play Store sooner than you think**. Approval can take 1-2 weeks the first time; subsequent updates are usually <24h. If we want the apps live on launch day, we need to start the submission cycle ~3 weeks before. Capacitor wrap + minimal native features (push notifications, native share, Sign in with Apple) is enough for first approval.

## Why magic links are wrong for mobile

You're right to flag this. Magic links have a known mobile UX failure mode:

1. User taps "Sign in" in the mobile app
2. Types email, taps Send
3. **Switches to their email app** (this is the killer step)
4. Finds the email, taps the link
5. The link opens **either** the akhar mobile app via deep linking (good, IF correctly configured) **or** Safari/Chrome (bad — confused user is now in a browser, not the app, has no idea where their "logged in" session went)
6. Industry data on this: ~30–40% drop-off between "magic link sent" and "user actually logged in" on mobile

Plus: **App Store requires Sign in with Apple** as soon as we offer any other auth method, so we're adding that regardless.

## My recommended replacement

Three options, all natively supported by Supabase Auth, no passwords anywhere:

| Method | UX | Use case |
|---|---|---|
| **Sign in with Apple** | 1 tap, Face ID confirms, done | Default on iOS. Required by App Store anyway. |
| **Sign in with Google** | 1 tap, Google sheet confirms, done | Default on Android. Almost every heritage user has a Google account. |
| **Email + 6-digit OTP code** | Type email → get code in email → type code in app | Fallback for users who don't want social sign-in. No email-to-app handoff because the code is typed in the app. |

No passwords. No magic links. All three work identically on web and mobile.

**Order on the sign-in screen** (matters for conversion):
- Big "Continue with Apple" button (on iOS / macOS users this is one tap, biggest delight)
- Big "Continue with Google" button (works everywhere)
- Smaller "Use email instead →" text link → reveals email + OTP flow
- No "create account" vs "sign in" split — same flow for both (Supabase auto-creates on first OTP/social)

This is the same auth pattern Linear, Vercel, Cal.com, and Notion all converged on. It's the modern default.

## The migration

Existing magic-link users: they're stored in Supabase Auth by email. When they sign in with Apple/Google using the same email address, Supabase auto-links the accounts. Their existing data carries over. No data migration needed.

The only change is the UI on `/login` (replace email form with the three buttons) and the `signInWithOtp` server action (replace with `signInWithIdToken` for social + `signInWithOtp` with `type: 'email'` for OTP code instead of magic link).

> AGENT

That's a known-painful flow — Apple's enrollment system is notoriously brittle. The 403 on `submitIndividualEnrollment` plus the DNS resolution failure suggest something in your network or browser is interfering.

## Most likely causes (in order of likelihood)

### 1. iCloud Private Relay / VPN / browser extension blocking
The `ERR_NAME_NOT_RESOLVED` is the telltale — something is intercepting Apple's DNS requests. Fix:
- **Turn off iCloud Private Relay** (Settings → Apple ID → iCloud → Private Relay → off)
- **Disable any VPN** (NordVPN, Cloudflare WARP, etc.)
- **Disable browser ad-blockers** (uBlock, Privacy Badger, Brave Shields)
- **Use Safari, not Chrome** — Apple's enrollment flow is most reliable in their own browser

### 2. 2FA not enabled on the Apple ID
Apple requires two-factor authentication on the Apple ID used for Developer Program enrollment. Without it, the submission silently 403s.

Check: **System Settings → Apple ID → Sign-In & Security → Two-Factor Authentication** — must be **On**. If not, turn it on, sign out and back in, then retry enrollment.

### 3. Apple ID country/region mismatch with payment card
If your Apple ID region is set to one country and your card billing address is another, enrollment fails. Verify:
- Apple ID region: appleid.apple.com → Personal Information → Country/Region
- Card billing address country matches

### 4. If you picked "Organization" — D-U-N-S Number missing
Organization enrollment requires a D-U-N-S Number for Reliq Digital Ltd. If you don't have one, Apple's form 403s.

Fast workaround: **switch to Individual enrollment for now.** No D-U-N-S needed, approval is much faster (often same-day), and you can upgrade to Organization later inside the developer portal. The apps + revenue still go through correctly — the only difference is your name appears as the seller on the App Store instead of the company.

For akhar at this stage, **Individual is fine**. Switch to Organization once Reliq Digital is properly trading.

## My recommended retry sequence

1. Open **Safari** on macOS
2. Turn off iCloud Private Relay temporarily
3. Disable any VPN / browser extensions
4. Verify 2FA is on your Apple ID
5. Go to **https://developer.apple.com/programs/enroll/**
6. Pick **Individual** (not Organization)
7. Sign in with your Apple ID
8. Walk through the form, pay $99
9. Should submit successfully

If it 403s again from step 8: try from your phone hotspot instead of home WiFi (some ISPs / routers block parts of Apple's API).

## If it's STILL broken after all that

Call **Apple Developer Program Support: 0800 048 0408 (UK)** — they can manually push the enrollment through. Mention "403 on submitIndividualEnrollment endpoint" and they'll know exactly what's wrong. Phone wait is usually 5-15 min.

## What's now live

### 1. Audio fix
The 4 quiet words in sakhi 1 (ਸਨ, ਤੋਂ, ਕੋਈ, ਅਤੇ) all re-synthesized with the doubled-input pattern. They went from 2-3KB to 4-8KB and should be properly audible now.

The MIN_HEALTHY_BYTES threshold in the live synth pipeline is now 3000B (was 2000B), so any new word that comes back smaller will auto-retry with the doubled-input fallback.

If you hear any others that are still quiet, tell me the exact words and I'll add them to a force-resynth list. Or re-run `tsx scripts/audit-audio-quality.ts --sakhi 1 --min-bytes 3500` (bump higher) to catch more.

### 2. Full-sakhi player
At the top of every sakhi page there's now a sticky bar with a play button + "Reading sentence N of M" indicator + Restart. Tap play and it walks through every sentence sequentially with a 400ms pause between each.

Pause/resume picks up at the current sentence.

### 3. Word-by-word highlighter
While each sentence plays, the current word gets a darker amber highlight. The whole playing sentence also gets a subtle background tint.

**Honest limitation**: word timings are *estimated* from character proportions because Chirp 3 HD doesn't expose word-boundary data (only Wavenet does, via SSML marks). The highlight is usually within 100-300ms of the actual word — reads as "follows along" but won't be frame-perfect. If we want pixel-perfect later, we'd need to either:
- Generate alternate Wavenet audio just for timing (extra synth cost, only for premium maybe)
- Use a forced-aligner (heavy infra, doesn't support Punjabi well)

For MVP the approximation is fine. **Test it on the preview** and tell me if the visual sync feels off.

Let's actually think about both. They're connected to a bigger question — what is the akhar reader *for*?

## Point 1: hiding unprocessed sakhis

Quick answer: **yes, do this immediately.** Right now we have:
- 1 sakhi fully verified (sakhi 1, hand-cleaned PA + EN, all words have audio + dictionary entries)
- 404 sakhis where the body text is raw-ish OCR with some auto-translation
- A library index that promises all 405

Anyone clicking a random unprocessed one will see: OCR-quality Punjabi text + iffy English + hover-lookups that may trigger expensive Punjabipedia + nano-gpt calls + sometimes return nothing useful. **First impressions are catastrophic.** Better to show 5 good ones than 405 broken ones.

Implementation is straightforward: add a `processed` flag on the sakhis table, filter all index queries to `processed = true`. The hidden ones still live in the DB so we can process them incrementally and they become visible as they're ready. Index pages can show "5 sakhis available · 400 being added" to set expectations without overpromising.

**The hard problem this exposes**: at our current rate (a few hours per sakhi to fully verify), we'll process maybe ~50 sakhis before MVP launch. That's enough to feel like a real library but it's only ~12% of the corpus. The pipeline upgrade (LLM-assisted cleanup) is critical because manual processing alone won't get us much further than that.

## Point 2: archaic vs modern Punjabi — this is bigger than UX polish

You're hitting something fundamental here. Bhai Vir Singh wrote 1900-1950. The diction is genuinely hard:
- `ਨਾਉਂ` instead of modern `ਨਾਮ` (name)
- `ਖੱਤ੍ਰੀ` instead of `ਖੱਤਰੀ` (Khatri caste)
- Sanskrit-origin vocabulary
- Persian loan-words still common
- Literary syntax (passive constructions, archaic conjugations)

For a heritage learner who can speak conversational Punjabi but never learnt to read, opening sakhi 1 and seeing literary 1920s diction is... an honest disappointment. They came to akhar to reconnect with the language they grew up hearing, and what we're showing them is a 100-year-old textbook style they don't recognize.

This was actually the whole reason for the **two-wings architecture** I sketched in `content-architecture.md`:

- **Read** wing (the sakhi library) — classical Punjabi literature. Position it as "Read the classics" rather than "Read Punjabi." Think Penguin Classics, not Duolingo.
- **Learn** wing — modern Punjabi via the Book 2/3-style content we author ourselves. Modern conversational stuff: family routines, going to a shop, weekend plans, GCSE-aligned vocab.

If we frame sakhis honestly as "classical literature for confident readers" and build modern content alongside, we serve both audiences without lying to either. Heritage learners who want easy modern reads go to Learn → Beginner Readers. Heritage learners who want to read the sakhis their dadi-ji used to tell go to Read → Library.

### Options to make sakhis themselves easier without changing what they ARE

A few practical things within the sakhi reader:

**A. Era badges on every sakhi listing.** "Classical · Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s". Sets expectations before they click. Honest framing reduces the disappointment vs an unmarked "Punjabi" sakhi.

**B. Difficulty stars (1-5)** computed from average word length, archaic-form frequency, sentence length. So a learner can pick "easy classical" first.

**C. Archaic-form annotations in hover.** When the user hovers ਨਾਉਂ, the popover already shows the lemma (ਨਾਮ). We could surface that more prominently: "ਨਾਉਂ (modern: ਨਾਮ)". Small change, big trust signal — "akhar knows this is archaic and explains it."

**D. A "modern voice" toggle.** Optional sidebar showing a contemporary Punjabi paraphrase next to the original. Tons of work to commission, defer for later. Maybe never — it conflicts with archival purpose.

**E. Curate the order.** Of the 405 sakhis, some are more accessible than others. Hand-picking which ones to show first means a beginner's first 5 sakhis are the easiest. Not random; chosen.

### What I'd actually do in the MVP

Combine the cheap version of A, B, C, and E:

1. Add `register` (classical/modern) and `difficulty_score` (1-5) to the sakhi table — populated manually as part of the verification pass
2. Surface both as small badges on the library index
3. For now, ALL processed sakhis are "Classical · Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s · difficulty 3-4" — but the badges are there so future modern content slots into the same UI without a redesign
4. In the popover, when the morpheme's lemma differs from the surface form, show "modern form: X" inline rather than buried in a note field
5. Curate the 5 free sakhis specifically as the most accessible ones — short, narrative, recurring vocabulary

This doesn't *solve* the archaic problem (the texts are what they are) but it acknowledges it and helps users navigate. Long-term answer is the Learn track's modern content, which we're not building in MVP but is the next major content investment.

### The honest framing for marketing

We should be upfront about this on the landing page. Something like:

> akhar is two things in one.
>
> The **library** is classical Punjabi literature — the sakhis of the Gurus, Bhai Vir Singh's writings, the texts our grandparents read. Beautiful, formal, demanding. Read with us.
>
> The **course** (coming soon) is modern conversational Punjabi — for learners and GCSE candidates. Simple, practical, real.

This avoids the "I came for spoken Punjabi and got 1920s prose" disappointment because we set expectations honestly upfront.

> AGENT

Your grandma's reaction is actually really important data — keep it in mind.

Here's what I think:

## The grandma test tells us something

If a native Punjabi speaker is reading the classical sakhis and *learning* — finding it cool because she's encountering vocabulary she didn't know — that's not a UX problem to solve. That's the **product working as intended**. The classical register is doing real pedagogical work even for fluent speakers. That's a genuine moat, not a bug.

Compare what we'd lose by simplifying: this version of akhar can be the only place online where a Sikh kid in Slough reads the same words their great-grandfather read in 1920 — and where their grandma also encounters words she hasn't seen in 50 years. That's a piece of cultural transmission no Duolingo-style product can ever offer. The "challenging" part is the value.

## Why I'd argue against writing our own easy/medium/hard versions

Three real problems with the graded-reader-of-sakhis approach:

**1. The authority problem.** A Bhai Vir Singh sakhi about Guru Nanak isn't a random folk tale — it's a literary work by a recognized author treating sacred biography. Rewriting it as "ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਇੱਕ ਚੰਗੇ ਮੁੰਡੇ ਸਨ। ਉਹਨਾਂ ਨੂੰ ਪਾਣੀ ਪਸੰਦ ਸੀ।" *(Nanak ji was a good boy. He liked water.)* invites a real "who said you could?" backlash from the community. Even well-intentioned rewrites of sacred narratives have a way of looking presumptuous. Imagine a startup writing "easy versions" of Bible parables and selling them — same dynamics.

**2. The art problem.** Bhai Vir Singh's prose is literature. Our "easy version" would be the workbook summary. We'd be replacing art with paraphrase. That's a quality downgrade, not a quality upgrade. The grandma-test only works because she's encountering *the actual writing*, not someone's simplification of it.

**3. The brand-split problem.** Right now akhar means one thing: a careful archive of classical Punjabi with modern tools wrapped around it. If half the library is "real sakhis" and half is "akhar's simplified retellings," users have to mentally track which is which. The brand stops meaning a single thing.

Also a practical one: writing 3 versions of 50-405 sakhis is *years* of content production, and most of it would be us paying someone (or generating with LLM) to produce sub-Bhai-Vir-Singh prose. That's a content-arms-race we can't win.

## What I'd do instead — same problem, different solution

The beginner-can't-read-sakhi-1 problem is real. But the answer is **scaffold around the original, don't replace it**:

**Pre-reading aids per sakhi:**
- **Plot summary in modern Punjabi** (5-10 lines): "ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਦਾ ਜਨਮ ਤਲਵੰਡੀ ਵਿੱਚ ਹੋਇਆ ਸੀ। ਉਹਨਾਂ ਦੇ ਪਿਤਾ ਜੀ ਮੇਹਤਾ ਕਾਲੂ ਸਨ..." This is a SUMMARY, not a rewrite. It says "here's what happens in this sakhi." Different category — like reading a Wikipedia plot summary before tackling a novel.
- **Vocab preview**: 8-10 key archaic words from the sakhi shown upfront so they're not blocking comprehension. "Before you read, learn these words."
- **Audio playback** (we just built it) — reading along helps comprehension enormously
- **Archaic→modern annotations** in the popover — `ਨਾਉਂ → modern: ਨਾਮ`. We already do some of this; could surface it more prominently

**Honest reader UI:**
- Era badges: "Classical · Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s"
- Difficulty stars
- "Recommended for beginners" tag on the 5 most accessible ones

**Separate "modern reading" content under the Learn wing — NOT in the sakhi library:**
- The Book 2/3-style readers we already discussed
- Original modern Punjabi narratives by us (or commissioned writers)
- Topics like family routine, school day, weekend plans — *deliberately not religious*, so there's no authority question
- This is where total beginners get easy modern content
- Clearly framed as **language learning content**, not as sakhis

This way:
- Sakhi library stays a respected classical archive (your grandma's experience preserved + cultural integrity maintained)
- Learn track has clean modern content for beginners (their easy reads exist, just not pretending to be sakhi material)
- Two audiences served, brand stays sharp on both sides

## The one place rewriting CAN work — much later

If we ever do want sakhi-style content in modern Punjabi, the right move is **commission published Sikh authors who write Punjabi-language kids' books** to write new stories in the spirit of the sakhis, in modern register, with clear authorial credit. Not "akhar's simplified Sakhi 1" but "ten new sakhis written by [respected author] for young readers, in modern Punjabi." This is artistically legitimate. People will buy it. It doesn't try to replace Bhai Vir Singh — it sits alongside him.

But this is a Year 2 content investment, not an MVP feature.

## Concrete recommendation

For now:
1. **Don't write easy/medium/hard sakhi versions.** Keep the classical originals intact.
2. **Add the scaffolds**: per-sakhi plot summaries + vocab previews + era/difficulty badges + the modern-form annotations in the popover.
3. **Keep Beginner Readers in the Learn track** as a separate future feature — modern content, no sakhi pretense, no authority issues.
4. **Position carefully**: akhar reads classical Punjabi *brilliantly*. Learning resources for absolute beginners are coming. Don't conflate.

This honours your grandma's experience, avoids the community-authority landmine, doesn't dilute the brand, and is dramatically less content-production work than writing 200+ retellings.

Does that resonate, or are you still drawn to the simplified-versions idea? I can argue for the other side too if you want to pressure-test it.

You're right and I overargued the first time. Let me redo this honestly.

## What I got wrong

I conflated two things. There's a real difference between:

1. **"Easy version of Bhai Vir Singh's sakhi"** — taking his 1920s prose and dumbing it down. This is what I was arguing against, and I still think it's wrong.

2. **"akhar's own modern retelling of Guru Nanak's birth"** — an original work about the same well-known events, written in modern Punjabi. This is what you're actually proposing. And it's totally legitimate.

The thing is, **there's a 200-year tradition of writing new sakhi retellings**. Every generation has produced new kids' books about Guru Nanak's life — Bhai Vir Singh's "Gur Balam Sakhian" itself was a re-telling for his era. Punjabi children's publishers like Singh Brothers and many UK Gurdwaras publish their own modern sakhi books constantly. Nobody objects because nobody's trying to replace the originals — they're adding to the tradition.

So the authority problem I raised is largely an *artefact of framing*. If we frame it as "akhar's simplified Bhai Vir Singh," it looks presumptuous. If we frame it as "akhar Stories: original modern retellings of the same events that Bhai Vir Singh and many others have told," we're a contributor to a healthy tradition, not a replacer.

## What I'd build

**akhar Stories** — a small, curated, modern-Punjabi library of the most important sakhis. **Not** a translation or simplification of anyone else's work. New original tellings of the same source events.

Two libraries side-by-side:

| Library | What | Who's it for |
|---|---|---|
| **The Archive** | Bhai Vir Singh, Bhai Kahn Singh Nabha, and the published classical sakhi authors. Texts as written. | Confident readers, grandmas, anyone wanting the literary register |
| **akhar Stories** | Our own modern retellings of ~20-30 core sakhi events (Guru Nanak's birth, Bhai Lalo & Malik Bhago, the journey to Mecca, Mata Khivi's langar, the founding of Khalsa, etc.) | Beginners, kids, heritage learners getting started |

Both clearly labelled with provenance. Same hover-translation + audio infrastructure. Cross-linked: at the end of an akhar Stories piece, "Read the classical Bhai Vir Singh version of this sakhi →".

This is honest, traditional, and useful. It also opens the door for **commissioning respected Punjabi authors** later to write more — that's how the Sikh publishing tradition has worked for a century.

## The right scope for MVP

Not 405. Not even 50. **The 20-30 core sakhi events every Sikh family knows.** I can list them out — these are the ones that appear in basically every Sikh children's book:

- Guru Nanak's birth at Talwandi
- Childhood: the cobra shade, the snake-and-tree miracle
- Failed business: ਸੱਚਾ ਸੌਦਾ (the true bargain, feeding sadhus)
- Bhai Lalo & Malik Bhago (the honest bread vs the rich man's feast)
- Visit to Mecca (turning the feet)
- Encounter with Sajjan Thug
- Wāhi-Guru emerging
- Guru Angad receives the gaddi
- Mata Khivi & langar
- Bibi Bhani's devotion
- The martyrdom of Guru Arjan
- Guru Hargobind's transformation: miri-piri
- Bhai Gurdas's writings
- Guru Tegh Bahadur's sacrifice for Kashmiri pandits
- Guru Gobind Singh: childhood, swords, the formation of the Khalsa at Anandpur (Vaisakhi 1699)
- Chamkaur, Sirhind, the Chaar Sahibzaade
- Baba Deep Singh's martyrdom
- ...

20-30 of these is the canon. They overlap heavily with what's already in the classical corpus we have, so we'd be telling **the same stories** the archive does — just in modern accessible Punjabi.

## Production realism

What this actually costs:

- **20-30 stories × ~500 words each in modern Punjabi** = ~12-15k words of original content
- **Workflow**: Claude/GPT drafts in modern Punjabi (it's pretty good at this — we use nano-gpt already for glosses), you and/or a fluent native speaker edit for register and accuracy, audio prewarm via Chirp 3
- **Time**: ~1-2 weeks of focused writing + editing if you do it yourself, or commission for ~£30-50/story
- **Per-story production**: probably 1-2 hours including editing + audio prewarm

Realistic to ship **5 akhar Stories as part of MVP**, then add 2-3 per week post-launch until the 20-30 are done. Within 2-3 months we'd have the full core canon in modern accessible Punjabi.

## Updated answer to your original question

So my recommendation flips. The MVP plan should be:

1. **Hide unprocessed classical sakhis** (still the right call)
2. **Process ~5 classical sakhis** for the archive (the most accessible Bhai Vir Singh ones)
3. **Write ~5 akhar Stories** for the modern library (the 5 most-loved core sakhis in accessible modern Punjabi)
4. **Two clearly-labelled libraries** in the reader UI
5. Same hover-translation + audio + save-to-deck experience across both — they're parallel content libraries, not different products

Free tier sees a couple of each. Pro tier sees all of each.

Grandma reads the classical ones because she's learning new vocab from Bhai Vir Singh. A heritage parent reads akhar Stories with their kid before bedtime. Both feel like akhar is built for them.

- **Do you (or someone in your network) feel confident writing modern-Punjabi prose**, even with LLM drafts? Or do we need to plan from day one to commission?
- **Are you comfortable being the editorial voice** for the akhar Stories — picking which events to cover first, sanity-checking the register, ensuring respectful tone?
- **List of the first 5 sakhis to write** — want to do this together? I can propose a list, you (and ideally grandma) sanity-check it.

If yes to all three, I think this is the right call and your instinct was correct. I was wrong to push back so hard.

Yeah you're right, I overthought the two-library thing. One library with smart difficulty/era labelling is better UX and stays conceptually cleaner.

## What one unified library looks like

The user opens **Library** and sees all sakhis in one place. Each entry shows:

```
ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਦਾ ਜਨਮ                              ★★    [Modern]
The birth of Guru Nanak                                    akhar Stories · 2 min read

ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਦਾ ਜਨਮ                              ★★★★  [Classical]
The birth of Guru Nanak                                    Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s · 8 min read

ਬਾਲ ਜੀਵਨ - ਸਪ ਦੀ ਛਾਂ                       ★★★   [Classical]
Childhood: the snake's shade                               Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s · 6 min read
```

Same story (Guru Nanak's birth) appears once as modern akhar Stories at ★★, once as the classical Bhai Vir Singh at ★★★★. User picks based on where they're at. No "two libraries" friction — it's one shelf with everything on it, labelled honestly.

Alternative grouping (probably cleaner): one row per *event*, with the versions stacked underneath:

```
The birth of Guru Nanak                          [Guru Nanak · Book 1 · Childhood]
  ★★   Modern · akhar Stories · 2 min          → Read
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s · 8 min → Read

Childhood: the snake's shade
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s · 6 min → Read
  (modern version coming soon)

Childhood: the cobra at midday
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh, 1920s · 7 min → Read
  ★★   Modern · akhar Stories · 3 min          → Read
```

This makes it really clear: same story, pick your level. The grouping itself is a feature ("I want to read about Guru Nanak's childhood — what versions do you have?"). And it makes the modern coverage visible without making it look like a separate product.

## Implementation impact

Schema change to support this is small. Add three columns to the `sakhis` table:
- `event_key` — text slug like `guru-nanak-birth`, links related versions
- `register` — `classical` / `modern`
- `difficulty` — int 1-5

The current 405 classical sakhis all get `register='classical'` and a hand-picked `difficulty` during the verification pass. The new akhar Stories we write get `register='modern'` and matching `event_key` to the classical they retell.

Library UI groups by `event_key` when there's more than one version, falls back to a flat row when there's only one. Filter chips at the top — "All / Easy first / Modern only / Classical only" — let users slice however they want.

## Reading experience stays the same regardless

Both versions share the same reader: parallel text + hover translation + audio + save-to-deck + sentence playback + word highlight. We're not building two readers, just two content types feeding one reader.

The only different bit is the **header info** at the top of each sakhi:
- Classical: "Bhai Vir Singh · Gur Balam Sakhian · 1920s · ★★★★"
- Modern: "akhar Original · written by [you/contributor] · 2026 · ★★"

Honest provenance, never hidden.

## What this changes about the build plan

Mostly nothing — the architecture I had assumed two libraries but the data model + UI move easily to one library with badges. The pieces still split as:

1. **Hide unprocessed sakhis** (same as before)
2. **Add `register` + `difficulty` + `event_key` columns**
3. **Library UI**: group by event_key, show difficulty + register badges, filter chips
4. **Write 5 akhar Stories** — modern retellings of the most-loved core events, matched to existing classical versions by event_key
5. **Audit pass on 5 classical sakhis** so they're properly processed for launch

By MVP launch the library could look like:

- 10 events total
- 5 of them have both modern + classical versions  
- 5 of them have classical only (the more advanced ones)
- 0 modern-only — every modern retelling is paired with the classical it covers, so the cross-link always works

That's a real library with depth on day one, in both registers, **with each story being one entry that user picks their level for**.

- Same story can appear as multiple versions, grouped together

Honest answer, since you asked:

**The akhar Stories idea is genuinely good. But I'm worried about something bigger.**

We've been planning this product across several long sessions. The engineering is solid — alphabet curriculum, sakhi reader, hover translations, audio, SRS deck, auth, mobile pass, all real and well-built. The strategy docs are thorough. The grandma anecdote is real product insight.

But here's the uncomfortable thing: **nothing has shipped to real users yet, and we keep adding scope.** Every conversation produces a new idea worth building. Each is reasonable. Cumulatively they're the difference between launching in 6 weeks vs 6 months.

The akhar Stories idea is the latest example. It's a good idea. It's also another content production project that doesn't exist today, that requires us to write modern Punjabi prose, that adds a data-model change, that delays MVP. We don't know yet whether the **existing** classical-sakhi reading experience even works for real users — because no real users have tried it.

What I'd actually do if I were you:

**Ship the existing MVP scope in the next 2 weeks.** What we have now — alphabet + sakhi reader + hover translations + audio + SRS + auth + onboarding + mobile-responsive — is genuinely more than 90% of "Punjabi apps" currently on the market. Put it on akhar.io behind the free tier we already designed, and **send the link to 10 people**: grandma, her friends, a couple of GCSE Punjabi candidates from a local supplementary school, 2-3 heritage parents in your network. Don't even charge anyone yet. Just watch.

You'll learn more in one week of real users hovering real words than in another month of strategy. You'll find out:
- Whether anyone gives a damn about the classical sakhi specifically
- Whether they understand the alphabet curriculum
- Whether the hover popovers actually help or just confuse
- Whether the audio is the killer feature or the SRS deck is
- Whether grandma's reaction generalises or was n=1

**Then** — based on real signal — you decide whether akhar Stories is worth writing or whether the priority is processing 20 more classical sakhis or building GCSE topic packs or fixing something we haven't even noticed yet.

The pattern I'm worried about: smart founders sometimes plan their way into never shipping. The product gets theoretically better with every conversation but practically worse because no learning is happening. We're not there yet but we're sliding that way. The questions keep coming because we keep widening scope.

**My recommendation for next steps:**

1. **Stop the akhar Stories decision for now.** It's a good idea, write it down, defer it. We don't have enough information to know if it's actually needed.
2. **Spend the next 1-2 weeks on**: processing 10-15 more classical sakhis (LLM-assisted pipeline upgrade I keep mentioning) + hiding the unprocessed ones + getting akhar.io DNS live + final polish.
3. **Send the link to 10 real people**. Tell them it's a free preview, no paywall yet, you want honest feedback. Make sure PostHog is wired so you can see what they do.
4. **In 2 weeks we revisit this conversation** with actual data. Maybe the answer is "grandma's friends loved the classical, no need for modern retellings" and we save weeks of work. Maybe it's "everyone bounced because they didn't understand the words, we need akhar Stories ASAP" and we know to prioritise it. We don't have to guess — we can know.

I think the akhar Stories instinct is correct directionally, by the way. The grandma loved classical, but grandma is fluent — most of your audience isn't her. Modern accessible content probably IS needed. But "probably" is doing a lot of work in that sentence, and we can replace it with "definitely" or "actually no" with two weeks of real users.

That's my honest take. **Stop adding scope, ship what we have, learn from actual users, then decide what's next based on data.**

Right. 3+3, paired together. Let's do it. On the blending question — yes, I think it works, but the UX detail matters. Let me walk through it.

## The natural grouping: pair them by event

If we pick the **same 3 events** for both modern and classical versions, the library is dead simple:

```
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  GURU NANAK · CHILDHOOD                                  3 stories

  ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਦਾ ਜਨਮ                   The birth of Guru Nanak
  ★★ Easy · akhar Stories · 2 min          → Read
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh · 8 min   → Read

  ਸੱਚਾ ਸੌਦਾ                         The True Bargain
  ★★ Easy · akhar Stories · 2 min          → Read
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh · 7 min   → Read

  ਭਾਈ ਲਾਲੋ ਅਤੇ ਮਲਿਕ ਭਾਗੋ            Bhai Lalo and Malik Bhago
  ★★ Easy · akhar Stories · 2 min          → Read
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh · 9 min   → Read
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
```

User sees 3 story titles. Each is "the same sakhi, two ways." Beginner reads the modern one first; if they enjoyed it they tap the classical version next and now they're primed — they know the events, so the harder vocabulary just adds register and depth.

This is the *killer pedagogical loop* honestly. It's exactly how language teachers use "graded readers" — read the easy version to internalise the plot + core vocab, then read the source to feel the literary register. We get that for free by pairing.

## Why this beats the alternatives

Three other layouts I considered:

**A. Flat list with badges** — every sakhi is its own row. ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਦਾ ਜਨਮ appears twice (once Easy, once Classical) at different positions. **Problem**: feels like duplicates. User doesn't realise they're the same story until they read both.

**B. "Two libraries" sidebar** — separate tabs for Modern vs Classical. **Problem**: the very thing you (rightly) pushed back against. Splits the audience, makes one side feel like the "real" library and the other like the simplification corner.

**C. Toggle switch at top of each story** — "Read as Easy / Classical." **Problem**: hides the choice from the index. Users don't see "ah there's a modern version available" until they've already opened the classical one and bounced.

The grouped layout is the only one where the choice is visible AND honest about what's available AND positions both as first-class.

## One real question: what if a story only has one version?

For MVP, the **3 events have both versions**. Future content will have asymmetry — many classical sakhis with no modern equivalent, possibly some modern-only ones for very young readers. The layout handles this fine:

```
  ਪਾਣੀ ਨਾਨਕ ਨੂੰ ਉੱਪਰ ਚੱਕ ਲਿਆ           The water lifted Nanak
  ★★★★ Classical · Bhai Vir Singh · 6 min   → Read

  ਖੇਡਾਂ                              Childhood games (coming soon)
  ★ Easy · akhar Stories                    → Notify me
```

So users see what exists, what's coming, and what they can do now. No mystery, no clutter.

## What this needs from the data model

Schema change is small. Add to `sakhis` table:

- `event_key` text (e.g., `nanak-birth`, `sacha-sauda`, `lalo-bhago`) — same value for paired versions
- `register` text (`classical` / `modern`)  
- `difficulty` int 1-5
- `source` text (`Bhai Vir Singh — Gur Balam Sakhian, 1920s` / `akhar Stories`)
- `processed` boolean (already discussed)

For the 3 new modern stories, they get NEW rows in the same `sakhis` table — not a separate table. They share the event_key with their classical counterparts. They show up in the library grouped together automatically.

This means **akhar Stories aren't a separate system** — they're just sakhis with `register='modern'` and `source='akhar Stories'`. Same reader, same audio infrastructure, same save-to-deck, same everything. The classification is in metadata, not architecture. Easy to extend later (commission more, etc.) without restructuring.

## The 3 events I'd pick

Universal-recognition tier — these are the sakhis every Sikh child knows from grandparents' bedtime stories:

1. **ਨਾਨਕ ਜੀ ਦਾ ਜਨਮ — The Birth of Guru Nanak**
   - Talwandi, the holy birth, Pandit Hardayal naming him
   - We already have a classical version (current sakhi 1)
   - Easy modern version is short: ~150 words of accessible Punjabi

2. **ਸੱਚਾ ਸੌਦਾ — The True Bargain**  
   - Father gives young Nanak 20 rupees to invest; Nanak feeds sadhus instead
   - Classic moral/spiritual lesson, kid-friendly
   - Already in the classical corpus

3. **ਭਾਈ ਲਾਲੋ ਅਤੇ ਮਲਿਕ ਭਾਗੋ — Bhai Lalo and Malik Bhago**
   - Honest carpenter's bread bleeds milk; rich man's feast bleeds blood
   - Striking imagery, very memorable
   - Already in the classical corpus

These three together = the "Guru Nanak essentials" trio. Anyone who finishes these gets the foundational stories of Sikhi. Anyone who reads all 6 (modern + classical of each) has had real reading practice across registers.

## Honest question this raises — who writes the modern versions?

I want to flag this clearly. To write 3 modern akhar Stories that don't sound like LLM slop, we need a workflow:

**Option 1: You + Claude draft + grandma sanity-checks.** You set the register and vibe, Claude drafts based on the classical Bhai Vir Singh text, you edit for flow, grandma reads it and tells you what sounds off. Workable, gets done in a few days.

**Option 2: Commission a Punjabi writer.** ~£30-100 per story from a UK-based Punjabi children's-book author. Better register, more authentic, slower (a couple of weeks). Adds cost.

**Option 3: You write them entirely yourself.** You speak Punjabi at home. If you're comfortable writing 150-200 words of prose three times, do it. Most authentic.

**My recommendation: Option 1 for MVP.** It's fast, you stay in editorial control, the LLM drafts are usable for register guidance, grandma's involvement gives them real authenticity. Move to Option 2 when we commission more stories post-launch.
