Research is back — here's the synthesis + my opinionated take on the current landing page + a proposed redesign. Let me know which direction before I build.

# What's wrong with the current landing page

Going through it ruthlessly (`app/page.tsx`):

1. **"Read classical Punjabi, word by word"** — you nailed it: this positions us as a *reading tool*, not a *learning product*. It's literally a feature description, not a value prop. A heritage learner scanning this in 2 seconds thinks "academic" and bounces.

2. **Hero subtext does positioning, methodology, AND audience in one breath** — Babbel/Pimsleur all keep these as separate sections. Cramming "modern + classical, hover for etymology, save to deck" into one paragraph means none of it lands.

3. **Library shown to logged-out users** — your call to gate it, and I agree (more on this below).

4. **"Built for the diaspora — beginners, heritage learners, and anyone who never got the chance to learn the language they grew up hearing"** is actually the *best line on the page* and it's buried at the bottom in 12px grey text. That's the *real* emotional hook and it should be way more prominent.

5. **No mention of stories, Gurus, history, or context** in the hero — which is the actual differentiator. We're hiding what makes us different and leading with what makes us sound like a dictionary.

# Key research findings (compressed)

- **Sub-agent looked at**: Duolingo, Babbel, Busuu, Pimsleur, italki, Hallow (faith), Bible.com, Rosetta, Heritage Language Tool (the closest direct comp).
- **One headline, one promise, one CTA.** Don't try to do everything above the fold.
- **Specificity beats superlatives.** "92% improved in 2 months" beats "highly effective."
- **Show the product, don't describe it.** italki shows teacher faces. Bible.com shows story names. We should show a real sakhi with a hover popover mid-action.
- **Heritage positioning wins by naming the asymmetry**: "you can understand it but can't read it." The Heritage Language Tool's *"the language is yours, but it doesn't always feel that way"* is the emotional register.
- **For akhar specifically, the biggest anti-patterns**: stock photos of smiling tourists in cafés, "learn Punjabi for free" SEO bait, Duolingo-style gamification imagery, vague "new you" copy.
- **Credibility signals that work for us** (we don't have user-count to brag): corpus depth ("405 sakhis from 5 classical sources"), source attribution (Bhai Vir Singh, classical janamsakhis), an endorsement from one named granthi or AQA teacher, AQA syllabus alignment called out by name.
- **The risk to avoid**: sounding *worthy/reverent*. Sakhi content drifts there fast and reverent copy doesn't convert 20-somethings.

# Proposed redesign

**Above the fold — one screen, three jobs:**

- **Headline**: `Learn Punjabi through the stories your grandparents grew up with.`
- **Sub-headline**: `Real sakhis from the lives of the Gurus, in modern Punjabi and the classical literary register. Hover any word for meaning, etymology, and audio. Made for heritage readers and GCSE/A-Level Panjabi students.`
- **Visual** (the hero image): a **live screenshot of a sakhi** — parallel Gurmukhi + English, with one word actively hovered showing a popover with romanisation + gloss + audio button. The product *is* the screenshot. No phone mockup, no smiling stock face.
- **Primary CTA**: `Sign up to start reading` (since you want content gated). Secondary: `How it works` anchor link.

**Sections below (in order):**

1. **The library, named (but locked)** — show 6-8 actual sakhi titles in a "what's inside" preview, each with a lock icon → sign-in. Heritage learners see *"Birth of Guru Nanak"*, *"The True Bargain"*, *"Milk or Blood"* and recognise these — instant credibility.
2. **For you if...** — three columns: heritage reader · GCSE/A-Level student · adult learner reconnecting. Self-qualifies traffic.
3. **How it works** — three steps with real screenshots: parallel text → hover any word → sentence audio with highlighting.
4. **Why sakhis, not flashcards** — the manifesto. Address the objection: *"Not a scripture app. Not Duolingo for Punjabi. Stories first, language second."*
5. **Two reading levels demo** — same sakhi shown classical vs modern, side by side. Uniquely visual differentiator.
6. **Built on the classical sources** — list the books we're working from (Bhai Vir Singh, etc.). The corpus stats give us authority no startup can fake quickly.
7. **FAQ** — "Do I need to know Gurmukhi already?" / "Is this religious?" / "AQA coverage?" / "Pricing?"
8. **Closing CTA**.

# One tension I want to flag before building

You said *"if you are not logged in you shouldn't see or do any content."* The research strongly disagrees — Bible.com, Hallow, italki all let you *taste* before sign-up because content IS the proof. For us, that would mean letting a logged-out visitor read **one** sakhi (probably the modern Birth of Guru Nanak) without auth, with the rest of the library locked. The deck, hover lookups, audio, etc., would still need sign-in to *save* — but they could read one to *feel* the product.

> AGENT

Your friends are right and you should listen to them. "Just sakhis" works for a specific kind of learner (you, me, the heritage-cultural-curious crowd), but most people learning Punjabi want to be able to actually *talk* — to their dadi-ji, at the dukaan, with their cousins. That's a different job from reading classical literature. If we don't serve that, we're a niche literary tool, not a Punjabi learning app.

Here's the tension I want to be honest about: **sakhis are our differentiator, but they're nobody's "must-have."** People want sakhis when they discover us, not when they're searching for "learn Punjabi." So we need basics as the *gateway* and sakhis as the *depth*.

A few ways this could shake out, with tradeoffs:

**A) Bolt on a basics module.** Short lessons on greetings/family/numbers/colors/tenses, separate from sakhis. Clean, fast to build. Risk: we look like Duolingo with a fancy library attached — basics feel un-akhar.

**B) Themed lessons that bridge into sakhis.** Each theme (family, food, work, weather) has bite-size vocabulary + a sample dialogue + a short sakhi snippet using those words. *"Family" lesson teaches mata/pita/bhain/veer, then you read the opening of sakhi 1 where Tripta Ji and Mehta Kalu Ji are named.* This is the most "us" approach but content-heavy.

**C) Conversational track + sakhi track as parallel paths.** Two clear journeys on the homepage. Users pick "I want to talk to my family" or "I want to read the stories." This is the most honest about the two audiences but feels less unified.

My instinct is **(B), pared down for MVP**: pick 5-6 themes that map to existing sakhi content (people, family, household, food, money, conflict — all heavily present in sakhi 1, 16, 38), build short lessons around them, and the sakhi reading is the "now apply it" payoff. It gives basics-seekers immediate gratification AND keeps the sakhi-centered positioning intact.

But before I weigh in further, two things I need to know to give you a real answer:

1. **Who's giving this feedback?** Diaspora Punjabis like your friends? Total beginners with no family connection? UK GCSE candidates? Their needs are quite different.

2. **What specifically did they ask for?** "I want to chat with my dadi" is a different product than "I want to memorize 1000 vocab words" which is different from "I want to understand Punjabi songs." Did they name use cases, or just say "more basics"?

What did you actually hear?

Good — that gives us a clear north star. **Basics → Reading practice → Sakhis → Gurbani**, with a real progression system that always tells you "what's next."

Here's a sketch of how I'd lay out the spine. Push back hard on anything that feels wrong.

## A 5-stage path

**Stage 1: Foundations** (weeks 1-2)
35 letters, matras, diacritics — bite-size, audio-first. First ~50 essential words drawn from sakhi vocabulary (people, simple nouns). Outcome: can sound out any Punjabi word.

**Stage 2: Everyday Punjabi** (weeks 3-6)
Themed mini-lessons (5-10 min each): family · numbers · colors · time · home · food · weather · money · feelings · simple verbs. Each theme = 10-15 words + 1 sentence pattern + a 2-3 sentence "reading bite." Tenses introduced gently as you meet them. Outcome: can read short modern sentences.

**Stage 3: Modern sakhis** (weeks 7-12)
The akhar Stories. Full readings with hover etymology + audio + saved-words flowing into your deck. Lessons here = "read one sakhi + review its new vocab." Outcome: independent reading of modern Punjabi prose.

**Stage 4: Classical sakhis** (weeks 13+)
Bhai Vir Singh + janamsakhis — literary register, archaic vocabulary, longer sentences. Outcome: read Bhai Vir Singh comfortably.

**Stage 5: Gurbani** (advanced)
Selected shabads with translation, transliteration, grammar notes for archaic constructions. Optional — your "now I can finally read Japji" payoff.

## What a single lesson looks like

5-10 minutes, always the same shape:
1. **3-5 new words** with audio + romanization + meaning
2. **One pattern** showing how they connect ("X ਦਾ ਨਾਉਂ Y ਹੈ")
3. **One reading bite** — a real sentence using them, sakhi-derived where possible
4. **Quick check** — recognize / hear / type
5. **Words auto-save** to your deck for SRS

## The path UI

Think Duolingo's vertical path, but adult: a clean vertical line of nodes, each node a lesson or a sakhi or a reading. You always see the next 2-3 nodes ahead. You can jump back to anything you've completed. **Onboarding's `prior_knowledge` field drops you in at the right stage** — `none` starts at Stage 1, `spoken` at Stage 2, `readable` at Stage 3.

## What I'd steal from Duolingo (and what I wouldn't)

**Steal:** the always-visible "next step," 5-min commitment threshold, topic clusters, visual progress, light streak signal.
**Skip:** XP/crowns/leagues, owl notifications, endless drilling, child-energy gamification — your audience is adults learning their ancestral language, not 12-year-olds.

---

## Three decisions I want your gut on before going further

1. **Sequential or open?** Strict unlock (Stage 2 locked until Stage 1 done) vs suggested-but-skippable (path shows the recommended order but power users can jump anywhere). I lean **suggested-but-skippable** — heritage learners often have weird gaps and hate gatekeeping. But strict gives stronger "I'm progressing" feeling.

2. **How early do sakhis appear?** Three options:
   - *Stage 3+ only* (clean separation: basics first, sakhis as reward)
   - *Stage 2 micro-snippets* (one-line sakhi quotes appear as reading bites, full sakhis come at Stage 3)
   - *Day one* (every reading bite, even on Day 1, is a real sakhi line) — most differentiating, hardest to author

3. **Grammar — explicit or implicit?** Babbel teaches grammar in dedicated lessons ("today: past tense"). Pimsleur teaches it by repeated patterns, never naming the rule. For reaching Gurbani eventually, I think we need **some explicit notes inline** ("This verb is in the historic present — common in classical Punjabi narration"), surfaced when it matters but not as standalone lessons.

Which of these three do you have the strongest opinion on?

The vocab-gated sakhi idea is the best thing in this conversation. Let me sketch what it actually means in practice, because it changes how we design lessons.

## The unlock loop

Every sakhi gets a **vocabulary requirement list** — the high-frequency words you need to know to read it without the hover popup feeling like a crutch. Each lesson teaches a chunk of those words. When you've learned enough, the sakhi "unlocks" and appears on your path as the next milestone.

**Concretely, for sakhi 1 (Birth of Guru Nanak):** maybe 25 required words — mother/father/sister/brother, the names (Nanak/Tripta/Kalu/Nanaki), to-be/to-say/to-do, "village," "year." Do the *family*, *names*, and *common verbs* themes → "Birth of Guru Nanak" unlocks on your path.

This creates a really nice loop:
- "Learn these 15 words and you can read the Birth of Guru Nanak" — concrete motivation
- Sakhi acts as the **payoff**, not as another lesson
- Reading it teaches you ~20 new words in context, which feed back into your deck and unlock the *next* sakhi
- The path naturally rhythms: **lessons → sakhi → lessons → sakhi → lessons → classical sakhi**

## What this means for the 4 stages

Same shape, Gurbani gone:
1. **Foundations** — letters + sounds + first ~50 sakhi words
2. **Everyday Punjabi** — themes (family, food, numbers, etc.) — each theme also pre-loading words for an upcoming sakhi
3. **Modern sakhis** — akhar Stories unlock as you complete the relevant themes
4. **Classical sakhis** — Bhai Vir Singh + janamsakhis unlock once you've absorbed enough modern register

Themes aren't arbitrary — they're chosen because **each one unlocks 1-2 specific sakhis**. That's the design discipline that keeps it sakhi-focused without forcing sakhis on Day 1.

## The next thing to nail

I think the question to answer next is: **what does the user actually see when they log in?**

Two candidates:

**A. A vertical path** (Duolingo-style). Today's lesson at the top, next 2-3 nodes visible, sakhi milestones glowing with "12/15 words to unlock." Feels like progression. Risk: rigid, easy to feel "behind."

**B. A dashboard** — "Continue lesson" tile + "Saved words to review" tile + "Sakhis you can read now" tile + "Sakhis you're close to unlocking" tile. More flexible, less "what to do next" obvious.

I lean **A** for absolute beginners (the path is the whole product) and a **dashboard that includes a path** for returning users. Maybe the same screen, just configured by stage.

Which one feels more like how you'd want a learner to land?

> DEVELOPER

I think the duolingo style is nice. I also think that we have to be sure that all the vocab for the sakhis are actually in the theme. And I don't think we should teach names in vocab because those are strictly sakhi based you know?

> AGENT

Both calls are right and they tighten the design discipline significantly.

**On names** — yes, agreed. Names are sakhi-specific (Tripta/Kalu/Mardana/Bhai Lalo/Malik Bhago aren't reusable vocabulary), and padding "family theme" with them would make the lesson feel hollow. Best handling: each sakhi gets a tiny **"cast" intro** at the top — *"In this sakhi: Mehta Kalu Ji (Nanak's father), Mata Tripta Ji (his mother), Bibi Nanaki (his sister)..."* — quick read, doesn't pretend to be a vocab lesson. Names get hover popovers too, so the reader can always re-check.

**On coverage discipline** — this is the rule that makes or breaks the whole thing. Every non-name word in a sakhi must be covered by the themes the learner has done by the time it unlocks. Otherwise the unlock is a lie. This means we design themes *backwards from the sakhi vocabulary*, not forwards from a generic textbook.

Practically, that means for any sakhi:
1. Tokenize it
2. Strip out proper nouns (auto via the cast list)
3. Strip out things that are purely classical-register decoration handled by hover (e.g., literary archaisms — they get glossed in-line, not pre-taught)
4. Whatever's left = the required vocab set
5. Every word in that set must belong to a theme

The themes that emerge will probably look weirder than Duolingo's. Sakhi 1's vocab will demand themes like *"common modern verbs (be/say/do/go)"* and *"family + village life"* — but it'll also force in things like *"the small world of a child"* (cradle, courtyard, pond) and maybe *"religious figures we'll keep meeting"* (sant, faqir, sadhu — these recur across sakhis 1, 16, 38). That's fine — it's honest to the reading goal.

If this works cleanly we'll see a reasonable curriculum emerge (probably 8-12 themes to unlock sakhi 1). If it doesn't — if we end up with weird ungroupable words or a 30-theme monster — the design needs rethinking before we build anything.

Ah — themes-first, sakhi as the cultural payoff for each theme. That's much cleaner than what I was proposing. Let me play it back to make sure I've got it:

## The actual structure

**Each theme has three things:**
1. **Vocab + grammar lessons** (standard — colors, family, school, weather, etc.)
2. **Generic practice sentences** (the fabricated "Raj goes to school" Duolingo stuff — useful for drilling)
3. **One short simple sakhi** as the cultural payoff — a real sakhi event, rewritten by us in modern Punjabi, focused on that theme's vocabulary

So the user's experience per theme: learn the words → drill them in everyday sentences → unlock and read a real Sikh story that uses them. Classical sakhis come *later* as Stage 3, no theme-mapping required — by then the learner has enough Punjabi to wade in.

## We're partly there already

The 3 modern akhar Stories I wrote are exactly this pattern — short, real sakhi events, modern simple Punjabi:

- **Birth of Guru Nanak** → naturally maps to *family / village life*
- **Sacha Sauda** → maps to *work, money, giving*
- **Bhai Lalo & Malik Bhago** → maps to *food, honest work, hospitality*

So three themes are already covered. We'd need to write maybe 7-10 more simple sakhis to cover the remaining themes — significant content work, but tractable, and they'd compound in value (each one becomes the payoff for one theme).

## The tension I want to flag

Some themes won't have a natural sakhi event:
- **Colors** — no sakhi is about colors
- **Numbers / dates / telling time** — these appear *inside* sakhis but no sakhi is *about* them
- **Days of the week, months** — same
- **Modern things** (technology, transport, jobs) — anachronistic to sakhis

For these we'd have a theme without a sakhi payoff — just vocab + generic practice. That's probably fine, but worth deciding now whether:
- (a) Every theme tries to have a sakhi, even if it's a stretch (force *"colors"* into the Wali Kandari story because there's a mention of water/sky/mountains?)
- (b) Some themes just don't have a sakhi payoff and that's OK
- (c) We design *themes* so they naturally map to sakhis, even if that means smaller / weirder themes ("the small world of childhood" instead of "colors")

I lean **(b)** — be honest, not every theme needs a story. Mundane themes can be efficient vocab learning, sakhi themes can be the deeper payoff. Forcing it would feel contrived.

## The next concrete question

**What's the initial theme list?** Probably 10-15 themes covering everything a learner needs to read modern Punjabi comfortably. Want to brainstorm that list together right now — or shall I take a first pass (with notes on which already have a sakhi vs need one written vs probably no sakhi) and you push back on it?

> AGENT

Yes — the alphabet → mukta → muharni → simple sentences → vocab sequence is the *actual* traditional Punjabi pedagogy. JS Nagra structures his book this way for a reason: muharni in particular is the unlock that takes you from "I recognise the letters" to "I can read any word." Skipping it is what most modern apps get wrong.

## Stage 1 as I understand it

This is pre-vocab-themes, all about reading capability:

1. **Paintee akhar** — the 35 letters with audio + recognition
2. **Mukta words** — reading consonant-only words (ਘਰ, ਕਮਲ, ਜਨਮ). Builds *letters → words* fluency before matras complicate things.
3. **Muharni** — the systematic consonant × matra grid (ਕ ਕਾ ਕਿ ਕੀ ਕੁ ਕੂ ਕੇ ਕੈ ਕੋ ਕੌ ...). Heavy drill but the unlock.
4. **Diacritics** — tippi/bindi (nasalisation) + addhak (gemination)
5. **Basic sentences** — short sentences using everything above, sakhi-derived where possible

Only *after* Stage 1 do we get into themed vocab.

I think our `/alphabet` curriculum already covers letters + mukta + some diacritics (from what I saw in `data/curriculum.ts`), but I'm not sure muharni gets its own dedicated drill section. Worth checking — if not, that's a real gap.

## Stage 2 — the themes question

Here's where I think real research helps, because there are three different lenses for picking themes:

1. **Standard L2 curriculum themes** (CEFR-aligned) — greetings, family, numbers, food, time, weather, home, school, transport, feelings, etc. ~20-25 themes.
2. **Traditional Punjabi school curriculum** — what India's state Punjabi syllabus actually teaches in years 1-3 (often different priorities, more cultural).
3. **Heritage-learner-specific** — what *diaspora* learners actually need first: family terms (for dadi-ji conversations), religious-cultural figures (sant/sadhu/granthi for sakhi reading), Punjab geography, common honorifics, kinship terms beyond the English equivalents (mama, masi, bua etc).

The right answer is probably a blend, but the proportions matter — heavy on heritage-learner needs in the early themes, mainstream L2 themes coming in later.

## Where research would help

I'd want to look at:

- **JS Nagra's full curriculum sequence** (since we already model on it) — what specifically does he have for muharni + what order do his post-alphabet topics come in?
- **The Indian Punjabi school syllabus** (CBSE / Punjab board, Years 1-3) — what's the canonical order Punjabi-medium kids learn in?
- **Heritage-language curricula** — how do Yiddish/Hebrew/Mandarin heritage programs sequence themes for diaspora kids vs total beginners?
- **Sikh missionary college Punjabi courses** (if any are public) — they have specifically the heritage-learner-meets-cultural-text problem we have

## What I'd ask you

Before I spin off a research agent — **which of those three lenses do you want to weight heaviest?** I think there are three possible product positions:

- (a) **Modern L2 standard** with cultural seasoning — basically Duolingo's themes, with sakhi payoffs. Broadest market, least differentiated.
- (b) **Traditional Punjabi-school sequence** — what an Indian kid learns first. Most "authentic" but maybe too primary-school-feeling for adult diaspora.
- (c) **Heritage-learner-led** — themes ordered by what your dadi-ji conversation needs first, then broadens. Most differentiated, narrowest initial audience.

My gut says **(c) with (a) layered in later** — own the heritage learner first, then broaden. But I want your read.

> AGENT

Research is in. Synthesizing + making the auto-mode calls on the open questions.

## Top three findings

1. **Personal-world-first is universal** — every credible curriculum (UK GCSE, PSEB, NETA Hebrew, Ma Liping Chinese, Spanish-for-Heritage) opens with the same 4-6 themes: greetings → family → home → school/daily routine → food. Not coincidence; tracks actual beginner discourse frequency.

2. **Heritage learners are NOT L2 beginners.** The HL literature is unanimous: skip vocabulary your learner already knows passively (colors, numbers, "uncle/aunt"). Teach instead the things English flattens out — kinship granularity (chacha/taya/mama/phupa), religious-cultural register, idioms parents use untranslated.

3. **Sakhis should ANCHOR themes, not END them.** This is the biggest design shift from what we discussed. NETA, Ma Liping, Workers Circle all bind language to text-as-identity *early*. "Vocab lesson → sakhi as reward" is the L2-beginner mistake HL pedagogy warns against. Each theme should be *built around* a sakhi, not chase one.

## Proposed 12-theme spine (full table in `docs/curriculum-research.md`)

| # | Theme | Sakhi anchor | Have it? |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hello & me | "Sat Sri Akal" etymology | No sakhi needed |
| 2 | Family | Birth of Guru Nanak | ✓ |
| 3 | Home | Bhai Vir Singh domestic scene | use corpus |
| 4 | Food & honest work | Bhai Lalo & Malik Bhago | ✓ |
| 5 | Daily routine | *amrit vela* explainer | No sakhi needed |
| 6 | School & learning | Young Nanak / broken slate | **need to write** |
| 7 | Festivals & gurpurabs | Vaisakhi 1699 | **need to write** |
| 8 | Seva & community | Bhagat Puran Singh | **need to write** |
| 9 | Places & travel | Panj-aab geography | No sakhi needed |
| 10 | Nature & seasons | Bhai Vir Singh nature poem | use corpus |
| 11 | Trades & work | Bhagat Namdev / Kabir | **need to write** |
| 12 | Emotions & inner life | Guru Tegh Bahadur sacrifice | **need to write** (sensitive) |

After theme 12 → existing classical sakhi track.

**Defer past v1**: tech/social media, sport, politics, careers, healthy living. GCSE noise for our audience.

## My calls on the open questions (auto mode)

1. **Audience age** → 35-yo professional reconnecting. We already stripped GCSE positioning; the path follows.
2. **How religious** → Sikh-Punjabi explicitly. You're a Sikh-Punjabi building this; all 3 existing akhar Stories are Sikh; the FAQ already handles non-Sikh sensitivities ("not a scripture app, the focus is the language"). Pretending we're equally serving Hindu/Muslim Punjabis would be dishonest positioning. Sikh-Punjabi-first; non-Sikh Punjabis welcome but not the design target.
3. **Modern vs village Punjab** → Lean classical-village for sakhi content (that's our corpus moat), but contemporary framing for *theme cards* and explainer copy. Don't try to manufacture modern sakhis we don't have.
4. **Pacing** → 1 theme per 1-2 weeks of casual use; 10-12 week course total for foundation → all 12 themes. Roughly Ma Liping-pace, half NETA-pace.
5. **Sakhi gap (5 new ones needed)** → LLM-assisted draft → grandma editorial pass, same pipeline that produced the existing 3. Not a blocker for curriculum design; gates rollout of themes 6-12.
6. **Patshahi 6/7/8/9 coverage** → Align with theme rollout (Guru Tegh Bahadur for theme 12, Guru Har Rai for theme 8) — kills two birds.

## Next concrete step

The research changes one important thing in our model: **sakhi-as-anchor, not sakhi-as-payoff**. That has implications for how the path UI looks (the sakhi is the centre of each theme, not a milestone at the end) and for the landing page copy (which still says "save words to your deck and read sakhis as you go").

I'd hold off on changing the landing again until we've agreed the new theme-anchored model is right. The natural next step is to **sketch what one theme actually looks like as a sequence of lessons** — pick "Family" (since we have the sakhi), design the 5-8 lessons that lead in and out of the Birth of Guru Nanak, see if the shape works. If it does, the path UI and landing copy follow.

## The paywall conversation

Standard heritage-app patterns I've seen — Babbel, Memrise, Hallow, language apps in general — split roughly on this axis:

- **Free** = "enough to fall in love with the pedagogy"
- **Pro** = "the whole library + the moat"

Here's a sketch for akhar — push back hard:

| Feature | Free | Pro |
|---|---|---|
| Foundations (paintee + mukta + muharni + matras + diacritics + sentences) | ✅ Full | — |
| Themes 1-3 (Hello/me, Family, Home) | ✅ Full | — |
| Themes 4-12 | Locked preview | ✅ Full |
| First 3 sakhis in library (the akhar Stories) | ✅ Full | — |
| All 405 classical sakhis | First 3 only | ✅ Full |
| Hover popovers (gloss + audio) | ✅ Full | — |
| Full dictionary entries (Punjabipedia, etymology depth) | Hover gloss only | ✅ Full |
| Save words to deck | Up to 30 | ✅ Unlimited |
| SRS review (the FSRS engine) | Daily limit (5 cards) | ✅ Full review session |
| Offline audio downloads | — | ✅ Full |

**Why this split:**
- Free is *genuinely useful* — someone can learn to read Punjabi and finish 3 sakhis without paying. That's the "fall in love" allotment.
- Pro is *the moat* — the full course, the classical library, the SRS engine running at full strength. The completionist + serious learner stuff.
- The "value moment" is sharp: you finish the Family theme + sakhi, hit the next-theme paywall, you can *see* the rest of the path. Conversion intent maxes out exactly there.

**Pricing I'd float** (no strong opinion until we test):
- £6.99/month
- £49.99/year (annual saves ~40%, hits the "this is good value" mental threshold)
- Maybe lifetime at £149 — heritage learners are emotionally committed buyers; some will pay once and never bother again. Also helps cash flow.
- 7-day free Pro trial on signup

2. **Paywall split** — does the free vs Pro table above feel about right, or are you instinctively more generous / less generous? The biggest specific call: **is /alphabet (the foundations) free or Pro?** I had it free above because gating the entry pedagogy would tank early funnel, but you've previously said you thought the full alphabet was "a bit generous" to give away free.

## One real product gap I want to flag

When a beginner finishes the foundations curriculum, they currently have no obvious "next step" except *"go open a sakhi."* That's a big difficulty jump. Without vocab themes as a bridge, a learner will hit even the easy akhar Stories cold and feel overwhelmed.

Two ways to soften this without building the full theme path:

**(a) A guided first-sakhi flow.** The first time you finish foundations + open Birth of Guru Nanak, the reader walks you through the first paragraph with explicit "tap this word" prompts. A scripted onboarding moment, not a full theme system. 1-2 days of work.

**(b) Just accept the jump for MVP** — the akhar Stories are already much simpler than the classical sakhis, and the parallel English on every line is a real safety net. Watch what the analytics say and address if people bounce at that point.

I lean **(b)** — ship faster, learn from real signal. Add (a) if early data shows people dropping off at first sakhi.

- The vocab-themes mention in "How it works" step 1 (*"using real sakhi vocabulary"*) needs the same treatment unless we actually sakhi-derive the alphabet exercises before launch.

Yeah yeahts done, it uses token. But the page does say Set up custom SMTP

> AGENT

That warning matters — Supabase's built-in email service is rate-limited (~2-4 emails per hour on free tier, sometimes a bit more). If your launch brings any kind of signup burst — even 10 people in a WhatsApp group all trying to sign up at once — the later ones will hit the limit and get nothing. Bad day 1.

Two ways forward:

**(A) Set up Resend (or similar) as custom SMTP before launch — ~30 min**
- Sign up at https://resend.com (free tier: 100 emails/day, 3,000/month — way more than launch needs)
- Add `akhar.io` as a sending domain
- Add the SPF + DKIM TXT records they give you to **Cloudflare DNS** (which we're touching anyway for the Vercel domain flip in C3 — kill two birds)
- Grab the API key
- In Supabase → Auth → SMTP Settings → paste Resend SMTP credentials + set the "from" address
- Send yourself a test OTP to confirm
- Adds ~30 min before launch but unlocks real scale

**(B) Ship with the built-in SMTP, accept the risk**
- Fastest path to live (no extra work today)
- If signups arrive faster than ~3/hour, the later ones get nothing for ~20 min
- You'd be live but with auth flaking under the lightest burst
- Switch to Resend day 2 when you see actual volume

For a **launch day specifically** — where any flaked auth leaves a sour first impression — I'd genuinely recommend (A). 30 minutes is cheap insurance against "I tried to sign up but never got the code."

## Step 1 — Sign up at Resend + add akhar.io

1. Go to **https://resend.com/signup** — sign up with your work email (whatever you want associated). Free tier is fine.
2. Once you're in, in the left sidebar click **Domains** → **Add Domain**.
3. Enter **`akhar.io`** (just the root, no subdomain).
4. **Region**: pick **Frankfurt** (EU) since the rest of our stack is EU. Default may already be that.
5. Click **Add**.

You should land on a screen showing **3 DNS records** to add (an `MX` record, a `TXT` SPF record, and a `TXT` DKIM record — sometimes a 4th `TXT` for DMARC).

**Don't add them to Cloudflare yet — just keep that Resend screen open.** Once you're at the "verify DNS" screen, tell me you're there and I'll walk you through Step 2 (Cloudflare).

> DEVELOPER

I added to cloudflare check it pls

> AGENT

Almost there. The MX + SPF on `send.akhar.io` look right (route1/2/3 on the root are Cloudflare's existing email-routing — different system, no conflict). One thing to double-check on the DKIM record — it shows the `p=...` (the key) but I want to make sure it's prefixed with `v=DKIM1; k=rsa;` since some DNS UIs strip it:

> AGENT

Two of three look correct, the DKIM is suspicious. Let me explain:

| Record | What I see | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| `send.akhar.io` MX → `feedback-smtp.eu-west-1.amazonses.com.` (pri 10) | ✅ correct |
| `send.akhar.io` TXT → `v=spf1 include:amazonses.com ~all` | ✅ correct |
| `resend._domainkey.akhar.io` TXT → `p=MIGfMA0G...AQAB` | ⚠️ might be missing prefix |

The DKIM value normally starts with `v=DKIM1; k=rsa; p=...` — but yours shows just `p=...`. That can happen if the Cloudflare UI stripped the prefix on paste. Sometimes it works anyway, sometimes Gmail rejects emails as unsigned. Easiest fix path:

1. Go to your **Resend → Domains → akhar.io** page
2. Click the **Verify** (or **Verify Records**) button there
3. Tell me what Resend says about each of the 3 records

If Resend says all 3 are verified → we're golden, proceed to API key step.
If Resend says DKIM failed → I'll walk you through editing the Cloudflare TXT record to add the `v=DKIM1; k=rsa; ` prefix in front of the existing value.

Don't change anything in Cloudflare yet — let Resend tell us.

## Step 4 — Create a Resend API key

The API key is what Supabase will use to authenticate when sending the OTP emails. Don't reuse the test key Resend gives you on signup — create a fresh one scoped just for this.

1. In Resend, click **API Keys** in the left sidebar → **Create API Key**
2. Name it something memorable: **`akhar-supabase-smtp`**
3. **Permission**: pick **"Sending access"** (NOT full access — Supabase only needs to send, not read or manage)
4. **Domain**: restrict to **`akhar.io`** so this key can only send from that domain
5. Click **Add** — Resend shows you the key **once**. Copy it immediately (starts with `re_...`).

**Don't paste the key here** — it's a secret. Just confirm you've got it copied. Then I'll walk you through Step 5 (Supabase SMTP config), which is where you'll paste it.

A space crept into the Vercel-stored `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` value — `J23KwX2iUrF 08VcUE7B59A` should be `J23KwX2iUrF08VcUE7B59A` (no space). Let me check the local value's clean, then overwrite Vercel:

> AGENT

Vercel updated. Now triggering a redeploy so the new value gets baked in:

> AGENT

**Build is READY** ✅ and your observation is fair.

The good news: that key — `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` — is *designed* to be public. Supabase calls it the "publishable" key (formerly "anon" key). It's literally meant to be in the client bundle, visible to anyone who opens DevTools. Security is handled by Row Level Security policies on the database side, not by hiding this key. So no actual security leak. **But** — yes, it's terrible UX that an underlying fetch error bubbled the raw header string into the user-facing message. A proper sign-up flow should catch that and show "Something went wrong — try again." We should harden that error path post-launch, but it's not a blocker today.

The error "Token has expired or is invalid" *almost always* means Supabase didn't recognise the code at verify time — which can be:
- Code mismatch (typo)
- Expired (Supabase's default OTP TTL is ~1 hour)
- Already-used (one attempt per code)
- **Magic-link clicked instead of code entered** — if the email contains both `{{ .Token }}` and `{{ .ConfirmationURL }}`, clicking the link would go through a flow that ends up with "Token invalid"

That last one is my top suspicion. If you have access to the Supabase email template again, double-check the body — it should ONLY have `{{ .Token }}`, no link, no `{{ .ConfirmationURL }}`.

Two layered things:

1. **The actual error** `flow_state_already_used` happens before any final redirect. It means Supabase is rejecting the OAuth state from a *previous* attempt that's still in your browser cookies (the abandoned localhost flow earlier).

2. **The akhar.io redirect on error** happens because Supabase's behavior is: redirect to `Site URL` (not the requested `redirectTo`) when OAuth errors out. So even though we set Site URL to `akhar.io` correctly for production, it bites us during testing while DNS isn't pointed yet.

Two fixes:

### Immediate — clear stale OAuth cookies + retry

Open https://akhar-git-dev-reliq-digitals-projects.vercel.app in **a fresh incognito window**. Old localhost test left state in cookies which is what's tripping it. Incognito = no cookies = clean slate.

Honestly? You're right and this is the actual hole in the product. Right now `/alphabet` is a long index where you can click anything → there's no sense that you've *learned* anything. No "you mastered this, now try that." It's a hosted textbook, not a learning app.

What real mastery-based progression looks like:

**Per letter / unit**
- See letter → pick sound (or audio → pick letter): 4/5 correct in a row before "✓ learned"
- Locked next-letter tile until current one's ticked
- Letters come back periodically as spaced-review, so you don't forget by Lesson 30

**Per line of letters** (the 5-at-a-time grouping the traditional curriculum already uses)
- After each individual letter, a mini-quiz on the *whole line* (10 items mixing recognition + audio + match-meanings)
- ~80% to pass → unlocks the next line

**Per matra → mukta-words → reading-sentences**
- Each stage has its own "you can do this" check
- Reading sentences: tap the meaning of the sentence, drag English fragments into order, etc.

**Per sakhi**
- After reading: 2-3 comprehension questions ("Who is Mehta Kalu?", "What did Nanak give the sadhus?")
- "Read the next sakhi" only unlocks after passing

The principle: **progress should feel earned**. Right now nothing about /alphabet is gated, so finishing means scrolling, not learning. With mastery checks, getting to the next letter *means something*.

What I'd steal from Duolingo: visible "next" with lock icon, clear "you need X to unlock Y" messaging, the small dopamine hit of ✓ when you complete an exercise.
What I'd skip (same as before): XP, streaks, hearts, owl, leagues. Adult heritage learners hate that energy.

Yes — this is the right design and the **course vs reference** split is the clarifying move I didn't see. Right now /alphabet is *trying to be both* and failing at each. Let me lay out a plan for what each thing actually is.

## The split

**Reference** (always-accessible, no gating, no progress):
- Full alphabet chart — all 35 at a glance
- Muharni table — consonant × matra grid
- Sakhi library — what `/library` is today, just text + audio
- Dictionary lookups (already there via hover popovers)

**Course** (sequential, gated, progress-tracked):
- A path of lessons, each one teaching one concept (a letter, a matra, a sound rule, then later a sentence pattern, then a whole sakhi)
- Each lesson = ~5-8 exercises mixing 3-4 types (see below)
- Locked tiles ahead; clear "do this lesson to unlock the next"

Nav: `Home / Path / Reference / Deck / Profile`. The Path is the *action*; Reference is what you check when stuck.

## The path itself — first-pass layout

Each block is **one lesson** unless noted. Lessons inside a section are sequential; sections themselves are sequential.

**§1 Letters: line 1 (ੳ ਅ ੲ ਸ ਹ)**
- L1: ੳ
- L2: ਅ
- L3: ੲ
- L4: ਸ
- L5: ਹ
- L6: **Line 1 check** (mixes all 5 — ~12 exercises, 80% to pass)

**§2 Letters: line 2 (ਕ ਖ ਗ ਘ ਙ)** — same shape, line check at end, but mixes in some §1 letters as reinforcement.

**§3-§7** same pattern through all 7 lines.

**§8 Nukta letters** (ਸ਼ ਖ਼ ਗ਼ ਜ਼ ਫ਼) — one lesson each + line check.

**§9 Matras: short vowels** (sihari / aunkar) + check.

**§10 Matras: long vowels** (kanna / bihari / dulainkar / lavan / dulavan / hora / kanaura) + check.

**§11 Diacritics** (tippi, bindi, addhak) + check.

**§12 Mukta words** — read 3-letter words. Lessons teach 5 words at a time. Section check at the end.

**§13 Simple sentences** — sentence-level reading with comprehension multiple-choice.

**§14 Sakhi 1: Birth of Guru Nanak** — actual sakhi as a path unit. After reading: 3 comprehension Qs. Pass → §15 unlocks.

**§15 Sacha Sauda** → comprehension → unlocks the next.

**§16-onwards**: each processed sakhi is a path unit.

After §16+, the *path* runs out at our current content limit — natural stopping point that signals "you're caught up, more coming."

## Exercise types per lesson

Five types, mixed within a lesson so it doesn't get stale:

1. **Audio → tap the letter** (multiple choice, 4 options)
2. **Letter → tap the matching sound** (4 audio buttons)
3. **Type the romanization** ("type how you'd write ਕ in English letters" → "ka")
4. **Match pairs** — 4 letters left, 4 sounds right, drag to pair
5. **Tap in order** — 5 letters shown, audio plays a sequence, tap them in order

In a "Letter ਕ" lesson, maybe: 2 of (1), 2 of (2), 1 of (3), 1 of (4) — 6 exercises total. **Mastery = all 6 right.** Get one wrong, it reappears later in the same lesson. Get the SAME one wrong twice → redo the whole lesson.

For section checks: 10-15 exercises, 80% to pass.

## Placement test — yes, this is the right call

Onboarding's self-report is unreliable (people overestimate, underestimate, or aren't sure). A real test removes the lie. Proposed structure:

**7 questions, ~4 minutes, static (not adaptive — adaptive is v2)**

1. *Letter 1*: audio of "ka" → tap the right letter (4 options including ਕ ਖ ਗ ਟ)
2. *Letter 2*: see ਮ → tap the right sound (audio buttons)
3. *Letter 3*: audio of "tha" → tap the letter (harder — confusing similar letters)
4. *Matra 1*: see ਕਾ → tap "kaa" (vs "ki", "ku", "ke")
5. *Matra 2*: type the romanization of ਪਿਆਰ
6. *Mukta read*: see ਘਰ → tap "house" (vs "tree", "dog", "river")
7. *Sentence*: read ਉਹ ਘਰ ਜਾਂਦਾ ਹੈ — tap the meaning ("He goes home" vs distractors)

**Placement based on score:**

| Right | Starting position |
|---|---|
| 0-2 | §1 Lesson 1 (full course from scratch) |
| 3 | §3 (line 3 of letters) |
| 4 | §8 (nukta letters) |
| 5 | §9 (matras) |
| 6 | §12 (mukta words) |
| 7 | §13 (sentences) — or skip to §14 Sakhi 1 if they got Q7 right |

**Critical rule**: placement sets your *current* position. **Everything *before* is "available" (you can revisit for review, gets a green ✓-but-unstarted icon); everything *after* stays locked.** No skipping forward.

## The "do it more than once" question

Duolingo's mastery model is: each lesson has multiple levels (Bronze → Silver → Gold → etc.). You unlock the next lesson after *Bronze* (first pass), but you can come back to push to Gold.

I'd skip that complexity for v1. Simpler: each lesson is "done" when first-passed. Spaced review brings letters back in *later* lessons' exercises automatically (e.g., the §3 line check includes some §1 + §2 letters). The need to remember stuff comes from those reappearances, not from grinding the same lesson 3 times.

We can add a deeper "Push to mastery" mode in v2.

## Scope honest reality

This is **~3-4 weeks** of focused work to do well. Breakdown:

- **Week 1**: Schema (user_progress, exercise_results), Path UI shell, lesson runner shell, two exercise types
- **Week 2**: Remaining exercise types, all letter content converted to path lessons, line checks
- **Week 3**: Matras + mukta + sentences sections, placement test, onboarding rewire
- **Week 4**: Sakhi-as-unit (comprehension questions on each), Reference section UI split out from current /alphabet + /library

You said earlier you wanted to ship today. Now you're describing 3-4 weeks of work. Honest question:

## The strategic call

- **(A)** Ship today's MVP (foundations + sakhis + deck, no path). Build path as v1.1 over 3-4 weeks. Real users see the foundation now, shape what we build next.
- **(B)** Delay launch by ~3-4 weeks, ship with the path mastery flow live. First impression is "this is the real thing" — much stronger but takes the runway.
- **(C)** Hybrid: ship today, but block the homepage signup behind a "joining the beta — get early access" form. We collect emails now, ship to those users when path is ready. Lets you keep momentum without launching a half-thing.

What's your gut? I'd lean **(C)** — keeps you shipping today (you've earned that with how much you've done), but doesn't burn the first-impression on a half-built thing.

## 1. PME Book 1 order — the actual sequence

PME Book 1 (and the traditional Punjab-school approach) goes roughly:

> **Letters → Mukta words → Matras → Diacritics → Muharni → Reading**

Your instinct's exactly right — mukta words come straight after letters because *you can already read them with just letter knowledge* (no matras needed). It's the first real "I read a Punjabi word!" win, and it consolidates letter recognition before adding matra complexity. So the path becomes:

**§1-§7**: The 35 letters, line by line (5 per line, with a Line Check after each)
**§8**: Nukta letters (the 5 borrowed sounds — ਸ਼ ਖ਼ ਗ਼ ਜ਼ ਫ਼). Could also do this after Mukta-words; I'd put it here so all letters are taught before they appear in words.
**§9**: **Mukta words** — read actual Punjabi words with just letters you know. ਘਰ ਕਮਲ ਜਨਮ ਫਲ etc. Each lesson teaches 5-8 words; section ends with reading-comprehension check.
**§10**: Matras — short vowels first (sihari ਿ, aunkar ੁ). Each one becomes one lesson. Lessons end with reading words using that matra (ਕਿਰਨ, ਪੁਲ, etc.)
**§11**: Matras — long vowels (kanna ਾ, bihari ੀ, dulainkar ੂ, lavan ੇ, dulavan ੈ, hora ੋ, kanaura ੌ). Same shape.
**§12**: Diacritics (tippi ੰ, bindi ਂ, addhak ੱ) — one lesson each + check.
**§13**: **Muharni** — the systematic drill. Each consonant ×each matra in a grid. This is where it all consolidates. Probably 3-4 lessons covering rows of consonants.
**§14**: Multi-letter / mixed-matra words — start reading longer words combining everything.
**§15**: Simple sentences — sentence-level reading with comprehension.
**§16+**: Sakhis as path units (Birth of Guru Nanak first — already the simplest one).

So **15 sections before sakhis**, ~50-60 lessons total. Matches PME's gradual pace.

One **deviation from PME** worth flagging: PME does muharni as a *systematic chant table* (like multiplication tables — ਕ ਕਾ ਕਿ ਕੀ ...). Pedagogically powerful but also dull for adult diaspora learners. I'd keep the muharni section but make the lessons interactive (audio-pair, type-romanization on each grid square) rather than recite-the-table. Still get the systematic coverage without the drudgery.

**Question for you**: do you want me to dig up an actual PME Book 1 TOC to validate this sequence exactly matches, or are you good with this as the working spec?

## 2. Finger-tracing — yes, technically possible. Here's the honest picture.

**Can we do it?** Yes, fully. HTML5 Canvas + pointer events captures finger strokes on touch screens (and mouse on desktop, useful for tablet/laptop users with a Wacom etc.). Three implementation tiers, increasing in quality:

1. **Pixel-coverage check** (simplest, ~1 day to build): show a faint letter outline; user traces; we check what % of the letter's pixels are covered by their drawing. Not perfect — they could scribble everywhere — but works as a "you traced it" check.
2. **Stroke-order check** (~3-4 days): we hand-define the canonical stroke order for each letter (start point → end point + direction). User's strokes must match. This is what most Asian-script writing apps actually do, including the good Mandarin/Japanese ones.
3. **ML handwriting recognition** (~weeks + infrastructure): train a model on user inputs vs canonical letters. Way more work than it's worth for v1.

**My recommendation**: tier 2 (stroke-order check) is the right target. The hand-define-the-strokes step is maybe an hour per letter set; doable in a weekend.

**Timing**: I'd put writing in **v1.2** (post-path-mastery launch), not v1.1. Reasoning:
- The reading path alone is 3-4 weeks of work. Adding writing on top stretches that.
- A *lot* of heritage learners want to read but don't care about writing — adding it changes the path scope without serving everyone.
- For those who do want it: a clean "Writing" toggle on each letter lesson — "I've traced this letter 3 times correctly" — is a clean separate concern.

**Free vs Pro?** I'd put **writing on the free tier**, not Pro. Reasoning:
- It's a real pedagogical differentiator — *most* apps don't teach writing. Making it free is a strong free-tier wedge.
- Pro should be: more content (themes 4-12), advanced SRS, offline audio, classical sakhi corpus full access. Things that scale value with engagement, not features that exist or don't.
- Writing exists or it doesn't — same code runs whether user pays or not. Locking it feels stingy.

What I'd put behind paywall later: SRS deck full review (free gets daily limit), full sakhi library (free gets first 6), themed vocab modules. The actual *learning capability* stays free.

## Corrected PME Book 1 sequence (verified)

| # | Section | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| §1–§7 | **35 letters**, line by line | Painti order = stops → affricates → retroflex → dentals → labials → approximants → sibilant+h. The 7 lines of 5. |
| §8 | **5 Nukta letters** as one group | Right after Painti, before any matra. Your recollection confirmed. |
| §9 | **Mukta words** | Reading actual single words with no matras. |
| §10 | **Matras** in PME order | Kanna → Sihari → Bihari → Aunkar → Dulainkar → Lavan → Dulavan → Hora → Kanaura. Exactly what you said. |
| §11 | **Diacritics** | PME order: Addhak → Tippi → Bindi. (I'd had Tippi first — wrong.) |
| §12 | **Conjuncts** | Pairin Rara (subscript ਰ), then Pairin Haha (subscript ਹ, which also doubles as tone introduction). I missed these entirely in my earlier plan — they're real lessons in PME. |

## Two things PME Book 1 does NOT have (and I'd been assuming it did)

1. **No muharni drill.** The systematic consonant×matra grid is a PSEB / traditional-pathshala thing, *not* a Nagra tradition. PME just shows example words per matra for a handful of consonants.

2. **No sentences, no dialogues, no connected text.** Book 1 stops at *single-word level*. Sentences start in Book 2.

3. (Also: no in-text quizzes — those are in a separate workbook. So mastery checks are our addition, which is fine.)

## Decisions this forces

**(a) Muharni — include or skip?**
PME doesn't do it. But for diaspora adult learners who can't pattern-match like a kid immersed in the language, a systematic drill (read every consonant + matra combination) genuinely accelerates fluency. I'd argue *include it as our addition* — clearly framed as "after PME, our own consolidation drill" — but I want your gut here, since you said follow PME exactly.

**(b) The sentence-reading gap.**
PME Book 1 ends at conjuncts. Our next unit is sakhis, which are sentences. There's a real difficulty jump there. Two options:

- **Follow PME strictly** — finish conjuncts → jump straight into Sakhi 1 (Modern). The reader's hover popovers + parallel English do the scaffolding work that sentence-reading lessons would otherwise do.
- **Extend with our own sentence-reading section** between §12 and Sakhi 1 — covers basic sentence structure, common particles (ਹੈ, ਨੇ, ਨੂੰ, ਦਾ, ਦੀ, ਦੇ), simple SOV sentences, before jumping to sakhi prose.

I lean *extend with our own* — the sakhi prose is a sharp jump from "word level," and even with hover help, the leap risks bouncing learners. The PME-strict approach gambles that the sakhi reader's scaffolding is enough.

**(c) Pairin Rara + Pairin Haha — yes or merge?**
These appear in PME as discrete lessons (subscript ਰ for words like ਪ੍ਰੇਮ, subscript ਹ for tone). They're real and worth their own lessons in our path. But pedagogically dense — beginners hit conjuncts and tone at the same time. Could split, or could compress to one lesson.

**(d) Strict order: matras *all* come after the Painti + nukta + mukta, not interleaved.**
PSEB sprinkles matras in early (kids see ਕਾ within pages of ਕ). PME front-loads all letter shapes first. Your "follow PME" call means we're firmly in the PME camp: full Painti + nukta + mukta first, then all matras. That's a more adult-L2-friendly sequence anyway.

## What I'd propose as our v1.1 path

If you say yes to (a) and (b):

```
§1-§7 — 35 letters (Painti, by phonetic line)
§8     — 5 nukta letters
§9     — mukta words (real word reading begins)
§10    — 9 matras in PME order
§11    — addhak, tippi, bindi
§12    — pairin rara, pairin haha (tone introduction)
§13    — muharni consolidation (our addition — drill)
```

## Honest scope reality

The v1.1 plan I just wrote is **~3-4 weeks of focused work**. That's not me being slow — that's the minimum to do it well:

- Week 1: schema + path UI + lesson runner + 2 exercise types
- Week 2: remaining exercise types + 35 letter lessons + line checks
- Week 3: nukta + mukta + matras + diacritics + conjuncts + placement test
- Week 4: muharni + sentence reading + sakhi units + reference split + polish

**"Fast" can compress some of this, but it can't disappear.** If we ship a half-built path, that's worse than shipping no path — broken progression destroys trust harder than missing progression.

## Three honest options

**(A) Minimum-viable path — 5-7 days hard**
- Just §1-§7 letter lessons + line checks
- 2 exercise types (audio→letter, letter→audio)
- Basic mastery (4/5 correct, unlock next)
- No placement test (everyone starts at §1.1)
- No reference section split — current /alphabet stays as reference
- Skip §8-§14 (matras, diacritics, conjuncts, muharni, sentences) entirely
- Sakhis stay as library only, not path units
- **Launch positioning**: "akhar — start with the 35 letters of the Punjabi alphabet" — narrower than the current landing promises. We'd need to soften landing copy too.

**(B) Real foundation path — 14-18 days hard**
- §1-§12 (everything up to conjuncts) but skip §13 muharni + §14 sentences
- All 4-5 exercise types
- Placement test (7 questions, static)
- Reference section split out cleanly
- Sakhis stay in library as their own thing — not path units yet
- **Launch positioning**: "akhar — learn to read Punjabi from scratch with a proper path." Honest, complete, ships with sakhis as the discoverable next-step.

**(C) Full v1.1 as planned — 3-4 weeks**
- Everything in the spec
- Sakhis as path units with comprehension
- Reference section properly built
- **Launch positioning**: the original vision. The thing we actually want.

## What I'd push back on if I were you

You've done huge work today — auth, profile, home, SMTP, the SQLite fix. **None of it gets thrown away by delaying.** It all stays on dev waiting. Delaying isn't "losing today" — it's "saving today for the right launch."

I'd also flag: every time we've added scope today ("we need profile" → "we need home" → "we need path"), we've been right that the thing matters. But that pattern means **we're not actually trying to ship today** anymore. We're discovering what real-shippable looks like, which is something else.

## My recommendation

**Option B (real foundation path, 14-18 days).** It's the smallest version of the product that doesn't feel like a half-promise. Option A would mean we'd have to walk back landing copy and that hurts. Option C is the dream but you're already stretched.

If we go (B), I'd suggest:
- Today: stop trying to launch. Commit current dev work as "v1 foundation" and shift to v1.1 build.
- Day 1-7: schema + path UI shell + lesson runner + 2 exercise types + first 5 letter lessons working end-to-end (proof of mechanic)
- Day 8-14: remaining exercise types + all 35 letters + nukta + mukta + matras + diacritics + conjuncts content + placement test
- Day 15-18: polish, reference split, smoke testing, launch

What's your gut — A, B, or C? And: is there a real deadline driving "fast," or is it momentum-based ("I just want to be live")? Knowing which helps me make smarter calls on what to compress.

Two real bugs — let me fix both:

**1. Multiple sound plays**: my FeedbackPanel useEffect depended on `onContinue` which is recreated on every parent render. Each new closure re-triggered the effect → new audio.

**2. Sound plays on its own**: the AudioPickLetter renderer has `autoPlay` on its letter audio. When phase switches `exercise` → `feedback`, the JSX branch changes, the renderer *remounts*, and autoPlay fires again — so you hear the letter, then the feedback sound, sometimes overlapping.

Go to https://akhar-git-dev-reliq-digitals-projects.vercel.app/reference/alphabet — all 35 letters in a 7-line grid, each with its Punjabi name (the exact string sent to `/api/tts`), ISO romanisation, IPA where useful, and a play button.

The name shown on each card is *literally* what the TTS pronounces. If one sounds wrong, two things are likely:

1. **The name itself is unusual or rare** (e.g. ਟੈਂਕਾ for ਟ uses an English-influenced spelling that Chirp may stumble on)
2. **A diacritic combination Chirp 3 mishandles** — sometimes adding/removing tippi or addhak changes the output dramatically

When you find bad ones, just tell me the letter + what it currently says vs what it *should* say. The fix is usually one of:
- Edit the `name_pa` in `data/curriculum.ts` for that letter (change spelling so Chirp pronounces it correctly)
- Or, swap to a different pronunciation prompt entirely for the audio

Either way: change the prompt → next request regenerates + caches in R2 → both the course and the reference page pick it up immediately, no code redeploy needed for individual letters.

Send the list when you've worked through them.

## On the cross-line recaps — yes, this is the right call

Your instinct is exactly the spaced-repetition principle. Without it, by Line 7 the user has seen Line 1 letters maybe 6 times (across §1 + the §1 check), but nothing since. That's classic forgetting territory.

**Where the recap sits in the path:**

I'd append it to the section that just finished, as the last tile before moving on. So §2 becomes: 5 letter lessons → §2 check → §1+§2 recap → unlocks §3.

That way the user sees a clear pattern: *finish a line → check that line → recap everything → move on*.

**Question I want your gut on before building:**

A recap that includes *every* prior letter grows: 10 exercises after §2, 15 after §3, ... 30 after §7. That's a real "you sit down for 10 minutes" lesson by the end. Two ways to handle:

- **A) Full cumulative.** Every prior letter, 1 exercise each. Comprehensive — the user is genuinely re-touched on every letter. But late recaps are long.
- **B) Smart cumulative.** Cap each recap at ~12-15 exercises. Bias selection toward the *oldest* letters (highest forgetting risk) and the *current* line (freshest practice still needed). Same coverage philosophy, manageable length. This is what Duolingo's "personalized practice" does.

I lean (B) — pedagogically it's *better* than (A) because the longest review (post §7) covering 30 letters would have you doing each one only once anyway, which is barely better than nothing. Better to do 12 well-chosen ones with focus on the at-risk ones.

Either way, the implementation is similar — just the exercise-picking logic differs. Which feels right?

- **(b) Disable audio for ੜ entirely** — hide the play button, accept that this letter has no good TTS in the current voice. Honest about the limitation.

> DEVELOPER

hmm I really dont know becasue I notice this issue but also we can't test people on thier listening skills if the letters sound the same. What can we do aboyt htis??? lets dicuss

> AGENT

Real discussion — let me think this through with you.

**The honest linguistic situation**: nuktas are vestigial in modern Indian Punjabi. Many native speakers (especially in India) genuinely don't distinguish ਗ from ਗ਼ in speech — they pronounce ਗ਼ਜ਼ਲ as "gajal" not "ghazal", ਖ਼ਬਰ as "khabar" (regular ਖ) not the throaty /x/. Pakistani Punjabi (Shahmukhi) preserves them better, and Punjabi/Urdu literary tradition does too. So Chirp isn't wrong — it's reflecting how millions of people actually talk.

**What the nukta really teaches** is therefore two different things depending on how you frame it:

1. **A distinct phoneme** — the "correct" Urdu/Persian sound. Useful for advanced learners reading literary text, learners of Pakistani Punjabi, etymological understanding. Audio differentiation matters.
2. **A spelling distinction** — a visual marker that "this came from Persian/Arabic". The letter looks different, may sound the same as the base in casual speech. Visual recognition matters; audio doesn't.

For your audience (heritage-curious beginners targeting sakhis), **(2) is probably the honest goal**. Sakhis don't have many nukta-loaded words. Conversation doesn't require the distinction. Learners will encounter the visual form occasionally and need to recognize "ਖ਼ is just ਖ in a Persian/Urdu loanword."

**My recommendation**:

- **Keep nuktas in the alphabet** (visual recognition) — show them, name them, give an example word
- **Skip nukta letters from listening-only exercises** — `audio-pick-letter` (hear a sound, pick the letter) would be unfair since they sound identical. Exclude the 5 nukta letters from that exercise type.
- **Add a teach-screen note** on each nukta lesson: *"In modern spoken Punjabi this often sounds like ਗ. The dot marks Persian/Arabic origin — keep it when reading and writing."*
- **Accept Chirp's pronunciation as-is, verify them** (they sound like the base — that's modern colloquial reality)

This is honest, doesn't require recording, doesn't ship a Hindi accent.

**Alternative if you want phonetic accuracy**: spend 5 min recording yourself saying the 5 nukta names — drop into R2 under the override keys. Your accent, perfectly distinct sounds. One-time cost, never breaks. I can wire up the override slots and you slot the MP3s in.

What's your gut?

> AGENT

Yeah this is a real Chirp issue — it tacks ~200-500ms of dead air on the front. Two-pronged fix: (1) bake leading-silence trim into the synthesis pipeline so all future audio is clean, (2) a batch script to walk every cached MP3 in R2 and trim what's already there.

> AGENT

Now wire into `synthesize()` so future audio is auto-trimmed.

> AGENT

Now the batch script — list everything in R2 and trim each.

> DEVELOPER

woah woah but you can't jsut blindy trim by a set amoubt!!!! what is your strategy???

> AGENT

Sorry — let me lay out the strategy before doing anything destructive. **Not** a fixed amount.

**Per-clip silence detection:**
1. Pipe the MP3 into `ffmpeg silencedetect=noise=-25dB:d=0.05` — same threshold + duration we've used successfully for the carrier trims (ਉਹ ਸਨ। auto-trimmed cleanly with this).
2. Find the FIRST silence interval whose `silence_start ≤ 0.05s` — that's *the leading dead air* specifically, not interior pauses.
3. Cut at `silence_end - 80ms pad` so we keep a small pre-onset buffer (preserves the phoneme attack — same pad value the carrier-trims use).

**Safety bailouts (the buffer is returned unchanged in any of these cases):**
- No silence interval found that starts at t≈0 → already tight, leave it
- Leading silence shorter than 50ms → not perceptually worth cutting, risk of clipping onset
- ffmpeg fails for any reason → soft-fail, log warning, return original (never break the pipeline)

**Where it applies:**
- In `synthesize()` after Chirp returns, before R2 cache write — so future audio is always tight
- In a batch script over the existing R2 cache (the part I hadn't written yet) — this is the risky bit

**Honest caveats** about the batch:
- Threshold `-25dB` matches what worked for our carrier trims, but it's an empirical tune — soft-onset words (e.g. a word starting with /h/) might briefly read as silence past t=0 and trigger an over-cut. The 80ms pad protects most cases but not all.
- Idempotent if you re-run (already-tight clips bail at bailout #1)
- One-way operation — original R2 bytes are overwritten

**Two checkpoints I want from you before running the batch:**

1. The 80ms onset pad — tighter (50ms = snappier but more clip risk) or looser (120ms = safer but less snappy)?
2. Want a dry-run first that just reports "would-trim N clips by X ms each" so you can spot-check before committing?

> AGENT

Two issues, both real:

**(1)** The RLS error on verify — `upsert` does INSERT-or-UPDATE-on-conflict, but the table only has INSERT/DELETE policies. When you click verify on a clip that was already verified (e.g. you verified it in a different section), Postgres rejects the implicit UPDATE.

**(2)** Overlapping sounds — same audio key showing in multiple sections (a word can be both a letter example and a matra example).

Fixing both: add an UPDATE policy + dedupe display globally so each audio appears only once.
